I
JULIA AWOKE TO THE SOUND OF A LAWN MOWER
accompanied by a negro voice raised in spirited
song. Her eyes opened, then closed. She burrowed
her head deeper into the pillow, reluctant to be awak-
ened, clinging with instinctive tenacity to the sweet
oblivion of sleep.
The noise continued, a sustained whirr, a d oning
clatter, fading off at intervals into a pause filled
with the rise and fall of lusty notes. The melody was
familiar. Words formed in Julia's mind, hazily, then
more clearly, as sleep receded-
"Oh, de cat am de fish fo' me
Wid de onions an' de sassafras tea"
...
Wide awake, now, she turned, lay on her back,
her arms folded under her head. It was scarcely
even a coincidence, she thought, smiling at her tend-
ency to believe in signs and portents. Willie-Josey
was cutting the grass and that song was his favorite
tune. Still it was strange that on this particular
morning-
"Tell me about you," he had asked. She hadn't
been sure, then, that she liked him. She wasn't sure,
now. The amusement in his eyes when he looked ather, the teasing cadences in his voice, were a little
disconcerting. She'd not been quite sure what he
thought of her and had been determined to make
an impression. Driving with him through Rock Creek
Park, that first night after the dance, she had de-
scribed her life in Queensville, told him of the fam-
ily, Louisa, Willie-Josey, had sung for him the song
Willie-Josey was singing, now, beneath her window.
It had remained in her mind. She'd caught herself
humming it, afterwards, last week in Washington.
He'd liked it. He'd called it their theme song. That
was why -
Had she made home sound too picturesque?
Julia's brow creased with the intensity of trying to
remember. She was fairly sure that she hadn't. Cau-
tion had restrained her impulse to speak in terms
of pillars and balconies and old family retainers.
She'd known, even that first night, that he would
come to Queensville. She'd known, though he hadn't
told her, though he had made none of the gestures
she was accustomed to expect from men who were
attracted to her, who fell a little in love with her
when they met. Besides, she had felt that he would
have known if she hadn't told the truth. Odd that
she had felt that. She'd never, with any one be-
fore
-
"He'll eat any bait he can get his peepers on
And he'll hang 'twell Gabriel blows his silber horn
Oh, de cat am de fish fo' me!"It was a lovely day. The light in the square, high-
ceilinged room was dim and faintly green, but sun-
shine made bright ladders along the bowed shutters
beyond the screens. Julia lay, relaxed, pleasantly
aware of excitement, anticipating, remembering,
painting small bright pictures against the dusk of
the shuttered room. And under the thoughts drift-
ing through her mind, accenting memory and antici-
pation, silencing questions, sustaining her, was the
consciousness of how pretty she looked when she
awoke, her dark eyes bright, her hair a dark cloud
against the pillows, her cheeks, the golden pink of
a tea-rose, faintly flushed
...
"Oh, de cat am de fish fo' me!"
Willie-Josey concluded his third repetition of the
song with a sprightly flourish. The mower clattered
off down the walk and along the pavement. In the
silence which followed the clock in the hall struck
nine.
Julia sat up and threw back the covers. What
should she wear? It was warm enough, to-day, for a
linen suit but every blouse she owned needed press-
ing. She thought for an envious moment of Gretchen,
Aunt Gena's maid. Pleasant to pull a bell-cord and
have things done for you. But she was at home now.
She sighed, then smiled. She did have a nice nature,
she thought, not complacently, but with a feeling of
wonder and surprise. Admiration had not turned
her head, the petting and luxury Aunt Gena pro-vided, eyes that turned to watch her pass, knowing
that she possessed a certain magic. She had not lost
her love for simple things. It came from being nat-
ural, she decided, not striving after effects. Absurd
that Caroline should suggest, as she frequently
did
-
But time was flying. Julia slipped out of bed and a
book fell to the floor. For a moment the title-Furni-
ture of the Georgian Period-engaged her attention.
Did she remember anything that she had read last
night? Words, phrases, fragments of sentences,
crowded into her mind. "Chippendale, mahogany,
pie crust tables, cabriole legs-" What were cabriole
legs? Odd that Scott Mitchell should be interested
in antiques. It seemed an effeminate hobby for a man
so vitally male. "Hepplewhite, heart-shaped shield,
Prince of Wales plume with three feathers-" Or
was it Sheraton who used the plume design?
She hadn't told him she knew anything at all about
antiques. He had assumed that she did, though, prob-
ably because Aunt Gena had lovely ones. Again, as
she unlatched the shutters and lowered the screens,
Julia's forehead creased in vertical lines above her
small straight nose. And Aunt Gena had certainly
strengthened the impression. "You remember this,
Julia?" she'd asked, showing Scott her furniture, in-
dicating a chair, a table, a fire-screen. "This low-boy
stood in Grandmother Peyton's drawing-room under
the Stuart portrait. I'll give it to you when you have
a home of your own because I know you will value
it. Caroline, now-" She had acquiesced in all thatAunt Gena said, wanting merely to give pleasure.
But she needn't have gone on pretending. She'd been
nettled by Scott's expression. Watching her, his eyes
had been amused, indulgent, not quite believing -
"Chippendale originated the pie crust table"-
Julia turned from the last of the four wide windows.
Sunlight, flooding into the room, struck sparkles
from the glass paper weight on the desk. Beneath it,
where she had left it last night, lay a letter scarcely
started. She read the salutation-"Dearest Jay" -
and felt a twinge of guilt. Her eyes lifted to a photo-
graph in a silver frame. Eight months was a long
time. She found it difficult to form from the photo-
graph, from her memory, a living likeness of Jay.
Difficult to write to him. Julia lifted and inverted
the paper-weight, stood watching the miniature bliz-
zard enclosed in the crystal globe. If she wrote of
what she was doing, he was distressed and sent her
frantic wires followed by penitent letters in which
he begged her forgiveness. He was satisfied only
when she wrote of herself in relation to him, when
she invented activities, anecdotes, which she knew
would please him. She couldn't continue to invent in-
definitely. He'd told her he wanted her to amuse
herself while he was away. And yet when she did
He loved her so desperately. It would have been
heartless of her to let him go away, ill as he was,
without promising to marry him. And she did love
him. Dear Jay! Her personal pleasure was unimpor-
tant. Love such as Jay gave her was a sacred trust.
She would never fail or disappoint him, never doanything to mar the image of her that was enshrined
in his heart. What if it did mean less to her than it did
to him? His happiness would be her reward. Julia
placed the crystal globe on the desk. Moved to
tenderness, glowing with pity and self-sacrifice, she
took up the sheet of note-paper and read what she
had written-
"I'm just back from Washington. It was pretty
warm but beautiful. You know Washington in
June. Aunt Gena has a new apartment. It's simply
enormous and way up high overlooking Rock
Creek Park. I have a new hair-do, a fringe over
my forehead. Aunt Gena insisted and it is rather
nice. I look a little like the miniature of Grand-
mother Peyton, the one in the oval of seed pearls
Aunt Gena has"
Was the fringe on her forehead becoming? The
sheet of paper fluttered down to the desk. Julia went
to the dressing-table between the two side windows.
She moved the stool a trifle forward and bent toward
the mirror. With her hair mussed she couldn't be
sure. She worked interestedly with brush and comb,
smoothing soft strands back behind her ears, twist-
ing short ends into curls at the nape of her neck,
fluffing the deep curled bang. Her warm coloring
deepened, her dark eyes were bright, when she
surveyed the completed effect. The fringe was a suc-
cess. She shrugged her nightgown down to reveal
smooth shoulders, the tender contours of small firmbreasts. There-! Framed in the gilt of the oval
mirror she did look like the miniature of Grand-
mother Peyton. A dreaming expression softened her
eyes. Pretty, so pretty, Julia Peyton Perry.
"Yes, it makes you look romantic."
...
Julia started at the sound of her sister's voice.
She had not heard the door open. How long had
Caroline stood there, watching? She bent forward
toward the mirror.
"I thought, last night, I was getting a rash," she
said in a light quick voice. "Strawberries, maybe."
She ran her fingertips along the curve of her cheek
and frowned.
"That would be a tragedy, wouldn't it? To-day,
of all days, to break out into a rash."
She hadn't spoken of her plans for the day. She
hadn't mentioned Scott Mitchell's name since she
had returned from Washington. How could Caroline
have known? Julia turned, surprise more compelling
than the embarrassment of having been caught ad-
miring herself.
Caroline stood in the opened door, a dress, freshly
pressed, folded neatly over her arm.
"What's that for?" Julia asked. "My dress-?"
"Mother thought it was a safe bet for a garden
party." Caroline came into the room, walking sound-
lessly in canvas ghillies across the bare spaces be-
tween the rugs. "She didn't want to wake you to
ask. Miss Jenny called to say she's sending the car
for you at eleven."
"Miss Jenny!" Dismay clouded Julia's face,echoed sharply in her voice. "Is this Garden Week?
Good heavens! Is her party to-day?"
"Yes, and you'd better step on it, sister." Caro-
line laid the dress on the bed, folds of peach-colored
organdy, crisp and fresh against the darker peach
tone of the counterpane. "It's nearly ten. Shall I
run water for a bath?"
"I can't!" Julia said, half wailing.
"Can't what?" Caroline asked in surprise.
"I can't go to Miss Jenny's. I have another en-
gagement."
"That isn't unusual, is it?"
"But this is important. I mean-" Julia paused
abruptly, conscious of having blundered, of having
revealed something, perhaps, which she preferred
Caroline not to know. "It's just very inconvenient,"
she went on smoothly. "A friend of mine is driv-
ing over from Washington. We're going to the
auction at Wye House."
"So that's why you've been boning up on an-
tiques." Caroline stopped to pick up the book lying
on the floor. Julia, watching, saw only the top of
a curly brown head, slim square shoulders under a
boy's white shirt, but she knew that Caroline was
smiling, half in amusement, half in scorn. Why
hadn't she hidden the book? Not that she was
ashamed of not wanting to appear a complete ignora-
mus. But Caroline would misinterpret her motives.
No, she amended honestly. Caroline was penetrat-
ing. She had probably guessed at the truth.
"I had to," she said with a rueful laugh, designedto be conciliating. Her voice, warm but clear, like
a brook rippling in sunshine, took on a confiding
quality. "Aunt Gena gave him the impression that
I was practically an authority on antiques. I had
to find out something about them other than that
Mother had sold all of hers one by one to Aunt
Gena before I was old enough to know that claw
and ball feet are Chippendale. Or are they?" A
laugh rippled halfway up the scale and down.
"Anyway, Chippendale originated the pie-crust
table."
"Did he?" Caroline commented, unimpressed.
Erect again, she walked across the room to Julia's
desk. "Is he a dealer-or an interior decorator?"
she asked.
"He?" Julia's brows lifted like bird wings in
flight. "Oh, you mean Scott Mitchell. Heavens, no,
he's a lawyer, temporarily in Washington. He's
representing a corporation, steel or asbestos or
something. His home is in New York."
"Why this interest in antiques?" Caroline had
placed the book on the desk. She stood with the
paper-weight in her hand, watching, as Julia had,
the miniature blizzard enclosed in the crystal globe.
"He's furnishing an apartment in New York.
Aunt Gena told him about the auction at Wye
House. I forgot I'd promised Miss Jenny. Won't
you go in my place?"
Caroline's reply was a short brusque laugh.
"There'll be nothing to do but serve sandwiches
and be pleasant," continued Julia."Can't." Caroline dropped down full length on
the chintz-covered sofa. "I have an engagement."
"What? You don't go to the office on Saturday."
"The Vet. is coming to look at the dogs."
Those dogs! Julia's nose wrinkled faintly with
distaste. But this was not the moment to antago-
nize Caroline.
"Please, Cal," she urged. "I won't ask you again.
I know I should have remembered."
"If you hadn't been walking around with your
head in the clouds since you came home, you'd have
been reminded. Mother has talked of nothing else
for the past two days."
Julia ignored the implication contained in the
comment.
"Be an angel, Cal -"
"Me?" Caroline turned the globe to catch the
sunlight. "I'd rather be comfortable. The Polk
gardens are beautiful. I don't like old ladies who
condescend to flowers. I wouldn't be pleasant. I'd
be rude. Besides, it's you Miss Jenny wants. You're
more decorative than I am. A rose among roses
and not a thorn to your name."
"Oh, nonsense!" Julia's glance rested upon her
sister whose eyes were lifted to a point on the wall
above the desk. There was truth in what Caroline
said, she thought, though she was not unaware of
the acid beneath the honey. Feature for feature
Caroline was nearly as pretty as she was. She had
the same warm coloring. Her hair was a brighter
brown than her own, cut short, curling all over herhead. Her eyes were hazel, like Father's, widely
spaced with long dark lashes. She had longer legs
and her shoulders were square which gave her a
boyish appearance not at all unattractive.
As she saw her now, in profile, pensive, absorbed
in some thought of her own, not being derisive or
scornful, Julia was obliged to admit that Caroline
had a certain distinctive attraction. She might be
popular. People liked her if she would let them.
But she wouldn't take the trouble to be charming.
She didn't care how she looked, going around in
shirts and slacks, tramping with the dogs, bare-
headed, in all sorts of weather. She had a prickly
disposition, too. She called out-and-out rudeness
being honest and frank. She lacked-well, glamour,
romantic appeal, the touch of magic that caught and
held the imagination. Caroline was as romantic as
plain bread and butter.
..
"Well-" Julia sighed and rose. Caroline turned
quickly. For an instant Julia fancied that her sister
looked self-conscious, as though she'd been caught
doing something of which she was ashamed. Then
the expression vanished. Her hazel eyes met Julia's
glance serenely, a little derisively.
"Sorry," she said, resuming the unfinished argu-
ment. "If you will make promises you don't intend
to keep, you'll have to bear the burden, my friend."
"It isn't a burden," Julia said, nettled. "I enjoy
the Garden Week parties. It's just that I can't get
in touch with Scott. He stayed with friends in An-napolis last night and is probably on the road now.
But I'll go to Miss Jenny's, of course."
"Why?"
"Because I promised."
"You could call and ask her to let you off. It
has been done. You might tell the truth, for instance,
that you forgot the date and made another en-
gagement."
Julia's reply came quickly, prompted by instinct
rather than reason.
"Oh, I couldn't," she said. "Miss Jenny would
be offended."
"Julia Perry never disappoints her public," Caro-
line murmured.
"I don't know what you mean," Julia said with
dignity.
"Yes, you do." Caroline sat on the sofa. "You're
keen on this Mitchell gent, aren't you, Ju?"
"Not especially." Julia chose words with care.
"He's a friend of Aunt Gena's who was pleasant
to me in Washington." What, exactly, was Caroline
up to? Usually she was resentful toward men who
paid her, Julia, attention, since Jay had been ill, at
least, since the family had known that she was to
marry him when he returned. Caroline seemed to
think that casual friendships with other men was
being unfair to Jay. Of course they didn't remain
casual friendships, she admitted. Sooner or later the
man had to be dealt with kindly but firmly. Miss
Jenny's nephew, Roger Polk. He was visiting at
Fairlee now. Poor Roger"Don't you ever do anything you really want to
do?" Caroline asked suddenly. "Just go ahead and
do it, I mean, without giving a darn?"
"Of course, I do."
"No, you don't," Caroline said bluntly. "You
spend your life making pictures of yourself and
then trying to live up to them. If I wanted to have
a date with somebody I liked, the whole county
couldn't stop me."
"There is a certain amount of consideration-"
Julia began.
"Consideration! Rats! You just want to be
cackled over by a lot of old hens, male and female.
'Julia Perry is so sweet with older people," Caro-
line mimicked. "I know. You needn't tell me. None
of my business." The heat went out of her voice.
She smiled gaily, disarmingly. "It's your life. You
can live it upside down or sliding down a drain pipe
if you please. But however you live it, you've got
to make a start." She sprang up from the sofa and
placed the paper-weight on the desk. "I'll run water
for a bath and bring you a tray." As she passed her
sister, she gave Julia's arm a quick, impulsive
squeeze. "You're such a nut, Ju," she said, reluctant
affection in her voice, and went hurrying from the
room.II
A CLOSED CAR OF ANTIQUATED BUT IMPRESSIVE DE-
sign moved with the majesty of an ocean liner
under the maples that shaded the street, came to a
stop at the curb. Caroline, sweeping the porch with
vigorous strokes, dropped the broom against a chair.
You expected a gangplank and whistles, she thought,
when the Polks' limousine made a landing. She
signaled to the Polks' Clarence behind the wheel and
went into the house.
"Mother! Julia!" she called from the lower hall.
"Are you ready? The Queen Mary has docked."
Mother appeared at the top of the stairs.
"Quietly, dear," she said in gentle rebuke. "Daddy
is sleeping."
A familiar exasperation stirred in Caroline's
breast. She knew very well that Dad was sleeping.
He usually slept until past noon on Saturday. But
not for any worthy reason such as Mother's rebuke
implied. Dad devoted Friday night, after the paper
was off the press, to amusing himself with his
cronies. He'd made a night of it last night. Dawn
had been breaking this morning when she'd heard
him let himself into the house, heard Mother go-
ing to meet him, quieting him in soothing under-tones, getting him to bed in the guest room across
the hall. Did Mother think she hadn't heard? Did
she really suppose-?
But of course she didn't. The fictions, implied
or expressed, which Mother invented to protect
Dad were pathetically transparent. She didn't care
whether you believed them or not, Caroline re-
flected, as she had on countless similar occasions
since she'd been old enough to think for herself.
You must not question them, though. You must ac-
cept them with a pretense of belief no matter how
strongly outraged honesty urged you to protest.
Mother's attitude was absurd, but it was, she
supposed, heroic. At least she was consistent. She
admitted no one, not even her daughters, into
an intimacy which allowed any criticism of Dad.
She'd been estranged from her family at times. Even
Aunt Gena, the sister closest to her both in age
and affection, periodically abandoned her to what
Aunt Gena called her "fantastic devotion to Will
Perry." She had no close friends among her ac-
quaintances in the town and along the Eastern
Shore. People spoke with admiration of her appear-
ance, her charm, her accomplishments as a house-
keeper and hostess, but they resented her reserve,
the dignity of manner which discouraged intimacies.
Mother's attitude remained unchanged. She didn't
care what people thought of her as an individual.
She was completely unselfish in her devotion to Dad.
Yes, Mother had courage. Exasperation softenedinto reluctant admiration, into the treacherous ten-
derness for the people she loved which lay beneath
Caroline's candid judgment of them, as she watched
Mother descend the stairs. There must be times,
she thought, even now when Dad was making a
success of editing the Observer, when she must be
lonely and hurt and frightened, when she would
have been grateful for the intimacy she discour-
aged. No human being could maintain that consist-
ency without occasional periods of despondency.
Those times, if they came, Mother endured in
silence and alone. Certainly there was no evidence
of despondency to-day, though her rest had been
disturbed last night and she'd been busy all morning
with household tasks. Mother's dark eyes were
serene beneath the shallow brim of her hat. She
smiled at Caroline as she walked past her toward
the mirror above the chesterfield, not in apology,
but with the quiet, sometimes mischievous gaiety
which was her most endearing characteristic.
"Is this dress becoming?" she asked, pausing in
front of the mirror, stepping back for a lengthened
view. "The color is nice, isn't it? Do you think
it's too fussy for a party at the Polks'?"
"You look sweet!" Julia, always prompt and gen-
erous with praise, answered the question before
Caroline had time to phrase a reply. She came
down the stairs, a picture, of course, in peach-col-
ored organdy and a wide brimmed hat, her kid
sandals with high tapering heels dancing along therunner that carpeted the steps. Julia did have a nice
disposition, Caroline was forced to admit. She was
pleasant at home as well as when she was making
conquests abroad. The desire to please which
prompted the reply was sincere but, Caroline knew,
Julia's reply would have been quite as enthusiastic
if, though that was beyond the reach of her imagi-
nation, Mother looked a fright. It was impossible
for Julia to tell the truth if the truth was likely to
give anyone in her immediate presence the slightest
twinge of pain.
In this instance, however, there was no conflict
between the truth and Julia's desire to please.
"It is becoming," Caroline said. "You do wear
clothes well, Mother."
"And your figure isn't getting bulgy, which is a
comfort." Julia fluttered about Mother with a flat-
tering air of interest and admiration. "Aunt Gena
must have selected that dress in one of her quieter
moments," she went on. "It suits you better than
her more vivacious attempts."
"I made it over in such a rush." Mother re-
volved slowly in front of the glass, paused for a
glimpse of her slender, still graceful silhouette.
"Eugenia is getting heavier," she added, a note of
complacency in her voice. "Her things used to fit
me perfectly and now they're much too large."
"She's fighting to the last ditch," Julia said, laugh-
ing. "Bananas and skim milk three times a day."
"Horrors!" Mother grimaced as she turned atlast from the mirror. "She'll ruin her health." The
complacency lingering in her voice indicated that her
concern for Aunt Gena's health did not detract from
her satisfaction with her own appearance. She picked
up her bag and gloves which she had dropped on
the chesterfield. "Come along, Julia. We mustn't
keep the car waiting." But at the door she halted
abruptly, gave an exclamation of distress. "I'm for-
getting the book I promised Miss Jenny."
"The rose book Uncle Tom sent you?" Caroline
asked. "Where is it? I'll get it, Mother."
"It's I don't remember. Upstairs, I think."
Mother's manner had altered. A preoccupied ex-
pression slipped across her face. She seemed to have
withdrawn from the companionship of her daughters
even before she turned and, murmuring absently,
"Never mind. I'll get it," went hurrying back to-
ward the stairs.
Julia opened the screen door and stepped out on
the porch. Caroline followed more slowly. Mother
was absurd, she thought, exasperation rekindling.
She'd wanted to go back and look in at Dad again
before she left the house, do something to insure
his comfort, smooth a covering, lower a shade. As
though Dad deserved consideration, the thought
continued, as though he were ill instead of sleeping
off a Friday night spree. Sharp words sprang to
her lips. She forced them back with an effort. Julia
didn't seem concerned. She stood, waiting for
Mother, on the walk at the foot of the steps, ab-sorbed in some personal preoccupation, singing half
under her breath-
"Oh, de cat am de fish fo' me
Wid de onions and de sassafras tea" -
The song changed the trend of Caroline's
thoughts. Julia had sung it at intervals during the
morning, in the bathroom as she dressed, while she
ate a hurried breakfast from a tray on the kitchen
porch. That was natural. The song had been run-
ning through her own head since Willie-Josey cut
the grass. But the fashion in which Julia sang it,
now, sharpened suspicions already aroused. She sang
it dreamily, as though it were a part of some re-
membered happiness, her clear light contralto giv-
ing the tune a romantic rhythm. Julia had some-
thing on her mind. This man who was coming from
Washington-
"If your friend-" she began, then asked care-
lessly, "Marshall? What's his name?"
The catfish song ended abruptly. "Scott Mitchell,"
Julia replied so promptly that Caroline knew she'd
been thinking of him as she sang. Julia's eyes lifted
to meet Caroline's glance. For an instant they were
dreamily unaware of what her prompt reply admit-
ted, then swiftly, consciously, the fringy dark lashes
fell.
"I was going to say-" Caroline went on blandly
"-that if he's interested in antiques, you should
wangle him a ride in that."Julia glanced at the car parked at the curb, then
back and up at Caroline, leaning against a post
above her on the porch. Her face, framed in the
wide circle of hat brim, looked faintly anxious, a
little excited, disarmingly lovely.
"You will be tactful, Cal," she said hesitantly.
"Curb your natural instincts, my sweet."
"Can't promise." Caroline's glance remained fixed
upon Julia. Julia was more than casually interested
in this Scott Mitchell, she thought, something she'd
glimpsed in her sister's face making a certainty of
suspicion. She'd tried hard enough to be casual, tell-
ing Mother it didn't matter whether he was invited
to stay for dinner or not, speaking of him as a
friend of Aunt Gena's and Uncle Clay's, even affect-
ing a pretty air of polite boredom about the whole
affair. Caroline was not deceived. Julia was excited,
really excited, and making an effort to conceal the
state of her emotions. Caroline's interest increased.
She was suddenly watchful and alert, conscious, too,
of a quite unreasonable sense of personal excitement.
"You'd better give me definite instructions," she con-
tinued. "Am I to entertain him or leave him here
alone on the porch-pardon me, the verandah-to
think wistfully of you? Do you suppose, by any
chance, he'd be interested in helping me work with
the dogs?"
Julia grimaced, then laughed, lightly, self-con-
sciously.
"I was afraid not." Caroline drew an exaggerated
sigh. "Don't you meet any veterinarians? I can takehim on a tour of the shrine," she went on briskly.
"They usually enjoy that. I'll show him the spot
where your sandbox stood."
"Oh, Cal-!" This time Julia's laugh chimed with
reluctant amusement. She stood looking up at Caro-
line, the appeal she hesitated to express in words
mirrored in the dark depths of her eyes.
Caroline ignored the appeal. "And the back
fence," she went on, warming to the possibilities of
the theme, "where you were marooned all one after-
noon while a skunk napped among the cucumber
vines. He'd be interested in the pear tree beneath
which you've gotten engaged twice every summer
since you were six years old. Is he the type who
would be likely to chip off bits of this and that if
I turned my back?"
"You idiot!" Julia laughed but her heart wasn't
in it. She appeared to be weighing the possible value
of a direct appeal. Caroline waited, pretending in-
difference, actually missing no shade of the con-
tradictory emotions reflected in Julia's face. This
wasn't merely an entanglement from which Julia
wished to be extricated gracefully, she thought.
There had been an endless succession of them. Julia's
charming insincerity was always getting her into
predicaments. This was different, though. Julia was
nervous, which wasn't like her, swinging between
pleasure and apprehension. What had Julia been
up to? The excitement Caroline had felt, unreason-
able though it was, hummed through her in a quick-
ening vibration."When he comes-" Julia said consideringly,
after an interval of silence, "just tell him-" She
paused as Mother came out on the porch. Caroline,
watching, saw her face clear, leaving no evidence
of anxiety or excitement. "Ready?" she asked with
her accustomed animation, with the air of interest
in their affairs which enchanted her elders. "Miss
Jenny will be anxious. She knows there's no one who
sets off her Georgian tea service as you do, Mother."
But Mother either had overheard or sensed the
situation.
"Ask Julia's guest for dinner," she said, looking
directly at Caroline. "I've told Louisa to fry the
chickens to-night. We'll make out with ham to-mor-
row. Bring up a jar of spiced peaches- No, the
brandied ones, since this seems to be an occasion.
Strawberry shortcake, Julia?"
"It doesn't matter," Julia said lightly. "If it's the
least bit of trouble, you aren't to bother."
"Strawberry shortcake, I think." Mother's voice
was composed but her eyes, meeting Caroline's, held
a glint of humor. The detached amusement with
which Mother viewed the activities of her daugh-
ters was unusual, Caroline thought. She seldom
fussed or interfered. It was as though her devotion
to Dad left her no time or energy for more than
a casual interest in them. That interest became pro-
nounced only when Dad was, in some way, involved
in their activities. The brandied peaches, which were
reserved for very special occasions indeed, indicated
that Mother had a personal interest in planning adinner for Julia's guest. Caroline was not surprised
when Mother said, as she walked down the steps,
"You might mention to Dad that Julia is having a
guest, a friend of Eugenia's from Washington. He
mentioned a business engagement, but I know he'll
want to be here."
Caroline was not so sure. For the past month
Dad had been making a week-end party of the Fri-
day night spree. Mother thought that a guest would
prove to be a counter attraction. It probably would.
Dad loved to make mint juleps for company and
urge them to repeated helpings of Louisa's excel-
lent food. He was at his best in any social gather-
ing, friendly, amusing, expansive. But how humiliat-
ing for Mother to be obliged to resort to subterfuge
to keep him at home.
Mother did not appear to feel any humiliation.
She smiled when Caroline said, "I'll tell him," called
a cheerful "Good-bye" and turned from the porch
toward the gate, moving with light unhurried steps
along the gravelled walk. Julia lingered, obviously
reluctant to leave the situation in Caroline's hands,
hoping for reassurance.
"Be an angel, Cal," she said coaxingly.
Caroline laughed, admitting defeat. "You look
like Bertie when he's begging for a biscuit," she
said. "Do your admirers tell you that you have
eyes like a spaniel? Go on, idiot. I'll be as tactful
as I can. Angels could do no more."III
THE POLKS' LIMOUSINE MOVED AWAY FROM THE
curb. Mother and Julia, sitting up unnaturally
straight on the high old-fashioned back seat, waved
through shining plate glass. Mother's gesture was
mechanical. Caroline saw her eyes lift to the sec-
ond floor windows. Julia's parting salute was fa-
miliar, the distress signal they'd invented when they
were children and used whenever either was in
need of the other's assistance. Clever of Julia to
have remembered it, Caroline thought, watching the
car turn at the corner and roll along the street
beyond the iron fence which bounded the Perrys'
side lawn. The wig-wagging fingers recalled difficul-
ties shared, crises avoided, hair-breadth escapes
from punishment or boredom or embarrassment.
She and Julia had learned early in life that in
union there was strength and that two heads were
more useful than one. Caroline capitulated as Julia
must know that she would. She returned the signal
in the instant before the car disappeared from her
view.
She loved Julia. Caroline dropped down on the
top step, cradled her knees in her arms, leaned back
against the post. They were fonder of each other
than sisters usually were, she thought, waving ab-sently at Mrs. Merryman sprinkling her flower
border on the shady side of the white house across
the street. There was less than two years difference
in their ages. She, Caroline, had never been forced to
suffer the humiliations to which an older sister sub-
mitted a younger. Mother's absorption in Dad had
thrown them upon their own resources. They had
been aware of their detachment long before they
were sufficiently mature to analyze or sit in judgment
upon their elders. They had lived in a close compan-
ionship, each relying upon the other, squabbling
often in private but presenting a united front toward
the world.
Yes, she loved her sister. But she had vowed
never again to act as an accomplice in Julia's de-
ceptions. There was simply no sense in encouraging
Julia. She couldn't expect to spend her life waltzing
like a butterfly over a garden without occasionally
pricking herself on a thorn. She did exactly that,
Caroline was obliged to admit. Julia had a simple
formula for evading the consequences of her
thoughtless insincerities. In a crisis she took the
course of least resistance and admitted everything,
relying upon her charm and the infatuation of any
second party involved to get herself out of a jam.
It worked. It worked out of all porportion to Julia's
just deserts. A lack of character, Caroline reflected,
and the luck of a three-legged cat. Or perhaps Julia
had never cared deeply enough for anyone or any-
thing to face an unpleasant issue.This man from Washington, though- There'd
been a change in her manner since she'd returned
from visiting Aunt Gena. She'd been more quiet
than usual, wanting to be alone, going downtown
for the mail, running to answer the telephone when
it rang. She had refused invitations, too. That wasn't
like Julia. Ordinarily, when she returned from one
of her numerous visits, she plunged with enthusiasm
into whatever activities were in progress at home.
And boning up on antiques last night. Still that
wasn't remarkable. She'd ploughed through Chaucer
last summer when the Merrymans' nephew, a pro-
fessor at Duke, was visiting across the street.
Something had happened to Julia. There was an
urgency in her appeal to-day which could not be
ignored. She was excited, not quite sure of herself,
swinging between pleasure and apprehension. Had
this Scott Mitchell aroused a genuine emotion? Or
had he failed to be impressed with Julia? That
would be sufficiently novel to pique her interest. But
he was driving from Washington to see her -
Was Julia in love with him? Again Caroline was
conscious of a quickening hum of excitement. She
marshalled her thoughts away from personal in-
trospection and concentrated on Julia. Strange, she
mused, you never thought of Julia being in love
but always of some poor devil being in love with
Julia. For Julia was not in love with Jay. Caroline
had suspected, at the time, that Julia had promised
to marry him merely because she was too kind-hearted to refuse when Jay was ill and going away.
If she'd had any charitable doubts, the thought con-
tinued a trifle grimly, Julia's subsequent behavior
would have dispelled them. Even though the en-
gagement hadn't been announced and she was free
to amuse herself as she pleased, Julia wouldn't have
wanted to flit from visit to visit collecting scalps if
she were, or had ever been, deeply in love with Jay.
Jay-Mrs. Merryman had turned on the lawn
sprinklers. Caroline, watching dreamily, saw Jay's
slight figure, the dark beauty of his sensitive face
through flashing rainbow arcs. Perhaps she'd never
seen him more clearly, she thought, slipping into
the personal introspection she had taken pains to
avoid. The way she felt about him distorted her
vision, perhaps, as now the refracted light of sun-
shine through spraying mist gave his imaginary fig-
ure a shifting brillance. The qualities which drew
her to him, the charm of his imagination, his silences,
his flashes of reckless gaiety, were illusionary, too,
perhaps. They fascinated her because she would
never know them intimately. He was not extraordi-
nary, perhaps, except in her glorification of him. She
had to believe that in order that she wouldn't be
too unhappy-
She wasn't unhappy, she told herself stoutly. She
couldn't remember a time when she hadn't loved
Jay or that Jay hadn't loved Julia. They'd gone to-
gether, the three of them, to Miss Rosalie's kinder-
garten, then on into the primary grades. They'dplayed together during vacations, after Jay had gone
away to school. She and Julia had visited in the
summer and sometimes during Christmas week, at
Castle Haven, the Summerfield place on the point
where the Choptank broadened out to meet the Bay.
They'd run in and out of the big brick house on
River Street, when Jay was at home, with the same
sense of familiarity that they felt at home. Caroline,
Julia and Jay. But always she had loved Jay and
Jay had loved Julia and Julia had loved them both
in the same affectionate, unconsidering way.
No, she wasn't unhappy. She'd fought that fight
and conquered emotions which had no justification.
She was grateful to Jay. She knew that Jay had
persuaded his mother to make Dad managing edi-
tor of the Observer which had come into her pos-
session at her husband's death as a part of his estate.
Half the county had been astonished at that appoint-
ment for Isabel Summerfield, beneath her decep-
tively feminine manner, was a shrewd woman and
Dad had never made a success of any enterprise
other than telling stories, collecting questionable
friends and concocting tall iced drinks. Half of the
county had not yet recovered from the greater as-
tonishment of discovering that Dad was doing an
excellent job. He seemed to have settled, at last,
into the niche for which his talents and disposition
had been created. Yes, for Mother's sake, for
Julia's, her own, she was awfully grateful to Jay.
But if Julia should fall in love, if she was capableof a stronger emotion than her mercurial affection,
if this man from Washington —
Caroline reined in her prancing thoughts, made
an effort to check the excitement that hummed
through her in singing vibrations. So what? she
asked herself with characteristic candor and common
sense. If Julia should change her mind about marry-
ing Jay, just what difference would that make to
her? Did she suppose, for an instant, that, in that
doubtful event, Jay would turn to her for consola-
tion? Certainly not, she told herself furiously, then
admitted with painful honesty that the fantastic idea
had set in motion the vibrations already subsiding.
A fine one she was to criticize her sister. Self-decep-
tion was a greater weakness than the deceptions
Julia practiced. She'd gotten herself in a state for
no reason at all. Well, she knew a remedy for that.
She'd clean out the kennels. Exercise was what she
needed, good hard grubby work.
...
A small roadster with a canvas top rattled under
the maples, jolted to a stop in front of the house.
Good heavens! Was that Julia's guest? Caroline
started up, then subsided with a sigh of relief. It
was Dusty Price in that second-hand car he'd been
threatening to buy. It wasn't beautiful, certainly,
but the motor was probably in good condition. Dusty
seldom made a bad bargain.
"Hi, Ca'line!" Dusty stepped out of the car and
approached the gate, walking in long unhurried
strides that gave an impression of indolence, hissandy head bare, a light summer suit draped with
debonaire nonchalance about his tall weedy figure.
"Hi-ya, Dusty!" Caroline returned the greeting
with warmth. She liked Dusty. His arrival was a
welcome diversion. "Is that the bus?"
"Pretty, isn't it?" He grinned up at her from the
foot of the steps, an ingratiating grin which nar-
rowed his bright blue eyes and displayed his even
white teeth. His voice, drawlingly pleasant added to
the impression of indolence suggested by his gait.
"I like the lines, don't you? The motor's sound as
a nut, though." He came up the steps and dropped
down beside Caroline. "What are you doing?"
"Thinking."
"That must be a strain on the brain cells." His
grin, wider now, robbed the words of any implied
discourtesy. "Pleasant thoughts?" he asked. "Not
that I'm curious"
Caroline laughed. "You're the most curious per-
son I know. You're never satisfied until you know
all the answers."
"Sassy, eh?" He regarded her with twinkling
amusement. "Well, skip it. A lady is entitled to all
the privacy she can get. I brought the mail for
your father." He took from his pocket a thin pack
of letters neatly enclosed in a rubber band. "Thought
I might as well bring the family mail, too. Helpful
little Dusty. How would you like to go down to the
ball game with me this afternoon?"
Caroline hesitated. The invitation was a temp-
tation. Dusty was an entertaining companion. Shehad discovered that this past year, since she'd been
doing odd jobs for Dad on the paper. Dusty was
Dad's man Friday. He handled the business details
of the Observer, subscriptions, advertisements,
printing jobs. The impression of indolence he gave
was misleading. Though he never hurried or raised
his voice, he, somehow, accomplished a great deal.
People in the town and throughout the county spoke
of Dusty with respect and admiration. His father
had edited the Observer. At his death Dusty had
returned to Queensville from Baltimore and a job
on the Sun. He and Dad had started to work
for the local paper at the same time. What Dad
would have done without Dusty, Caroline hadn't
the faintest idea.
"Well-?" he asked, breaking the lengthening
silence.
"I'd like to " Caroline began. She would. The
day was perfect for baseball. And in addition to
his sterling qualities Dusty was amusing. He had a
nimble wit and an amazing fund of information.
She'd grown to like the way he looked, though cer-
tainly he wasn't conventionally handsome. He had
prominent cheek-bones, a long nose, a wide flexible
mouth. His coloring was aptly described by his nick-
name. His sandy hair and brows were only a shade
darker than his skin which, in summer at least, was
sprinkled with pin-dot freckles. The nickname had
not been inspired by his appearance, however. It
was the major achievement of some prep school wit,
a comment, inversely expressed, upon Dusty's drawland languid gait. Dusty accepted it gratefully. He
disliked his own name which was Beverley Golds-
borough Price.
"Well-?" Dusty asked again.
"I can't. Honestly, Dusty. I'm sorry."
"Why not? Oh, come on, Ca'line. It ought to
be a good game. They say that young Fields on
the Easton team has plenty of stuff on the ball.
Shouldn't be surprised if he's going places. Good
southpaws are scarce."
"I've got to work with the dogs," Caroline said
regretfully.
"I'll help you to-morrow." Dusty's voice whee-
dled. "I'll buy you a bag of peanuts and an ice-
cream cone. To-day's pay day. Easy come, easy go.
Maybe a hot-dog, if you're good and don't throw
things at the umpire."
"Silly!" Caroline's spirits rose in response to his
infectious good humor. "I'm keeping anchor watch,"

ington. I don't know when he's likely to arrive."
"And where's Julia, pray tell?" The ironical note
in his voice was a compliment. Not the least of

her company to Julia's.
"She went with Mother out to the Polks'. She
forgot this was Garden Week when she made the

"So she leaves you holding the bag? Nice sisterly

"Then I won't waste my eloquence." Dusty made
motions which suggested a leisurely departure.
"You'll live to regret it, though. Here's the mail."
He stretched his long legs and seemed about to rise
from the step. "By the way," he added casually.
"Your father paid that bill for the new presses,
didn't he?"
Caroline turned in surprise. "I think so," she
said slowly. "I don't remember writing the letter.
Oh, yes" she added after a moment of thought.
"He said the agent came around to see how the

"I found a bill tucked back in the files behind a

"I know." Caroline smiled. Dad's haphazard dis-
position of routine details was a shared knowledge
and a mutual exasperation. "That's what you're
for, Dusty."
"Yeah." Dusty's voice was laconic. "I've been go-
ing through things, old bills and unpaid subscrip-

"Why?" Caroline felt vaguely uneasy. "They've
never done that before."
"I don't know." Dusty's casual manner was re-assuring. "We had a letter from Mrs. Summerfield's
lawyers. Didn't your father mention it? They are
making an appraisal of the entire estate."
"But what for?" Caroline persisted. In spite of
the reassurance in Dusty's manner the vague un-

"Mrs. Summerfield may want to liquidate cer-
tain assets," Dusty answered. "Jim Tilghman told
me she'd written him to sell Castle Haven if he

"Sell Castle Haven!" Caroline's voice, to her
own ears, sounded stressed with panic. "But why,
Dusty? Are they in difficulties, financially, I mean?"
"The Summerfields? Well, hardly! They'll still
be floating when there are Astors in the bread lines.
Probably too many unhappy memories connected
with Castle Haven. It was there Jay was hurt, you
know."
Caroline knew very well. She remembered with
painful clarity that tragic week-end at Castle Haven
last fall. Why had Jay gone off in the sailboat alone
in the face of a gathering storm? Was it because
Julia had driven into Cambridge with that boy from
Annapolis? For that question, recurring frequently,
as it had, she had found no convincing answer. Jay
was an excellent sailor. No one had worried about
him, even after the storm broke, until evening came
and he hadn't returned. It was a miracle that the
motor boat had found him, caught and crushed as
he was between the broken mast and the boom of
his boat. Half an hour later
-Caroline shivered in the warm sunshine of noon-
day in June. She would never forget how Jay had
looked when they brought him into the house, limp
and unconscious, his face as white as a marble mask,
that dreadful gash above his temple. But the gash,
fortunately, was above the temple. He had re-
sponded to treatment almost from the first. There
was a possibility, of course, that his lungs had been
permanently injured. That possibility, however, be-
came increasingly remote. Jay, in his letters to Julia,
seldom mentioned his health. But Miss Isabel kept
them informed. The dry air of the desert had been
splendid for him. His mother wrote that he was
almost completely well.

"Yes, I suppose," she said absently, her atten-

"Suppose what?" His expression was puzzled.
"What did you say?" she asked self-consciously.

"I didn't know-definitely." Caroline's breath

The feeling of panic subsided. Her heart steadied

subject for conversation.
"Do you think so?"
"Shouldn't wonder," Dusty repeated. "Young
Jay won't want it. At least I can't see him settling

"Why not?" Caroline asked, irritated by some im-
plied criticism she thought she detected in Dusty's

course, I suppose."
"I'd like to buy the paper," Dusty resumed, dis-

"Would you?" Caroline asked.
"Gosh, wouldn't I? If I could scrape up enough
money, I'd do it like a shot. My Dad always wanted

and buy shoes for. I haven't a chance either, I

"You've only yourself to buy shoes for," Caroline

Dusty glanced down at his feet.
"Yep, but look how much shoes it takes." He ele-
vated himself by slow degrees. "I'd better move

"You're passing up a treat. I'm an entertaining

"That one is magnificent. I was waiting for a

"You are sassy this morning." He placed the

myself open to another rebuff
"
"Come to dinner. Will you be back by seven
o'clock?"
"I will if I have to load that bus on a truck."His bright blue eyes twinkled at her appreciatively.

"'Bye, Dusty."
She watched until the roadster rounded the corner

The rest of the mail was for Dad. Two envelopes
were printed with the name of the firm which had

with the enthusiasm he displayed for any triumph

Not bad for a country paper. We'll turn your court
circulars out in fine style this spring."She didn't know what she had feared, what sus-
picion Dusty's questions had aroused. The measure

"Caroline!" Dad's voice called from the dining-

"Hello!"
"Sing the Star Spangled Banner, if sing you must."
The mellow voice was raised in cheerful entreaty.
"Sing anything for a change. I'm sick of that song."
"So am I," Caroline agreed quite as cheerfully.
"Are you dressed?"
"Dressed? Of course I'm dressed." Dad's laugh,

Shame on you, Caroline. What would Louisa think?"

"Red. The one you gave me for my birthday."
Caroline pinched one blossom on a stem with two

"You're fishing. I won't humor you, William."
But he did look amazingly well, she thought. There
was no visible evidence of last night's dissipation
except that the whites of his eyes were faintly tinged
with pink. He was still handsome, though no longer

he did, Caroline thought, walking toward the table.Worthy citizens who led respectable lives looked
their age. There was a quotation from the Bible

...
"White suit," she commented aloud. "The sum-

"Think I'll catch cold?"
"Heavens, no. It's as warm as July." She laid

"That editorial on the co-operative threshing pro-
ject isn't so bad." Dad folded the paper into a neat

"You got that idea across, William." Caroline

other. She supposed she felt that way because, when

fection for Dad, guiltily aware that, though she
loved and admired Mother, she felt a closer affec-
tion for her father.
If only Mother would stop trying to make him
over, accept him as he was, admit his imperfections
and enjoy his virtues, the atmosphere at home would
be more comfortable. But Mother, apparently, was
unable to admit an imperfection. In anyone else,
her daughters, her relatives, her friends. But not
in Dad.

minded him quietly."So I do." The admission was cheerful. "I sup-

without you. Well, you're right. I probably couldn't.
Not that I'd ever admit it to either of you. I'll look
these over later." He snapped the elastic around the

Caroline recalled Mother's request.
"Dinner is planned," she said. "You'll be here,
won't you?"
"Afraid not, baby. Have to see some men about
this school bus racket. I'll probably drive out through
the county with the committee. Good story in it
for next week's paper."
Dad's expression was guileless. He always had
plausible explanations for his absences from home. If
she questioned him, he would elaborate, giving her
names and details to strengthen his fabrication. Caro-
line, though she despised the practise and felt that
she did it clumsily, resorted to subterfuge.
"Julia will be disappointed," she said.
"Why?" Dad paused in the process of lighting his

"She's having a guest for dinner, a man she met

She shouldn't have spoken of Mother. A wary ex-

was familiar to Caroline. Dad knew that Mother
was up to her tricks. She was clumsy, Caroline
thought, her honesty offended. Julia would have
wheedled so prettily that Dad would have been un-

necessary? Why couldn't they be honest with each

amateur diplomats? Not that Mother and Julia were
amateurs. But she was, certainly. Mother shouldn't
have expected her -
"Do as you please," she said more tartly than she
intended. "We'll probably all survive if you aren't
here for dinner."
Twinkling amusement chased the wary expression
from Dad's eyes. He looked at her consideringly.
"You're no wheedler, are you, baby?" he said, in-
terpreting her thoughts with an accuracy which sur-
prised her. "Straight from the shoulder and no
tricks."Caroline laughed in embarrassed contrition.
"I'm sorry," she said. "I shouldn't have-blown
off."
"Why shouldn't you?" Dad asked reasonably.
"Feel better, dont you?"
"Much," she admitted. "But
"
"It's healthy to blow off now and then. Clears the
atmosphere. I wish-" Dad paused. Caroline waited
expectantly. But he did not complete the sentence.
"I'll get home for dinner," he said, and turned to-
ward the door.
"Seven o'clock," Caroline warned. "This is
Louisa's lodge-meeting night."
"I'll be home around five. Tell Louisa to crush
some ice but you pick the mint. We'll show Julia's
young man how a julep should be made. You don't
suppose he's a teetotaler, do you, like that Miss
Nancy she had here last month?" He turned at the
door to smile back at her. There was, unmistakably,
admiration as well as the affection to which she was
accustomed in his lingering glance. "Blow off when-
ever you want to," he said. "I'm all for it, baby."V
CAROLINE WALKED THROUGH THE COLONNADE TO-
ward the kitchen beyond. Louisa, bulky and shape-
less, a rich chocolate brown, sat on the kitchen porch
in a blizzard of drifting feathers.
"Dat Willie-Josey," she grumbled as Caroline ap-
proached. "Ah'll wring his neck. He promised to pick
these chickens and now he's gallivanted off to a ball
game. Just lemme git my hands on him. Shore's mah
name's Louisa Travers, Ah'll wring his neck!"
"Poor Willie-Josey!" Caroline laughed. She knew
that Willie-Josey's neck was in no danger. Louisa
adored her small bandy-legged husband. They were
"church married," as Louisa expressed it, implying
a distinction between this union and others less
orthodox.
"No good fo' nothin' but singin'," Louisa grum-
bled on in a muttered undertone. "Th'ow back his
head an' roar like a bull. What good all that roarin'
do me? Better he deaf an' dumb an' around when he
needed. Don't you put no dependence in no man,
Miss Ca'line. Dey all the same."
"Now, Louisa-" Caroline perched on the edge
of the biscuit block well away from flying feathers.
"You know you're proud of Willie-Josey."
"What Ah proud ob him fo'?" Louisa inquireddarkly. But her glowering expression brightened,
slowly melted into a beaming smile. "He do hab
qualities," she admitted. "He remind me some ob
yo' daddy, Miss Ca'line. He ain't got so much real
work in him but he pretty to have around."
Willie-Josey was the color of a dried fig. His legs
were shaped like the iceman's tongs. But Caroline
did not resent the comparison. She knew that Louisa,
in intention at least, had paid Dad a compliment.
She hastened to press an advantage.
"Dad wants you to crush some ice for him late this
afternoon," she said, "but he told me to pick the
mint. You sent him in parsley last time when Judge
Carroll was here."
"I didn't do no such thing," Louisa began, then
chuckled an admission. "Willie-Josey picked them
greens fo' Mister Will. It was dark as pitch an'
Willie-Josey he can't smell."
"There'll be guests for dinner to-night."
Louisa cocked a belligerent eye. "Miss Frances
ain't told me 'bout but one." She drew down the cor-
ners of her mouth. "Hot as it is, tired as Ah am,
comp'ny!" she grumbled.
"Dusty Price will be here, too."
"Oh, him. Ah don't call him comp'ny," Louisa
said, somewhat more mildly. "This friend of Miss
Julia's, though. Ah spec' Miss Frances goin' make
me wear mah new shoes an' dat ruffle on mah haid."
"Well, certainly, those aren't attractive." Caroline
indicated the magenta felt slippers which partiallyencased Louisa's feet. "I'll set the table for you,"
she added, slipping down off the biscuit block. "Listen
for the bell, please, Louisa. I've left the doors open
into the house. Miss Julia's friend should be here
soon. I'm going to work on the dogs."
"Miss Julia got plenty of friends. She do take
the cake," Louisa said admiringly. "When Mister
Jay comin' home, Miss Ca'line? Miss Julia ain't one
to sit back an' wait."
"I don't know," Caroline said shortly. "Call me
when Mr. Mitchell comes, and for heaven sake,
Louisa, put on your shoes before you answer the
bell."
Louisa's offended expression was a reproach. Caro-
line turned away, wanting to make amends, not
trusting herself to speak. Louisa meant no imperti-
nence in inquiring for Jay, she told herself as she
walked under the arbor, along the uneven brick walk.
Louisa had been with Mother for ten years. She was
devoted to all of them. Why shouldn't Louisa have
inquired for Jay?
She was all out of sorts to-day. Strange, a little
frightening, how a suggestion of no particular im-
portance had the power to destroy contentment.
She'd been cheerful this morning when she awoke,
happy because the weather was fine, anticipating an
uneventful but pleasant day. Julia's suspicious be-
havior had broken into her contentment and Dusty's
information had completed the havoc. The thoughts,
fancies, speculations which filled her mind had dis-torted her perceptions. Ordinarily she was not fool-
ishly sensitive or priggishly critical. Everything was
out of focus to-day as though her mental confusion
was a pair of spectacles through which she saw trifles
magnified and familiar truths distorted.
She knew the truth well enough. Jay was coming
home to marry Julia. There was no sensible reason
why she should anticipate that event with agitation.
You couldn't lose what you'd never had, she in-
formed herself with unsparing candor and common-
sense. She had nothing to relinquish except a romantic
idea. She certainly didn't intend to let an unrequited
affection ruin her life. The words, forming in her
mind, painted a picture so revoltingly sentimental
that, to her surprise, she laughed aloud.
The laugh, clear and spontaneously gay, reached
the kennels. A tumult of barking arose. As Caroline
came out from under the arbor, the dogs saw her
and the tumult increased. They hurled themselves
against the wire screening which inclosed the runway
in a frenzy of joyous welcome.
"Quiet, you mutts!" Caroline opened the gate in
the screening and stepped inside. "Do you want the
sheriff after you? Don't you know there's a town
ordinance against public nuisances, my friends?"
The spaniels remained ecstatically unimpressed.
They leapt up against Caroline, tails wagging, long
ears flapping, small silky bodies quivering with ex-
citement. Caroline dropped down on the coarse
cropped grass. They were beauties, she thought, sur-veying the results of her care with satisfaction and
pride. Gently cuffing the others away, she took Bertie
into her lap. He was, undeniably, the handsomest of

great promise for his future success in the field. Miss
Harriet would take him home with her at the end of

ing the separation.
Miss Harriet had invited her up to Connecticut
for the field trials. She thought she would accept the
invitation. Mrs. Merryman wanted Mary, and Judge
Carroll had spoken for Davie. She'd have money
enough to outfit herself appropriately and pay her
travelling expenses. Miss Harriet had asked her to
stay on indefinitely. Miss Harriet thought that she
had a real talent for handling dogs and had offered
to take her on as her assistant. It was an opportunity,
Caroline reflected. If she didn't want to stay at home
next year
Miss Harriet Adams was Mrs. Judge Carroll's
sister. She spent a part of every summer in Queens-
ville with the Carrolls. The rest of the year she lived
alone on a farm in Connecticut, raising cockers,
dashing to field trials and bench shows, running down

liked Miss Harriet. She'd given her Vicki last fall,
had Caroline send her to her kennels to be bred and
had kept in touch with the progress of the puppies.

really, than the preceding one when she had lived

-If Julia wasn't here- If Julia and Jay

ROGER POLK WAS GETTING OUT OF HAND AGAIN.
She'd done nothing to encourage him, Julia thought,
other than speak to him pleasantly which certainly
the laws of courtesy demanded. And here he was
trotting after her, getting in her way as she served
salad and sandwiches to his aunt's guests, looking
more and more like a moon-struck calf as the after-
noon advanced. If only he would stop apologizing
for what had happened after the Easter dance when
his conduct had been more emotional than discreet.
But here he was at it again, pressing close at her
side as she hurried across a stretch of velvet lawn
toward the tea table set under a copper beech.
"Oh, don't be ridiculous, Roger," she said. "I've
forgotten all about it."
"Forgotten!" His voice sounded wounded. She
glanced up at him, realizing that she had been tact-
less. He did look ridiculous. His features had not
been fashioned for noble suffering. His round face
and prominent ears were unbecomingly flushed. His
placid brown eyes returned her glance reproachfully.
Certainly, Julia thought, suppressing a smile, in a mo-
ment he would "Moo." But he looked awfully un-
happy. She slackened her pace, laid her hand on his
arm. "I didn't mean I'd forgotten the dance," shesaid gently. "I mean I'd forgotten any unpleasant-
ness. I loved dancing with you, Roger."
"Did you really?" he asked eagerly, his voice be-
coming foggy with emotion.
"Of course I did." Her lashes fell, then lifted,
giving him a glimpse of the melted stars in her eyes.
"I think of you as one of my oldest and dearest
friends. You do understand, don't you? It's simply
that I don't like being-mauled."
"I guess I was pretty clumsy." She had succeeded
again. The reproach in his voice was directed, now,
against himself. "You looked so pretty and were so
sweet to me that" He laughed awkwardly. "Well,
I guess I lost my head."
"You aren't to think of it again," she said. "We're
the same good friends we've always been." She gave
his arm a reassuring pat and removed her hand.
"You're too fascinating, Roger. I'm neglecting my
duties. Old Mrs. Atwood is waiting for her tea."
He quickened his pace as she hurried forward.
"Will you give me another chance?" he asked
humbly. "Will you let me take you to the Fourth of
July dance? I'm on the committee. I think it's going
to be good."
"Yes, certainly, Roger," Julia said absently, her
thoughts, now that she had conciliated him, no longer
concerned with her companion.
His delighted response made her suddenly aware
of having committed herself to a definite promise.
Now why had she done that? she asked herself in'dismay. He'd be more of a nuisance than ever.
People imposed on her good nature, she thought a
little irritably but with a feeling of complaisance
that her tact had saved an awkward situation. To
attend another dance with Roger Polk was the last
thing on earth she wanted to do. Her promise had
pleased him extravagantly, though. He was fairly
skipping along at her side. Irritability was lost in a
glow of pleasure. She did like to make people happy.
And, anyway, the Fourth of July was comfortably
remote.
She would have to take Roger in hand, she
thought, watching Mother pour tea into Haviland
cups, exchanging comments with Miss Jenny who
hovered about the tea table keeping an anxious eye
on the diminishing supply of refreshments. He'd
caught her off-guard to-day. She knew exactly how
to discourage unwanted attentions without hurting
the feelings of the suppliant or damaging her own
popularity. Solitude was necessary, though, and a
minimum of distractions. She couldn't employ the
gestures, the inflections, that flattered even as they
discouraged, while she was flying about the Polks'
lawns and garden with plates of salad in her hands.
She couldn't concentrate on Roger's feelings while
her thoughts were filled with speculations as to what
was going on at home. Later, perhaps, when they
had lunch together, she might have an opportunity
to deal with him, retaining his admiration but rid-
ding herself of his attentions.Two additional escorts, at lunch, had not figured
in her plans. But here they were. No sooner had she
settled herself into one of the wicker chairs Roger
had drawn into the shadow of the hemlock hedge
than Mr. Glenworthy, the young unmarried rector
who was officiating in the parish during old Dr.
Carmichael's sabbatical furlough abroad, approached
them, a canvas stool in one hand, a well-filled plate
in the other. Roger left his chair beside Julia to
assist the rector and his uncle, Mr. Richard Polk,
skipped into it. Roger, the victim of the old school
politeness Miss Jenny had instilled in him, was
obliged to sit on the grass at Julia's feet.
So there would be no opportunity to deal with
Roger at the moment. Julia turned attentively from
Mr. Glenworthy, who had had a year at Oxford and
was very British in manner and accent, to Mr. Rich-
ard, plump and pink and white, with curling white
hair, very bright blue eyes and the manners of an
anti-bellum beau.
"You enjoyed your years at Oxford, Mr. Glen-
worthy?" she asked, knowing he had had only one.
He gave her an appreciative glance and proceeded
to expatiate upon the beauties of the English coun-
tryside, turning his sculptured profile toward her as
often as politeness permitted. Caroline said that Mr.
Glenworthy had been ordained merely for the pleas-
ure of wearing a clerical collar and vest. That was
naughty of Caroline, but the cleric, the vestments on
Sunday, did set off to good advantage his fairness,his regular features, the spiritual expression which
had made him, already, the pet of all the old ladies
and the god of the Junior Guild.
The rector's voice, carefully modulated, had a
lulling effect. Julia's attention wandered from haw-
thorn hedges and cathedral bells. Had Scott arrived
yet, she wondered, feeling a stir of excitement as
she thought of him, consciously now, without inter-
ruption. What would Caroline's attitude be? Foolish
to be nervous, she told herself. Cal wouldn't let her
down. She'd been critical this morning, though, notic-
ing and sarcastic. She'd thought, sometimes, that
Cal was unhappy. There was no reason why she
should be, yet only unhappiness could account for
the sharp sarcastic way in which she sometimes spoke.
There was no sensible reason to worry. She wasn't

engagement. Scott wouldn't especially mind missing
the auction, though. That was merely an excuse for

different from anyone she'd ever known. She had no
idea what
Mr. Glenworthy paused significantly. Julia, feel-
ing that some comment was expected of her, caught

sciously heard.
"Oh, columbine!" she cried softly. "The English
columbine is beautiful, isn't it? I adore it."
Luck was with her. Mr. Glenworthy's appreciativeglance told her that she had made the expected

"Do you, Julia?" Mr. Richard asked brightly.
"Jenny has some fine ones. Come along, I'll take you

What did columbines look like, Julia wondered,

"Oh, but I do!" Julia protested, fearing that Mr.

"The bees and the honey jar," Mr. Richard mur-

He was more attractive than she had remembered
him, she thought, watching him speak in turn to Mr.
Richard, to the rector, to Roger Polk. His easy
manner was a source of great satisfaction to her when
it was not a personal annoyance. In the presence of
his vitality Mr. Richard seemed suddenly older and
the young rector looked anemic. His well-tailored
clothes, fitting smoothly over his broad shoulders,
were a delight to the eye. But he was attractive in a
physical sense only, she reminded herself. She couldnever really admire anyone who was so self-centered
and insensitive.
"My mother is here," she said when the amenities
had been completed. "And you must be starved." She
would not fail in courtesy or hospitality, she thought,
walking toward the tea-table. She had hoped that
she had misjudged him and was disappointed to find
that the impressions she'd formed in Washington
were correct. But she would not let him know that
she had thought of him at all, except as someone
who had, in his way, been pleasant to her, a casual
acquaintance met on a visit away from home.
Scott walked beside her. Her companions at
luncheon marched behind them three abreast.
"I'm not hungry," he said. "I had a delightful
lunch with your sister. She and the dogs made me feel
very much at home."
What had Cal been up to? But this feeling she
had wasn't jealousy. She'd never been jealous of
Caroline. It was just that she was disappointed in
him. She had expected, she had hoped -
"You didn't have luncheon in the kennels, I hope,"
she said with an airy little laugh. "Caroline prac-
tically lives with those dogs." She tilted her head to
look up at him, well aware of how becomingly the
hat brim framed her face. "Do you think we are
alike?"
"In appearance a little, perhaps," he replied. "In
temperament I imagine you and your sister are quite
different.""Of course you saw Caroline at a disadvantage,"
she suggested, hating herself for the implication she
knew the words contained, annoyed that the question,
usually productive of compliments, had somehow
missed its mark.
"I wasn't aware of any particular disadvantage.
I thought she was charming and friendly and natural.
She impressed me as being a completely genuine
person."
Had she been too obvious? Julia glanced away,
feeling her cheeks grow warm. Did he suspect that
she was fishing for compliments? Why had he driven
from Washington to see her if he thought her in-
sincere? She walked beside him, silent for a moment,
unable to phrase a comment which would change the
subject gracefully, too angry to speak.
His hand cupped her elbow, sending a vibration
up along her arm. It was unfair that he should be
physically so attractive, she thought, since he was the
sort of man she could never admire. She slackened
her rapid progress, fearing that he might interpret
her speed as an expression of injured pride. She cer-
tainly would not give him the satisfaction of know-
ing that any attitude of his could affect her in the
least.
"I'm awfully glad to see you," he said, lowering
his voice. "If we could manage to lose your friends-
Do you always move about with a guard of honor
following at ten paces to the rear?"
A natural laugh of amusement bubbled up in her
throat. Her escort must look ridiculous, marchingalong three abreast, the rector making conversation,
Mr. Richard audibly puffing, Roger sulkily silent. She
was moved, too, by Scott's expression of pleasure at
seeing her again. And the vibrations from the touch
of his hand on her elbow were certainly not unpleas-
ant. This was her opportunity to establish the mood
of their afternoon and evening together, a light
mood of gaiety and mutual amusement. All she had
to do was permit the laugh caught in her throat to
escape |
She did not permit it to escape. The faint sugges-
tion of irony in his eyes, his implication that she was
not sincere, still rankled.
"I'm afraid I'm still on duty," she said. "Mr.
Richard has some English columbine he wants to
show me. Older people are offended so easily, don't
you think? And Mr. Richard is really a dear."
She had meant to convey an impression of con-
sideration and kindness, tinged ever so faintly with
regret. She did not need the lift of his eyebrow or
the sudden quirk at the corners of his lips to tell
her that she had failed again. The words, in her own
ears, sounded smug and rehearsed, like the prattle
of an insufferably good little girl in a Sunday School
book. She was relieved that they had reached the tea-
table and that any comment which Scott might have
made was prevented by his response to her introduc-
tion to Mother.
She had to go through with it now.
"May I leave Scott here with you, Mother?" sheasked, so formally that Mother looked her surprise.
"He assures me that Caroline gave him luncheon,
but perhaps he could manage a cup of tea. Will you
excuse me, please, Scott. I'm going with Mr.
Richard."
Mr. Richard, arriving at the tea-table with Roger
and the rector, appeared to be considerably sur-
prised. He recovered his aplomb, however, with ad-
mirable promptness, made excuses to the gentlemen
for depriving them of Julia's company and gallantly
offered her his arm.
Julia regretted the role she had assumed. The
sun was hot and her sandals pinched and Mr. Rich-
ard, though a dear, undisputably wheezed. The urge
to glance back at the tea-table was too strong to be
controlled. Scott had pulled up a chair and was talk-
ing, with apparent interest, to Mother and Miss
Jenny. She saw only the back of his head above the
chair but she had the uncomfortable feeling that he
was aware of her backward glance. As she walked
on beside Mr. Richard, responding with polite ani-
mation to his sprightly remarks, she vowed that she
would put Scott Mitchell in his proper place if it was
the last thing she ever did.VII
DINNER WAS PROGRESSING PLEASANTLY. JULIA,
seated beside Dusty Price and across the table
from Scott, found that she was enjoying the meal.
She hadn't expected to. She had permitted Mother
and Scott to bear the burden of conversation without
her assistance as they drove home from the Polks'
in Scott's handsome roadster which she remembered
with such an odd mixture of emotions from her visit
in Washington. She had joined Mother in urging
Scott to stay for dinner, but coolly she thought, and
with no display of eagerness. Urging had not been
necessary. He had accepted promptly, without, ap-
parently, being aware of any lack of warmth in her
manner.
Later, however, her mood had altered. In the in-
terval before dinner, when they sat on the porch, the
men with juleps Dad had mixed, she and Mother
and Caroline sipping sherry, she felt that she had
misjudged him. He talked to the others but always
his glance came back to her, informing her that she
was the center of his thoughts, the inspiration for
his agreeable conversation.
She hadn't forgotten the vow she'd made. But it
no longer occupied the foreground of her thoughts.
Julia's response to any form of gaiety was swift andinstinctive. She was unable to bear a grudge for any
protracted period of time. Her natural desire to
please reasserted itself, her enjoyment of each mo-
ment as it came without anticipation of the one to
follow.
And this was a nice party. You could always de-
pend upon Mother to add the touches which made
all the difference. Julia, not much given to preoccu-
pation upon such subjects, wondered, fleetingly, how
Mother managed to convey an impression of grace-
ful living, even of simple elegance, when she had so
little material assistance. The lace table cloth was
darned, but so cleverly that the imperfections didn't
show. The mass of rambler roses in a silver bowl
which Dad had won as a prize for trap-shooting or
one of the other odd and quite useless accomplish-
ments in which he excelled, blended with the reddish
tone of the old cherry table, the lighter red sea-weed
pattern on Grandmother Peyton's Haviland dinner
set, preserved intact, by superhuman vigilance,
through the years. The candle light made a center of
soft brilliance in the gathering dusk, moved flicker-
ingly over silver and glass, over the deep soft yellow
of Caroline's dress, over Scott's brown hands.
They were a handsome family, she thought, only
half hearing the political gossip from Washington
which Scott was relating, in which Dad and Dusty,
and occasionally, Caroline, interrupted with com-
ments and questions. She found her life quite satis-
factory on the whole. The house they lived in wasjust a house and somewhat run down, at that. The
fine old furniture Mother had inherited from her
mother had been sold, piece by piece, mainly to Aunt
Gena. But Mother made the most of second best,
waxing and polishing and painting, making effective
draperies and curtains from cheap materials, never
admitting poverty, covering darns with rambler roses,
and yet retaining that proud little air she had of
dwelling in marble halls.
She couldn't really feel sorry for herself when
Aunt Gena spoke with indignation of Dad's utter
worthlessness and the afflictions it had brought upon
the family, though, because she knew Aunt Gena
expected her to, she looked as downcast as she pos-
sibly could. She'd never been conscious of any par-
ticular privation. She and Caroline had had pretty
clothes, when they were children, and parties and
treats. Aunt Gena had sent them both to Manor
Hall where they had made friends who could be
counted on for invitations and house parties and fun.
Now she and Caroline each had an income from the
trust fund Grandmother Peyton had established for
them, a tiny income, to be sure, but enough for
clothes and traveling expenses and their own enter-
taining. Mother had a small income from the trust
fund, too. Grandmother Peyton having watched Dad
make "drakes and tails" of Mother's inheritances
from Grandfather Peyton and Great-aunt Francie
had been more cautious than they in the disposition
of her own estate.Dad had his points, she thought, looking at him
as he sat at the head of the table, hearty and hand-
some in white linen, a little flushed by the juleps, ex-
uding an atmosphere of expansive hospitality. She'd
rather have him for a father than Uncle Clay who
was so formal and ceremonious and carried dyspepsia
tablets in his pockets. She thought that Mother, in
spite of everything, was more contented with her lot
than Aunt Gena was. Certainly she did less complain-
ing. Julia was not analytical. She found introspection
dull and usually abandoned it when any distraction
presented an excuse.
Louisa, coming in to clear the table for dessert,
provided an adequate distraction. Her round brown
face looked very solemn under the frill of organdy
which Mother demanded for state occasions, and she
moved mincingly, an indication that she was wearing
her new shoes. When she had a home of her own,
Julia thought, she would have a butler and maids
correctly costumed. But Louisa was a part of the
atmosphere of home. Aunt Gena's Emily, in her
plum-colored silk and high-heeled slippers, would be
ridiculously out of place here. She felt a warm rush
of affection for her home, her family, for fat brown
Louisa mincing around the table, mumbling under
her breath. She felt relaxed and happy. She was hav-
ing a very nice time.
A remark not directed to her, threatened the
felicity of her mood.
"You're interested in antiques," Caroline re-
marked to Scott during a lull in the conversation.He admitted that he was. Julia held her breath.
Was Caroline going to tell him that she'd spent last
night reading up on furniture?
But Caroline didn't. The glance she sent her sister
through the candle light was mischievous but prom-
ised restraint. Mother went on to express her regret
that Scott had missed the sale at "Fairlee," de-
scribing the place as it had been when the Malcolms
lived there, before they had been obliged to sell.
Then Dad launched into an account of the fox-hunts
he had attended at "Fairlee," in the days of tem-
porary prosperity immediately following Great-aunt
Francie's death, Julia supposed.
She relaxed again. Caroline wouldn't let her down,
she reminded herself. There was usually mischief
brewing, though, when Caroline and Dusty Price
were together. He encouraged Caroline to be critical
and sarcastic. Not that she didn't like Dusty, she re-
flected. He could be very amusing and, of course,
everyone knew he was smart. But his flippant manner
was sometimes out of place. He'd been here for

been embarrassed at the way Caroline and Dusty

instinct she had in such matters Julia knew that

love with him? You couldn't tell. Whomever she
married she'd probably make flippant remarks on

dogs to remember to go to the church.
Caroline looked pretty to-night. Julia's thoughts,
with unconscious avoidance, sheered away from the
subject of marriage. Yellow was becoming to her.
The candle light brightened the golden tints in her
hair and warmed the dusky rose in her cheeks. Scott
wasn't seeing her at a disadvantage now. The re-

amber of her present contentment. What had pos-

herself. It was simply exasperation. Probably the
way she'd acted, afterward, imagining an intended
slight in everything Scott said was simply because she
was ashamed of herself. Probably-
Again a diversion offered an escape from laborious
introspection.
"Why are you so pensive, my sweet?" Dusty
