"Probably because I think it's a becoming expres-

"I thought as much." His grin was appreciative.
"But I didn't expect you to admit it.""Life can hold surprises for you, then?"
"You're making progress. That was almost worthy
of Ca'line."
"You must learn to control your voice, Dusty. I'd

He opened his eyes, then narrowed them drolly.

But if Dusty observed that she was unusually

"Yes," he replied. "I've been in Washington only

"What about the 'Rod and Gun' club, Dad?"

Caroline's twinkling glance restrained Julia's pre-

"Next week? Tuesday?"
"Fine. That will give Robert a chance to sweep

"Thank you, Mrs. Perry. I won't forget."
Scott walked to the door with Mother, paused

"You are responsible, you know."
"Then I can't very well refuse. I'll lay in a supply

She stood beside him. His hand cupped her elbow.
She felt light and happy and agreeably excited. She

"I'm wondering," she said demurely, "how you
will look with a beard."VIII

and fro. Dad had forgotten, again, to oil the springs.

The silence was becoming uncomfortable. Grand-

"I went to school. My father was a foreman in
a wood milling plant. I worked vacations and sat in

"And then?" she asked attentively.
"I went to Penn State on a scholarship and later
to the University of Pennsylvania law school."

reading in the newspapers. No," he concluded, "I

sister, generously wanting to make amends. "Caro-
line is right," she went on in a tone of rueful self-
mockery. "Being pleasant to people does clutter up
one's life. If I'd gone to the sale with you this after-
noon, I wouldn't have promised to help the rector
with a pageant or to go to a dance with Roger Polk."
She knew that her candor surprised and charmed
him. He moved closer to her, so close that his shoul-
der touched hers and in the silence she imagined she
heard the quick beating of his heart. She had looked
forward to this moment, deliberately followed a lineof attack which would result in his unconditional
surrender. Now that the moment had arrived, she
felt confused and a little frightened. She had not
anticipated that her own heart would beat so quickly,
that his shoulder touching hers would arouse in her
this aching sense of desire. She remained motionless,
scarcely breathing, waiting for what the next mo-
ment would bring.
He bent toward her, not touching her, so close
that she felt his breath against her cheek.
"Julia," he asked, half laughing, but with an un-
dertone of emotion in his voice, "are you as ingenuous
as you seem or are you as clever as the devil?"
Her reply was unpremeditated.
"I don't know," she said with complete honesty
and an alarming sense of helplessness. "Honestly,
Scott, I don't know."
But his question, her reply, had a steadying effect.
The moment passed. He sat back and lit a cigarette.
Julia felt both disappointed and relieved, but mis-
tress of her own emotions again. She set the ham-
mock swaying with the tip of her slipper against the
floor, hummed lightly under her breath.
"You're humming our theme-song," he reminded
her when he had lit his cigarette.
"Am I?" she asked, relieved that her voice
sounded natural, a little annoyed by the lack of deep
emotion in his. "Willie-Josey cut the grass this morn-
ing. When he does, everybody in the house sings or
hums that song all day."
"They're delightful, your family. Will they mindif I'm rather a nuisance these next two or three
weeks?"
"Are you planning to be a nuisance?" It was a
game, now, and she knew all the rules. She did not
like to be touched. She liked, though she would not
have made the admission openly, to be not only a
woman to a man, but a dream, a phantom of delight,
a bright distant star. She wanted them to love her
almost beyond the point of control. But she insisted
upon control, diverting intentions by lightly rebuk-
ing devices which charmed while they reproved. Scott
was behaving beautifully. Strange that the sense of
disappointment, lingering beneath her surface poise,
should continue to be so acute

..
"Definitely," he said in reply to her question.

She promised him her occasional companionship,

The evening passed pleasantly. At no point was
Julia obliged to use any of her rebuking devices.Scott continued to behave himself beautifully. She
was relieved, she told herself, as the sense of disap-

He left in time to take the last ferry over to

had so oddly disturbing an effect upon her poise,

Heaven knows how, to cast a spell."

twenty-two. Beautiful child. The tones of his voice
echoed through her mind. How lovely the stars were
to-night. She leaned back against the post at the
gate, looking up through the foliage of the maples.The fragrance of mock-orange blossoms was breath-
lessly sweet.
"who has learned, Heaven knows how, to cast

THE REHEARSAL WAS ALREADY IN A CONDITION
approaching chaos. Julia, walking along the bricked
path which led past the rectory bay windows to
the back yard beyond, stopped dead in her tracks.
Such a fuss over nothing, she thought irritably, and
reminded herself that it wasn't because Scott poked
fun at the pageant but because she was thoroughly
bored with it herself that she was determined, this
afternoon, to resign from the cast.
There was fuss enough, certainly. Bryan Dudley,
home from art school for vacation, his costume de-
signs spread out on a hickory table under the mul-
berry tree, was arguing almost hysterically with Miss
Jenny Polk and Mrs. Judge Carroll who, as rep-
resentatives of the Senior Guild, were to have charge
of the costumes. His voice, rising shrilly above the
more modulated tones of the ladies, reached clearly
to Julia, standing unobserved in the screen of a lat-
tice hung with morning glory vines.
"But you can't get the same effect with cambric,"
he was protesting. "The whole effect must be one
of richness and restaint. The nigger pink will simply
scream against the burgundy I've planned for Pe-
truchio's cloak."
"Don't get excited, Bryan," Mrs. Judge Carroll,pink and white and bland as a fair weather cloud,
replied soothingly, while Miss Jenny, looking a little
distressed, suggested that they might be able to
manage sateen and it came in such pretty shades.
At a little distance, beyond the summer house,
Mr. Glenworthy was attempting, by precept and
example, to draw sparks from Roger Polk who was
proving to be a somewhat phlegmatic Petruchio, and
pretty Marjory Lane, sixteen and silver-blonde, with
wide blue eyes and a painted cupid's-bow mouth,
recited Katherine's lines in soft singing tones which
might have been, and probably were, a litany to the
rector. A few of the little girls who were to be
fairies in a scene from "A Mid-summer Night's
Dream," ran about, getting in everybody's way, slyly
resisting Allie Carter's efforts to keep them under
control.
No, she simply couldn't go on with it, Julia de-
cided, watching the scene before her with irritation
which rapidly lightened into amusement. It wasn't
as though she was adding anything to it, she told
herself bracingly. It was going to be a mess and
what was the use of having Aunt Gena send her a
rented costume if the others got themselves up in
Hallowe'en costumes made of paper muslin? Be-
sides, all this rehearsing was taking up a great deal
too much of her time and she had practically prom-
ised Scott to withdraw from the cast.
Logic was all on her side. She had every right to
do as she pleased with her time. She was, notwith-
standing, conscious of the hollow feeling at the pitof her stomach which she experienced whenever she
was obliged to decline an invitation or refuse a re-
quest. Wishing the next few minutes were safely
over, she stepped out from the shelter of the lattice
and advanced into the confusion.
Bryan Dudley saw her first.
"Greetings, fair Juliet," he called, almost knock-
ing the table over in his haste to reach her side.
"But soft! what light through yonder
window breaks?
It is the east and Juliet is the sun!"
He was really too ridiculous, Julia thought, sur-
pressing a giggle as she remembered that Scott had
called Bryan "the lad with the delicate air" when
she'd introduced them in the drug store one evening
last week. He wore his hair a little too long-what
a disappointment it must be to him that it didn't
curl! His shirt, with a collar so broad it was prac-
tically a stole, was opened poetically at the throat
and was, in color, a deep cerulean blue. The artist
as a young man -
"Hello, Bryan," she said, making her voice sound
clear and musical, because she remembered, too, that
he had told her it reminded him of silver bells chim-
ing when he had struggled to paint her portrait last
summer. She'd hated posing. It made her itch all
over. But it had seemed to mean so much to him
because his family hadn't much patience with his
ambitions and people thought he had ability though
the portrait of her hadn't turned out very well.He stood before her, blocking her progress. She
thought he would surely have kissed her hand if
there hadn't been so many people watching. Instead,
he gave it a squeeze full of meaning and looked
into her eyes.
"Come," he said, rousing as though from a trance
and speaking briskly. "You must help me persuade
these dear ladies that in a pageant the costuming is
everything. I can't seem to make them understand
that you can't get rich tones with cheap materials."
She spoke very nicely to the ladies, returning Mrs.
Judge Carroll's smile with a glance which indicated
that she thought Bryan was ridiculous, too, and yet
was sorry for him, so sensitive and ambitious, so
misunderstood at home. Miss Jenny looked greatly
relieved.
"Well, now that you're here, Julia," she said, "I
suppose we'll get somewhere."
She modestly disclaimed being of the slightest
possible use. But she couldn't help realizing that her
appearance had charged the atmosphere with an
animation it had not had before she came. She
simply could not help knowing that she had an en-
livening effect upon any gathering. It was silly and
ungrateful to deny it. Mr. Glenworthy hurried for-
ward to greet her, followed by Roger and Marjory.
Allie Carter left her fairies to their own devices,
wanting to be included in the little stir of excite-
ment and interest which centered about Julia.
"I'm afraid you'll be detained a little," Mr. Glen-
worthy said apologetically. "We won't be able torun through your scene until our Romeo has com-
pleted his activities at the bank."
She must tell him at once, she thought, catching at
her ebbing determination. But he looked so dis-
tracted, poor dear, and quite like any other mortal
in a white shirt and flannel trousers with grass stains
on the knees. He looked at her trustingly, as the
others looked, except little Marjory Lane, who was
noticing every detail of her appearance with the
ardent intentness of a novice taking pointers from a
master, and Allie Carter, whose pale eyes behind
glasses were fixed upon Mr. Glenworthy as he talked
to her, Julia. For an instant she wavered. She could
endure the boredom of rehearsals, she thought. It
wouldn't hurt her and it meant so much to the oth-
ers, to the rector. Besides, it was for a good
cause
But she had practically promised Scott- Think-
ing of him, remembering that he was waiting, now,
to take her to the camp for supper, revived her
determination.
"May I speak to you, Mr. Glenworthy?" she
asked, speaking calmly and sweetly, though she
was more than ever conscious of the yawning cav-
ern in her stomach.
"Privately?" he asked, looking a little startled,
but excited, too, and subtly pleased.
"If the others will excuse us." She smiled in
apology, missing no one in the group, not even Allie
Carter who looked as though she could cheerfully
have slain her on the spot."Certainly." Mr. Glenworthy led her to the sum-
mer house where, he had told her, he went every
evening to meditate. He dusted off the seat with
his handkerchief in deference to her white linen
dress, then sat beside her. "I hope you aren't go-
ing to tell me that I'm to lose my Juliet," he said.
She had meant to lead up to the subject gradually,
softening the blow. Now there was nothing to do

"I'm terribly sorry," she said, searching frantic-

didn't realize when the date was set that I probably
wouldn't be at home. I thought it would be more

minute."
His face looked stricken.
"But there is no one else I can ask," he said.
"I'm sure Allison Carter will be glad to take my
place," she suggested. That was mean. But she owed
Allie a dig for that glance she had given her. After
all she'd done for Allison Carter, too, including

good time. Righteous indignation silenced self-ac-
cusation. She hadn't deserved that from Allie-
"She wouldn't do at all," Mr. Glenworthy pro-

sides, she has learned all of the nurse's lines." He

"I'm terribly sorry." Her voice shook with very
real distress. That she was exasperated with herself,
did not mitigate the pain it gave her to disappoint

phone or written a charming note? That was always
easier. She wasn't required, then, to witness the

"I'm going to throw myself on your mercy," he

I don't need to tell you how limited the talent is.
Most of the pageant will be pretty amateurish, I'm
afraid. I'd counted on the scenes from 'Romeo and
Juliet' to compensate for the others. It is unfair,
perhaps, to place the burden of responsibility upon
you but I know no one else who would look-who

clear up the debt before Dr. Carmichael's return.
It is sad, perhaps, that it should be so, but clergy-
men must advance their own interests and I shall be
seeking an appointment next year."
This was dreadful. She saw the rector as he was,
an ambitious young man, fearing for the future,
anxious to make a good impression in this his first
charge. Common sense told her that his whole future
career did not hinge upon whether or not she played

whether genuine or assumed, influenced her reactions
more strongly than common sense. She enjoyed giv-
ing happiness. It was intolerable to her to be obligedto watch anyone suffer through her fault. She finally
promised the rector that she would see the pageant
through.
His gratitude gave her a pleasant glow. The glow
sustained her through a rehearsal of the scenes from
"Romeo and Juliet" when, perched precariously on
a step-ladder she uttered Juliet's immortal lines to

looking self-conscious and a little moist in his busi-
ness suit of heatherbloom tweed.
The glow dimmed, somewhat, when, the rehearsal
over, she walked past the rectory bay windows, ac-
companied by Bryan Dudley and Roger Polk. Scott's

had errands in town. She'd been longer than she in-

cowardly aversion to unpleasantness. She felt, all at

guilty, again, of evasions.
Her spirits lifted, however, when he saw her
walking around the side of the rectory, waved to

sense of excitement and pleasure which she invariably

Just his clothes were enough, she thought, if he

and better place. To see him waiting there for her,

didn't love Scott. Or was this feeling she had, now, as

She bade her escorts farewell, hearing her voice as
Scott must hear it, as Bryan obviously did, a chim-
ing of silver bells.
"I'm sorry I kept you waiting," she said as Scott
settled her against the deep leather upholstery.
"I made good use of the time." He took his place
behind the wheel, turning to smile at her before he
set the car into motion. "I had a hair-cut, bought
groceries, watched a dog-fight. A pretty exciting
afternoon."
She had hoped he would tell her that he had
missed her, that the waiting had been intolerable.
Scott never said things like that. It was a part of

strengthened determination to put him in his proper

"Do you mind the top down?" he asked as the
car moved away from the curb.
"I love it," she said.
"I believe you mean that."
"Why should you question it?" she asked, a little
nettled.
"Because you didn't, at first. You pretended
politely, but you hated to be blown."
His penetration annoyed her, but, oddly, pleased
her, too. Why was it that, in comparison with the
almost insulting remarks he made to her, compli-
ments seemed insipid?
"I didn't like being blown," she admitted, laugh-
ing. "I've never liked being tousled."
"You should," he said. "You're lovely when
you're tousled."
Her heart sang. Compliments, when they were
rare, had a particular value. She settled back against
the upholstery as the car purred smoothly along the
road out of town, really loving the wind blowing
through her hair, loving the blue sky above them,
the fields they passed foaming with daisies andQueen Anne's lace. But her contentment lasted only
until he spoke again.
"Did you chuck this pageant thing?" he asked,
presently.
"Well-" she temporized. "Well, I'm afraid not
exactly."
"You didn't tell the pretty rector that you were
sorry but he was taking up too much of your time,
that you preferred to spend it with me?"
"Well, hardly, Scott." His unmitigated conceit
shattered her contentment. She sat erect against the
leather upholstery, folded her hands in her lap,
looked as indignant as possible on the chance that he
might be looking at her.
"What did you tell him?"
There was nothing to be gained from retreating
into an offended silence. His voice sounded amused.
If she looked at him she knew exactly the expres-
sion she would find in his eyes. He remained ex-
asperatingly unimpressed by what she thought of as
her sensitivity to the feelings of others. But honey
caught more flies than vinegar. She gave a clear little
laugh and settled comfortably into the seat again.
"I tried," she said ruefully, knowing that Scott
enjoyed her delicately self-mocking confessions, "but
it didn't work. I meant to chuck the whole thing
because it is perfectly ridiculous. If you had heard
Bryan Dudley, Scott, your 'lad with the delicate
air.' He was practically hysterical on the subject of
velvet and cloth of gold while the costume committee
strained to manage sateen. And Roger Polk tryingto be masterful and poor Mr. Glenworthy with
grass stains on his pants from showing him how it
should be done
"So you felt sorry for him," Scott said, when she
paused for breath. "You told him that, yes, cer-
tainly, you'd be Juliet on a step-ladder."
"But I did feel sorry for him. This is the first
appointment he's had and he wants to make a good
showing so that the Bishop will recognize him as an
up and coming young clergyman and a credit to the
Cloth. So I thought that if, as he intimated, I

it was as little as I could do
"
Scott's laughter interrupted her.
"Julia," he said, "you're incorrigible."
"I don't think that's very kind of you."
"I adore you." He slackened the speed of the
car. "You're the most diverting as well as the most
beautiful young person I've ever known."
Her heart sang again. There was a note in his
voice- She sat still and tense, looking straight
ahead, conscious of an alteration in his mood, wait-
ing, waiting
"But you're a little evader, you know." The tone
in his voice which had aroused expectations was
gone. He was laughing at her, tenderly, to be sure,
but with disconcerting amusement. "Everyone seems
to take you perfectly seriously," he continued, "but
I'm on to you. Your kindness is a protective mech-
anism. She who smiles and runs away, lives to smile
another day."She felt deflated, a good deal incensed. But only
for an instant. The car slowed to a stop. His hand
touched hers and, glancing up, she saw that he was
looking at her with an expression half-serious, half-

"Don't be offended," he said gently. "I laugh at
you because I love you, because I love you, not
any of the characters you assume. Or perhaps I love

What, precisely, did he mean? A pulse beat in
her throat as though a bird were imprisoned there.
Her nerves, strung tightly like harp strings, gave off
soundless singing vibrations. She could not speak.
She perceived, with disturbing clarity, that now, in
this moment, when she was moved by genuine emo-
tion, none of her pretty gestures or devices would
serve either to hold him or to dismiss him grace-
fully, charmingly, with just the right measure of re-
gret. She felt, suddenly, defenceless, awkward, inept.
She let her lashes fall, sat, in silence, looking down
at his hand covering hers.
He lifted his hand, presently, and her hand felt
curiously bereft. She was glad she had not spoken,
though, for in a moment he was talking about boats,
interestedly, and with no evidence of the emotion
she had seen in his eyes.
She relaxed against the upholstery. The car shot
forward along the road.XI
THE SUN HAD SET BEYOND THE LOW SHORELINE
across the cove. Already the reflections lingering on
the surface of the water were losing their brilliance,
fading to soft amethyst, amber, rose. The cedars on
the opposite shore rose in dark cones against the
dimming luminance of the sky.
There was no wind. The cove was very still,
holding in its wood-fringed cup the deep waiting
stillness of approaching twilight. A fish jumped,
making a tiny splash. Julia glanced at Scott. He
smiled, watching her, seated beside her at the end
of the dock.
"It startled me," she said.
"One little fish?"
His smile was caressing, but he said no more.
Julia, too, was silent. They had sat here, since sup-
per, she leaning against the piling, Scott smoking
beside her. At first she had talked, telling him, be-
cause he had asked her, of her school days at Manor
Hall, of her trip to Bermuda with Aunt Gena. He
had listened in an abstracted silence, hearing her
voice, she thought, but paying no attention to the
words she spoke, so that, now, she too was silent.
She felt drenched in the stillness, too languid to
make any effort, yet not at peace, waiting -Presently he spoke. "This interval just before twi-
light never seems quite real," he said thoughtfully.
"There's an enchantment about it. Are you real,
Julia? Or are you a Hans Christian Andersen mer-
maid who has climbed up on the dock for a moment
and will presently disappear?"
The fancy pleased her. It was the sort of thing
she so often wanted him to say, but that he seldom
did. "It's so still," she said, her voice dreamy but
clear, each word a crystal note falling into the
silence. "As though everything, all Nature, is hold-
ing her breath, waiting-
"I'm holding my breath."
"Why?"
"
"Because you're so lovely, your profile, just as
you are, against that weathered, gray wood. You
look all golden, except your hair, and that, in this
light, is pure black, soft black like charcoal or soot.
Do you know that you are the most beautiful girl
in the world?"
She felt herself melting-with relief, she told
herself, the blessed relief of knowing that he was
not indifferent to her after the strain of uncertainty
and suspense. But, though the emotion she felt was
only relief, she was unable to phrase a casual reply.
The languor that had possessed her was gone. She
sat still and tense, her nerves humming like harp
strings, looking down at the silver sheen moving
across the water.
"I meant what I said this afternoon," he went
on. "I do love you, Julia.""Do you, Scott?" For possibly the first time in
her life she gave no thought to how her voice

the blue iris of his eyes.
"I wanted to tell you at supper. I wanted to tell
you every time I looked at you across the table. But
I couldn't very well with Robert slipping in and out
and swatting flies all around us. Then I thought I'd
wait until the moon came up and do it in the best
romantic tradition. I can't wait. I'm afraid you
might disappear like the little mermaid. I love you
and want you to marry me. Can you, will you,
Julia?"
This was her opportunity to make the crushing
reply she had been plotting ever since she had known
him. This was her chance to put him in his proper

wanted him where he was, close beside her, telling

he could look directly into her eyes.
"You do love me, Julia?" he asked.
"Oh, yes," she sighed. "Yes, Scott."
He smiled, a slow grave smile that did not reach

"You aren't just being-kind?"
"Oh, no!" She felt her eyes widen with fright
that he could think that possible. "I love you. I've

"I believe you." His smile widened, brightened

"Not entirely, I hope. I don't want you to change.
You're lovely as you are. But you must be honest

"It isn't easy to deceive you." She laughed rue-

loved you."
"I hoped you did, but I wasn't sure. So manypeople love you. I knew that I had to impress my-

"Only because I loved you and wanted you to

"It made me furious," she settled against him

"When did I?"
"That afternoon at the Polks'. I asked you if you

me so mad!"
"So you plotted my destruction."
She raised her head. His eyes, watching her, held
the expression of faintly ironical amusement which

"And you have," he said softly. "You've put me
inside your heart where I want to be. Isn't that my

"It is," she sighed blissfully. "I suppose I loved
you then, before that even, in Washinton. Do you
remember the night we drove through Rock Creek
Park?"
"You wore that floaty white dress with the stars
and a fur bunny-jacket. I thought you were the
loveliest thing I had ever seen. I wanted to kiss you,
then, but I was afraid. You seemed so self-assured
and yet so unawakened."
"I was. I've never been awake before."
"Sleeping Beauty?" he teased.
"I'm all different," she marveled. "I hated to be
touched. I used to want men to think of me as-
Will you laugh at me, Scott?"
"Probably. But only because I love you.""-as a dream, a bright, distant star," she finished
bravely.
"And now?"
Her lashes lifted, then fell. "I'm glad I am a
woman."
"You darling!" His arms tightened, held her
closer against him. "I hope you'll find it much more
satisfying to be a woman than the brightest, distant
star."
"It is," she said, sighing. "I'm so blissful. I love
talking to you like this, confessing the silly things
I used to do, knowing that you love me and will

"That's the comfortable part of loving," he said
gently. "You must tell me everything. No deceptions
or evasions. You must be honest with me, Julia."
"Oh, I will. You were right when you told me

I couldn't bear seeing people suffer. But I'm differ-

I won't even know there's anyone else but us in the

She was utterly sincere. With her capacity for

to-morrow

XII
JULIA SAT BEFORE THE MIRROR ABOVE HER DRESS-
ing table, dreamily brushing her hair. She must
look different, she'd thought, as she crept quietly
up to her room after saying good-night to Scott.
Her first eager glance into the mirror had been dis-
appointing. Love had not materially changed her
outward appearance. Her eyes were dark and bright
under the delicate wing-lift of brows, under the
curling fringe on her forehead, but then they usually
were. Her warm coloring glowed with the same
golden peach bloom as on any ordinary occasion.
Only her mouth gave evidence of the miracle. Her
lips, curved in a pleased, dreamy smile, still recalling
the touch of his mouth.
Now, as she sat brushing her hair, her arms
emerging from the lace and chiffon of her best
negligee, the lace yoke of her best hightgown film-
ing her breast, she was glad that the miracle had
not altered her appearance. She had managed to
escape the family to-night, but to-morrow she would
be obliged to face them and she didn't want them
to know just yet, not until she'd had time to break
the news gradually. She wanted only loving ap-
proval at this time of hushed and sacred happiness.
If they should criticize and reproach her -
-But why should they criticize? They would under-
stand when she told them that she hadn't known
what love was when she'd promised to marry Jay.
How could she have foreseen, then, that this miracle
was going to happen to her? Jay would understand.
He had always adored her. He would want her to
be happy. She did love him but in such a different
way, because, she told herself, he needed her, and
then he was awfully good-looking and spent money
lavishly, like a prince. She'd been proud of him,
when he had come for a dance at school or when
she had gone with him for house-party week-ends
at Charlottesville, so romantic-looking with his
slight but well-proportioned figure, his wavy dark
hair and patrician features, so adoring in his man-
ner toward her that other girls sighed with envy.
He'd been difficult, though, not satisfied with the
light love-making she had permitted him, wanting
more than she had been able to give -
She understood, now, why she hadn't been able
to respond to his ardor with satisfying warmth.
She'd thought, then, in that time so far away that
she remembered incidents dimly, that it was because
she'd been more fastidious than other girls. She had
tried to make him understand that her feeling for
him was on a higher plane than affairs other girls
seemed to have involving kisses and embraces. But
she knew now. Now that the miracle of loving Scott
had awakened her, she knew that Jay had not
touched her emotionally, that her feeling for himhad been, was now, a mingling of affection, pride,
compassion for him because he loved her so wildly.
Yes, he had been difficult. There were times when
his moods exhausted her. At a word or a glance
from her, his jealousy would be aroused and he'd
fling himself off into some madness. It certainly
wasn't her fault if he'd gone out in his boat alone
that night, last fall, when he had been hurt. She'd
merely been trying to be pleasant to his guests when
she drove into Cambridge with the boy from An-
napolis. Bowers? Powers? What was his name? It
was unkind of his mother, of Caroline, to inti-
mate -
His going away had been a distinct relief. She
admitted that, now, to be completely honest, to re-
adjust her life to a new scale of values, Scott's
values, which from this moment on would be hers.
Not that she hadn't been terribly shocked by
the accident or simply frantic with worry during his
illness. She had wept the night before he left when
she'd knelt by his couch and promised to marry him.
She'd been sure that she loved him, then. A feeling
of amused tenderness for the Julia she had been last
fall curved her lips into a poignant smile, an un-
awakened Julia who had mistaken compassion for
love. When Jay understood, when he knew -
She pushed the thought away from her, conscious
of a sense of uneasiness, almost of panic. Her face
cleared. Soothed by the rhythmic strokes of the brush
against her hair, her mind relaxed, filled dreamily
with visions which formed and dissolved like soapbubbles blown into still air. She saw herself floating
down the aisle at St. John's. There must be only
white flowers. Lilies? The wedding could not be too
expensive. Chrysanthemums this autumn or mar-
guerites? No, lilies. Aunt Gena would help. Would
reading the service prove too great a strain for

JULIA STARTED UP FROM THE SOFA IN THE LIVING-
room when she heard a car stop in front of the
house. She'd been undecided where to meet Jay,
whether in the house or on the porch. If she talked
to him on the porch, the darkness would partially
conceal his face and hers so that she wouldn't have to
witness the suffering she was obliged to inflict piti-
fully revealed. She had thought it would be kinder
to Jay, since, for her sake, he would try, of course,
to be brave. But to meet him on the porch where
moonlight lay in a silver sheen across the hammock,
the steps, might tend to inject a romantic element
into the reunion and that she was anxious to avoid.
She had, in a measure, regained her composure
after the telephone call, while she dressed, while she
waited for Jay. Forcing herself to remain seated on
the sofa, she had phrased over and over in her mind
the sadly loving sentences with which she would dis-
miss him. "Let me go out of your life gently, dear,
like a note of music fading into stillness when the
bow is lifted from the strings." That might appeal
to him. He had a sensitive appreciation for imagery
so that they had talked to each other in similes. She
had, last summer, especially, learned whole pages of
poetry to recite to Jay at romantic moments. She,herself, loved cadenced phrases and beautiful words.
Besides, when Jay's mind was mesmerized by poetry
he wasn't always wanting to make love to her -
There was really no use being nervous, she told
herself, relaxing firmly against the cushions. Other
girls had fancied they were in love and, when real
love came, had discovered their mistake. It was ex-
tremely fortunate that she had met Scott before she
married Jay, because, whenever she met him, she
would surely have fallen in love with him and, if it
had been afterwards, that would have caused a great
deal of trouble for everyone. She had only to be
direct and honest. "I was a child, then, Jay, a romantic
young girl in love with love. Will you let me go, my
darling, knowing that you are giving me life's great-
est happiness?" But should she call him "my dar-
ling"? Under the circumstances, would that be kind?
Perhaps Jay, too, had changed. Perhaps there
had been a girl in New Mexico with whom, suscep-
tible through weakness, he had fallen in love. She
had caught at that possibility with reviving hope,
though, at the same time, the idea had annoyed her.
A false hope, though. His voice, coming to her across
the telephone wire, had shattered her with its eager-
ness, its unrestrained joy. But still there was no use
to be nervous. She had only to tell him, with dignity
and kindness, that she had made a mistake. Natu-
rally she was distressed. It hurt her to hurt anyone.
She would tell him that -
But the composure she had gained was shaken by

up the steps. She sat motionless, one hand pressed
against her breast, her ears straining through the
stillness. The screen door opened and closed. She
heard Jay's voice call her name, softly so as not to
disturb the rest of the house, but with an urgency
she could not ignore.
He stood in the hall just inside the front door.
"Julia! Julia!" he cried softly as she came out of
the living-room.
"Jay! Jay, darling!" Her voice sounded terribly
fluttered but that was all right. He would interpret
it as a natural emotion. And he, for the moment
at least, did not rush forward to embrace her. Her
heart stopped knocking against her ribs and her
knees gave her a more reliable assurance of support.
He stood looking at her as she came toward him,
floating through the honey-colored dusk that filled
the hall.
"Julia," he said again as though repeating her
name, in this instant of seeing her again, satisfied
him completely.
"I'm so sorry I wasn't here this afternoon. You

"You're here now." His mouth quivered. His dark

had dreaded had arrived.Should she tell him now? she wondered wildly.
But she couldn't while he held her so tightly, while

-this thing she must do was a thousand times more

wasn't ready. If she'd been warned, if she had had
more time
"The sun in New Mexico is pretty hot," Jay wassaying when she gave him her attention again. "We

He was so happy, so sure of her. Why couldn't

After all, eight months was a long time

SCOTT RAISED HIS HEAD AS JULIA CALLED HIS NAME.
She hadn't been able to, just at first, when she had
walked down the path from the cabin and seen
him lying on the dock, his face hidden on his folded
arms, his back exposed to the sunlight. She'd seen
him with just swimming trunks on before but it was
different, now, since last night. Seeing his strong
brown body there in the sunlight, the muscles motion-
less under the skin, the breadth of his shoulders
tapering down to his narrow waist, had made her
tremble and almost stopped her breath. She'd had
to wait a moment before she could call his name.
"Hello!" He sprang to his feet. "What a grand
surprise!"
"Glad to see me?" She knew that he was. His
eyes looked bright and blue in his brown face,
browner, since he had come to the cabin than when
she had met him, and his smile made her heart beat
fast.
"Knocked for a loop. I can't kiss you, though.
I've been swimming. I'm all wet."
"I don't mind." She went into his arms, feeling
the exciting pressure of his lips.
"I'm sorry." He held her away from him. "I'vespoiled your dress and it looked so fresh and crisp
before."
"As though I mind!"
"You told me you hated to be mussed," he said
teasingly, holding her off, loving her with his eyes.
"That was-" She laughed, a light, happy laugh.
"Oh, ages ago when I was a little goose and terribly
vain."
"Then if you really don't mind -"
When he released her again, he stood looking at
her. "You're the solution of a dilemma," he said.
"What a lot of big words you know!"
"Darling! You are, though. I've been lying here
wondering how I was going to wait until evening to
see you. I swam for what I thought must be half an
ordinary lifetime and discovered I'd spent exactly
twenty minutes in the water. Since then I've been
trying to decide whether you'd like me better if I
were impetuous and rushed in to have breakfast
with you or was strong-willed and waited until
evening."
"I had breakfast ages ago." How fortunate that
she had come, she thought, while her lifted face, her
hand on his arm, expressed only joy at being here
with him again. It had occurred to her the very last
thing, when already a gray light crept into her room
and birds were stirring in the trees outside her win-
dow, that he might come into town this morning and
that Jay might be there. She was convinced, now, of
the wisdom of the decision she had made then. Her
eyes felt sandy from lack of sleep and she knew shelooked wan in spite of the petal touches of rouge.
But she'd been right to come here early so that she
might tell him about Jay, before, as might easily
have happened, he and Jay met, neither having ever
heard of the other before. She had, as Caroline often
suggested, the luck of a three-legged cat.
"Scott," she said hesitantly, "I've something to
tell you. It's likely to take quite a while."
"Then we'd better get out of the sun." His hand
on her arm, he turned back toward the shore-line
from the end of the dock. "Is it warm in town?"
"Frightful," she said, grateful for a moment's re-
prieve.
"I'm afraid it's going to be a scorcher out here.
The water is tepid and there's no breeze. Old Man
Weather is warming up for the Fourth."
The Fourth of July had some meaning for her
other than a national holiday but, at the moment,
she couldn't recall what it was. There was no reason
to feel nervous, she told herself. Scott wouldn't be
hurt. He had nothing to lose. But would he under-
stand that she hadn't meant to be evasive, that Jay
wouldn't be important any longer after she had told
him? Why hadn't she told Scott at first? Why did
things like this happen to her? She only wanted
everyone to be happy and comfortable so that she
might be happy and comfortable, too.
...
"You have something to tell me?" Scott asked
when they were seated on a bench in the shade at the
edge of the water.
"It's silly of me to feel nervous, isn't it?" Sheglanced up at him from under the brim of her rough
straw hat. His expression was thoughtful but when
his eyes met hers he smiled.
"Very silly," he said. "You promised to be honest
with me. You can tell me anything, Julia, except that
you don't love me."
"Oh, it isn't that!" She knew that her swift reply,
her voice, her face lifted to his had reassured him.
"Nothing else is important, is it? Don't be nerv-
ous, darling. Will it help if I hold your hand?"
It did help. His hand holding hers in a firm warm

fectly frank, she told herself, to admit everything.
She loved him so that her heart felt too big for her
breast, but that, she knew, was not the sole reason

"Scott," she said thinking it expedient to get the
worst of it over before she lost her courage, "there's
something I should have told you long ago when I

"You know now," he said quietly. "You knew last
night."
"Yes, I know," she said penitently, "I should have
told you last night, but I didn't want what was solovely to be spoiled by dreary explanations about
something that isn't really important at all
"
"If it isn't important, why are you disturbed?"
His expression was thoughtful again. She looked
away from his penetrating glance, feeling that she
hadn't begun very well.
"It is important, I suppose, now, at least. I meant
it isn't permanently important. It doesn't change

"If you want me to understand," he said and his
voice sounded almost stern, "will you try to be a little
more coherent, my dear?"
Why did he speak to her in that tone when she
was trying to tell him, when, if he loved her, he
should understand how terribly difficult it was for
her to tell him at all? Resentment gave her strength-
ened courage. She told him what she had to tell him,
speaking rapidly and breathlessly, not sparing her-
self, grateful for the wide brim of her hat, for the
steady clasp of his hand.
"And Jay came home, unexpectedly, last night,"
she concluded, daring, then, to look up at him. She
didn't know what she had expected, but seeing his
expression, as he returned her glance, tender, sympa-
thetic, a little amused, gave her a sense of relief so
strong, so blessedly welcome, that she felt tears
spring to her eyes.
"That must have been dreadful for you," he said
gently.
"It was." She felt like a child who has confessedand been forgiven as she basked in the warmth of
his sympathy. "You simply can't imagine, Scott."
"I think I can. I thought you looked a little wan
to-day. I don't wonder."
She looked wan because she'd spent the night
composing a letter to Jay. That solution had oc-
curred to her after he'd left, when she was preparing
for bed. She'd thought that would be kinder to Jay
because, in that case, he wouldn't be obliged to con-

a boy to take it to him as soon as she drove back

had been told. Why distress both of them by dis-
cussing the matter further?
But Scott, apparently, did not share her view of
the situation.
"And while I was here in the moonlight thinking
that you were tucked into bed and dreaming of me,"

that."
"It was simply heart-rending, Scott." Her tearsfell faster. She could have checked them, but the re-
lief of having told him, the blissful certainty of his

had gotten to sleep and she was tired

He made it seem so different than she had thought

If Scott should turn from her, now, when she loved

"Oh, Julia. No, darling!" His voice softened.
"You're none of them, fundamentally, except a cow-

never been forced to do anything difficult becausenothing has been of sufficient importance to make
you face an issue and fight it through."
"No," she said thoughtfully, honestly trying to

"You're right, though, Scott." She smiled ruefully.

"Despise you, darling? I love you dearly, in every

important, I think, to us, to our future happiness,

Julia?"
"Oh, no!" she protested with soft vehemence.

and begged me to marry him when he returned-
Well, I wasn't as fond of anyone as I was of him. I
didn't know you existed. I've never loved anyone as

ing that I'd see you like that, be with you-" She
paused in confusion, feeling her cheeks grow warm,
looked down, then, bending swiftly, pressed her lips
against his hand that covered hers as it lay in her
lap.
"Darling! Sweet Julia! My beautiful!"
She was not conscious of having convinced him.
The gesture had been spontaneous. She had meant
it with all her heart. She did not know that it moved
him more deeply than the protestations she had
made, than all the words she had spoken. She knew
only that when she raised her head, his arms, his lips,
were waiting.

IT WAS EASIER THAN SHE HAD, AT HER MOST CON-
fident moment, dreamed that it could be. Starting
had been difficult, of course, but now that she had
started, he was taking it very well. He hadn't been
able to believe her, at first, and his face had gone
white beneath the tan he'd gotten in New Mexico.
But, now, though he did not look at her, he listened
quietly while she talked.
She certainly wouldn't have chosen the drug-store
as an appropriate setting for this, their last moment
of intimacy. It had simply happened this way. When
she'd driven back into town from the camp in the
family's sedan, she'd seen his car parked in front of
the Observer office across the street. He'd been
lounging against it, talking to Caroline. She'd hoped
she might drive past without them seeing her, but
both of them had, so she'd been obliged to stop.
They'd spoken of the heat and Jay had invited them
to go over to the drug-store for a soda. He had in-
cluded Caroline in the invitation but Caroline had
pretended to be terribly busy reading proof in the
office.
"Run along, you lilies of the field," she'd said, "I
have a woman's work to do." And the look Caroline
had given her was scornful and yet, somehow, pity-ing, too, as though Caroline knew that she hadn't
wanted to be with Jay alone, knew that she'd been
to the camp and was sorry for her even while despis-
ing the tactics she employed. She'd had the impression
that Caroline hadn't slept last night, either. She
looked limp and washed out and she'd acted cross
as she always did when her nerves were on edge.
She, Julia, had crossed the street to the drug-store
with Jay, feeling limp herself and nervous and ex-
hausted.
But it was working out very well. She had told
Jay and he wasn't making a scene. Having it happen
here in the drug-store was probably just another
example of her phenomenal luck, she thought, ob-
serving that Jay's hand did not appear to tremble
as he lifted the glass to his lips. He couldn't very
well make a scene with Tommy Troy watching them
from behind the soda fountain and old Dr. Troy
selling face powder to Trudy Lynch at the counter,
though she and Jay were entirely alone in the space
at the rear of the store.
She was facing the issue, as Scott had told her
that she must, though she hadn't mentioned the letter
she'd written to Jay. She'd known when Scott talked
to her, when he'd held her in his arms, that she
couldn't send it, that she must be brave enough to
tell him face to face without resorting to cowardly
devices. Now that she had told him, now when she
sat in silence because he was silent and it seemed that
nothing remained to be said, she was terribly glad
that she had. She had proved her love for Scott bybeing honest and courageous. He would never doubt
her again.
She had told him directly, too. She had not used
any of the poetic phrases that had occurred to her
last night. As she had reviewed them in her mind
they had sounded falsely sentimental. She simply
couldn't imagine saying things like that to Scott. Be-
sides, this wasn't the proper setting for poetry, be-
cause whenever she looked through the glass top of
the table she saw the same Golliwog bottle of per-
fume, arranged with other feminine trifles on puck-
ered green silk, and it had put her off.
She sat, for an interval, glowing with self-approval
and an exalted sense of having done something diffi-
cult for Scott. But the glow faded as Jay remained
silent. How was it possible to terminate the interview
gracefully? She couldn't simply get up and leave,
though she wanted to desperately, though she found
it difficult to restrain her feet from beating an impa-
tient tattoo on the floor. The distress she had felt
for Jay was lost in irritation. Why did he keep her
here? Didn't he know how shattering this had been
to her? Why didn't he let her go?
He spoke at the instant when she felt if he didn't
she would scream.
"You mean this, don't you, Julia?" he asked
quietly.
She looked across the table at him. His face was

The possibility that Jay might come barging in at

She thought of the camp and longing was inten-
sified. She could sleep there, knowing Scott was near.
His strength and his sanity would quiet her, sooth

issue with Jay. And when she had rested she would
be able to feel the flooding relief she had expected
to feel. She was overwrought, now, too enervated
by the heat, by the emotional crises of the morning,
to feel anything except utter exhaustion.
But the camp was the first place they, Caroline,
would think to look for her if
She didn't mean that. Her nerves were playing
tricks, distorting her imagination. It was simply that
she didn't want to see anyone, not even Scott, until
she had rested. He was coming to the house to-night.
She wanted to be fresh, serene, looking her loveliest
for him.She would drive out to see Cousin Mollie on her
farm. She could sleep there all afternoon. Mollie
would be delighted to have her; always busy with
her turkeys or truck garden, she was restfully in-

Thinking of the clean, bare bedroom in which she
slept at the farm steadied her nerves. She wasn't

The relief of having told Jay and settled that began

brave

THE WORST OF THE STORM WAS OVER. COUSIN MOL-
lie had made her wait so that she was certain din-
ner would be over, at home, and she'd have to
hurry to have a bath and dress before Scott came.
But she felt wonderfully rested after the long sleep
she'd had and the rain, still falling, was deliciously
refreshing. How green the lawns looked, she
thought, driving through town, a fresh emerald-
green in the gray light, through silver streamers of
rain. The soaking they'd had brought out the fra-
grance of honey-suckle and roses, the cool woods
smell of trees and boxwood hedges.
She drove into the garage, taking the short cut
at the rear of the house. Caroline's dogs barked for
attention as she closed the door of the garage. They
acted, she thought fleetingly, as though they hadn't
been fed, but that was impossible, of course, because
Caroline was meticulous in her care of them. She
spoke to them, hoping to quiet the racket they made,
then ran under the arbor to the kitchen porch, enjoy-
ing the cool, drenched feeling of the rain on her face.
Louisa sat on a stool before the open door, doing
nothing, which was odd at this hour of the day.
"Is dinner over?" Julia asked, pausing a moment
to shake herself partially dry in the kitchen."You'll find plenty to eat in the dinin'-room,"
Louisa replied morosely. Julia turned to look at her,
her attention arrested by a note of melancholy ex-
citement in Louisa's voice. In the gray light that
filled the kitchen Louisa's round brown face was
unnaturally solemn, but subtly excited, too, and she
returned Julia's glance in a furtive but interested
way that held Julia's arrested attention.
"Haven't the family eaten?" she asked.
"They ain't even sat down," Louisa answered
complainingly. "Ah cook all afternoon in this heat
to git a nice dinner an' what Ah cook settin' on de
table stone cold."
"But why?" Julia was puzzled and, quite unrea-
sonably, a little disturbed, as well.
"Ah ain't been informed." Louisa's expression
reflected injured dignity. "Miss Frances jest tell me
to stop 'nouncin' dinner and go on 'bout mah busi-
ness. Ah ain't got no business 'til Ah can git at dem
dishes. Nobody thinks about me. Ah kin set here 'til
midnight fo' all they care, jest a twiddlin' mah
thumbs."
"Oh hush, Louisa !" Julia said a trifle crisply. "If
Mother asked you to keep dinner waiting, you know
very well she has a good reason."
"Yessum. Ah reckon she has." Louisa, curiously,
seemed about to break into muttered chuckles. "Yes-
sum, she got a reason, all right." She fixed Julia with
a slyly penetrating glance. "But effen Ah was you,
Ah wouldn't talk so brash, that Ah wouldn't."
What did Louisa mean? Julia wondered, hurryingthrough the colonnade, through the dining-room

dreadful was happening to them, to Mother, to

"Your Dad's never been any good, Julia." Dad
sank heavily into a chair, sat looking up at her, as
though, curiously, she sat in judgment upon him.
"No, that's true," he went on, silencing her cry of
protest. "I'm a fine fellow in some ways, good-na-
tured, pretty good-looking, able to string words to-
gether so people enjoying reading them. I'm fairly
good at fishing and hitting a duck on the wing and
roaring out a good bass on quartettes. I haven't been
a bad father, have I, up till now? Of course I've
never done anything for you, just been around and
made jokes and tried not to disgrace you before com-
pany. But I haven't been what you'd call a wicked
Dad, have I?"
"Oh, Dad, no!" Julia felt tears run down over
her cheeks. Mother was weeping, too, soundlessly,
making no effort to wipe away the tears as they fell.
"But I'm no good," Dad went on, speaking
quietly, reflectively, as though he were talking to
himself. "I've never been worth a tinker's dam.
Frannie ought to have left me years ago instead of
trying to make a man of me. She's done a pretty goodjob, at that, except that she's only made something
that looks all right to people but is rotten inside.
You'd have thought, wouldn't you, that when I got
that job on the Observer, I'd have had the sense to
appreciate it? But you can't pump integrity into a
man if he wasn't born with it. Now that Scott of
yours, he's sound through and through, not specked,
like I am, at the core and this business isn't going to
matter to him if he loves you. Don't you be getting
any thoughts of making sacrifices into your pretty
head."
"But what-what business, Dad?"
"Julia," he said, after a moment. "I've been using
money that didn't belong to me, the paper's money,
and Isabel Summerfield has found it out."
"But Dad- What for?" She knew that wasn't
important. The question was a cry of protest. It
didn't matter what she'd said.
"Oh, investments that sounded good but didn't
pan out," he replied. "And I've lost a good bit down
at Curley Lynch's shack on Friday nights. You see,
dolly," he went on, "I had an idea that I'd like to
own the paper. Dusty and I, maybe, and, like a good
many other fools, I had an idea I could get together
some cash that way."
"If it's only money, Dad," Julia said, feeling
relieved in spite of being terribly shocked, "we can
fix that, can't we? We can repay the amount you
have-used. You can have my next check and Caro-
line's. Or couldn't we borrow on it, somehow?"Dad's face brightened. A little color came into his
cheeks.
"It's like you to think of that, dolly," he said
gently, "but it won't do. Isabel Summerfield's going
to make a scandal. She won't take the money, even
if we could scrape it together. She wants to put me,
to put all of us through hell."
"Because-because of me? Because Jay shot him-
self? That horrible woman! Oh, Dad!"
"You can hardly blame her," Mother said, speak-
ing for the first time. "She idolizes Jay."
How, how withered Mother looked, Julia thought,
like a flower touched by an early frost, not killed
entirely, but never able again to hold its head erect
in the same proud way. This was hardest of all for
Mother. She'd always been so proud, so brave. And
she idolized Dad. Oh, how could he have been such
a fool!
"Mrs. Summerfield will let us repay her," she
said, forcing confidence into her voice. "She'll do
anything for Jay." She must comfort them, give them
hope. She couldn't endure seeing them suffer like this.
It was making her ill. "I'll talk to Jay, make him
understand
"
Caroline whirled around to face her.
"Jay doesn't want to see you," she said. “Mrs.
Summerfield left that message for you. Jay doesn't
ever want to see you again."
"But that's ridiculous!" Though Dad had told
her and she knew, herself, that she wasn't to blame,
Julia shrank from her sister's accusing glance.Caroline looked as though she hated her, she
thought, miserably. There was no pity, now, in her
voice, her expression, her manner. Caroline had
abandoned her and nothing would ever be the same
again. Neither Mother's still face wet with tears
that continued to fall, nor Dad's pitiful humiliation,
wounded her as Caroline's contempt hurt. She loved
Dad and Mother, was sorry for them, but Caro-
line -
Caroline blamed her for this dreadful thing that
had happened. It wasn't her fault. Sustaining anger
burned through her, giving her momentary courage.
She faced her sister, her cheeks burning, her head
flung back in justified self-defense.
"You're blaming me for all this," she cried
stormily. "You've held yourself up as the perfect
pattern of candor and integrity. You've despised
the little white lies I've told because I can't endure
seeing people suffer. Well, I told the truth to-day.
I told Jay I didn't love him, that I was going to
marry Scott. That was the truth, Caroline." Her
voice rose to an hysterical pitch. "For probably the
first time in my life I told the simple unadulterated
truth and see what happened!"
She was only half conscious of Mother's cry, of
the sound Dad made, of Caroline calling her name.
Turning, her head flung back, blind with tears, she
went running out of the room.XVII
"AND THAT'S THE WHOLE STORY, SCOTT," JULIA
finished with a tremulous sigh, laid down her fork,
and looked at him with misty eyes.
"You poor darling!" Scott, seated beside her, at
the end of the dining-room table, hitched his chair
closer to her, placed his hand over her hand.
She felt faint with relief. She had so dreaded tell-
ing him what Dad had done, the scandal they faced,
the possible consequences of Jay's violence. She felt
better than she'd felt when he came. She'd been
certain that she wouldn't be able to eat when he'd
had Louisa fix a tray for her. But she had eaten
every bit and, now, warmed by his sympathy, her
spirits lifted. She was able to think, to plan, to con-
sider possibilities. When he'd first come, she could
do nothing but cling to him, begging him over and
over to tell her that it wasn't her fault.
The house was very still. Dr. Braden had come
and gone. Caroline was upstairs with Mother. Dad
had gone out with Dusty Price. Dusty knew, had
known, that Dad was using money which belonged
to the paper. He'd known, too, that Jay had shot
himself. Everybody in town would know by to-
morrow. News traveled fast in Queensville where
nearly all the servants were related and lived, hud-dled together, in the two darky settlements on either
side of town. Everybody would know that Jay had
shot himself because she'd jilted him. Would they
blame her?
"Are you cold, Julia?"
She lifted inquiring eyes.
"You're trembling," Scott said.
"I keep thinking that it is my fault." But she
hadn't been thinking of that. She'd remembered the
way Dr. Braden had looked at her when she'd met
him at the door, the gravely quizzical glance he'd
given her from under his shaggy brows. He had
come directly from the Summerfields'. Had Miss
Isabel told him about Dad? Would everybody in
town turn against her and despise her?
She'd been petted and adored and, though she
realized it only vaguely as yet, popularity was the
breath of life to her. There were people who would
delight in her humiliation, mothers whose daughters
didn't receive the attention she did, girls who were
envious of her. It would be an undeserved cruelty.
She'd been kind to everyone, especially kind to
girls who were plain and unattractive to men, though
often they were catty and misunderstood her kind-
ness. If the whole town blamed her, despised her,
how could she bear it? "If I'd lied to Jay," she cried,
unaware of concealing any portion of her thoughts
from Scott. "But I didn't. I told the truth."
"I know you did, darling. It isn't your fault,
except that our lives are interwoven so that what
touches you, touches others. It's like well, turningon a chain of lights from a central switch. So many
people love you that, naturally, your range of influ-
ence is wider than that of less magnetic persons.
That's the price you pay for being kind and beauti-
ful, my sweet."
She thought it over, her brows crossed in a frown.
"But you know when you turn the switch that the
lights are connected," she said, after a moment.
"How do you know which act, which thing you do
will affect others?"
"That wasn't an apt analogy, perhaps," Scott re-
plied. "You can't know, of course. That's why hon-
esty is the only reliable security. If you'd always,
all your life, been truthful as you were to-day"
"You do blame me then!" She drew a little away
from him, reproach in her voice, pain and bewilder-
ment clouding her eyes. "You think, as Caroline
does, that it was, that it is my fault."
"Oh, Julia, no!" His voice, his hand reaching for
her hand again, were very reassuring. "It isn't your
fault that a melodramatic boy shoots himself because
you tell him the truth. And you're certainly not re-
sponsible for anything that your father has done.
Be sensible, darling. You don't feel that you are to
blame, do you?"
"No! Oh no! But when Caroline looks at me as
she does, when people, Dusty, Louisa, Dr.
Braden"
"Caroline has had a shock," he continued sooth-

strong emotion that we don't actually mean. Caro-line loves you devotedly. She will understand when
she's had time to think of this calmly and reason-

is it?"
"But if my friends misunderstand-" She broke
off, aware of his altered expression. "I don't blame
you for thinking what you do think, Scott. It is
selfish of me to think of myself when everyone is so
unhappy and thinking I'm to blame."
"I want you to think of yourself, but I want you
to think straight. There's nothing you can do to
alter the situation. I shall, of course, offer your
father any assistance at my command. I won't know
what can be done until I've heard all the facts. This

there may be some way of persuading her that such
a course will reflect creditably neither upon herself

fury like a woman whose beloved only son is jilted
by the prettiest girl in town."
"Oh, that is good of you, Scott." She held his
hand against her cheek. "I was afraid you'd stop
loving me when you knew. I can't think of Dad as a
scoundrel, though. I haven't any moral indignation,
I suppose. All this hasn't changed the way I feel

shred of character, have I?"
"Of course you have. It's natural that you should
have thought, first, of wanting to help him ratherthan of the moral aspects of the situation. I'm in a
position to know that these things happen every

he did was wrong and nothing can soften that fact.
But if he were permitted to make restitution pri-
vately, it would be easier for the rest of you. Your
mother
יי?
"She's simply crushed!" Julia's voice trembled.

His lips, pressed against hers, checked the torrent

His expression changed. The tenderness faded

"Don't you trust me, Scott?" she asked reproach-

"That isn't very kind of you."

Scott was mature and wise and reasonable. Hewouldn't ask her to do anything dishonorable, any-

But it would look like running away. Comments

But she didn't care. Let them think what they

She'd known well enough when people flattered

MOTHER'S EYES WERE CLOSED WHEN CAROLINE
opened the door, the lids seemingly dragged down
by the weight of the thick lashes that lay against her
cheeks. Moving quietly, she placed the tray she had
brought on the table beside the bed, lowered the
shade at the east window and went to the door. But
as she stood with her hand on the knob, she heard a
sound of movement.
"Caroline?" Mother said.
"Yes, darling." Caroline came back into the
room. How ill Mother looked, as though the cour-
age which had burned so fiercely, giving her anima-
tion and vitality had dimmed to a feeble spark. The
rose-colored bed-jacket she wore made her skin look
paper white in comparison and her dark eyes were
enormous in the pinched triangle of her face. "I've
brought you something to eat," she said, trying to
make her voice sound cheerful.
"I'm not hungry," Mother said in dull expres-
sionless tones.
"But Dr. Braden said you must have nourish-
ment. It's just egg-nog with brandy. If you don't
feel like lifting the glass, I'll feed you with a spoon.
Let's plump up the pillows," Caroline said. "There.
That's better, isn't it?" She hadn't realized thatMother was so slight. As she lifted her higher
against the pillows Mother's weight seemed nothing
to her young strength. "And since you aren't sleep-
ing, shall I raise the shades? It's a beautiful day
after the storm."
"Is it?" Mother asked listlessly, but since she
made no objection, Caroline raised the shades, let-
ting sunlight into the room. How could the sun shine
so brightly? she thought. How could the air smell so
fresh and fragrant when they were all so distressed.
All of them except Julia, of course. She was going
with Scott to Washington and leave her, Caroline,
to untangle the snarls. But this time the snarls
wouldn't untangle. How could Dad have done what
he had? How was it possible for Julia to be so in-
sensitive to the misery she had caused? Caroline
leaned, for a moment, against the window frame,
making an effort to quiet the rebellion seething in
her breast, to recover her composure.
"Caroline," Mother said. "Where where is
Daddy?"
Dad would be Mother's first thought. How often,
as at this moment, a stinging protest against Moth-
er's insane devotion to him sprang to her lips and
withered there unspoken! Caroline drew a steady-
ing breath and turned from the window.
"Do you want him?" she asked.
"No- No- I just wondered. He's all right, isn't
he? He's had breakfast? Did he find the fresh linen
suit that came with the laundry yesterday? I hung it
in the guest-room closet thinking that ""He found it, Mother. Yes, he's had breakfast
and gone to the office. This is Friday, you know.
The paper goes to press at noon."
"Friday?" A tremor passed over Mother's face,
then it was still and expressionless again. "Egg-
nogs! I don't like them." Faint animation strength-
ened her voice. "Did Dr. Braden say I must have
them?"
"He certainly did." Mother was keeping up the
pretense, Caroline thought in helpless exasperation.
She was avoiding any discussion of what Dad did
on Friday nights. That was stupid now, when she
must be aware of the futility of trying to shield
Dad; when she should be thinking only of herself.
An obsession as strong as Mother's attitude toward
Dad might lead to mental derangement, to complete
insanity. Dr. Braden had implied that possibility
when he'd talked to her and to Dad about Mother
last night, telling them that he'd been expecting
Mother to break for years, that her will and her
spirit had driven her body beyond the limits of physi-
cal endurance. Caroline felt frantic with rebellion
and helplessness. But she must be gentle with
Mother. She sat on the side of the bed, feeding her
with spoonfuls of egg-nog, forcing a smile when her
glance met Mother's glance, longing for wisdom
denied her youth, despising Dad and Julia but lov-
ing them, too, grieved, shocked, bewildered.
"Caroline-" Mother said between sips. "How
soon before people will know? How soon before
Isabel Summerfield-?" She was unable to go on.Caroline saw her throat constrict, saw her eyes sud-
denly alive with pain and fright.
"Never, probably," Caroline said with false hope-
fulness. "That was only a threat. Think how ridicu-
lous it would make her look. Everybody would
know she was doing it because Julia jilted Jay. She
was frantic when she came here. I-I called the
house this morning."
"Did you speak to her, to Isabel or to Jay?" The
eagerness in Mother's voice, the desire for reas-
surance she could not definitely give, filled Caroline's
heart with pity and affection, softening exasperation,
making her feel that nothing was as important as
giving Mother the encouragement for which her
dark eyes pled.
"I didn't speak to either of them," she said, not
telling Mother how almost rude Sherry, the house-
man's voice had sounded over the wire, Sherry who
had known her since childhood, who had obviously
been instructed to discourage any advances from the
Perrys. "I talked to Sherry," she went on. "He told
me that it's a flesh wound and not serious. They've
given out the usual story, that Jay was cleaning a
gun and it went off accidentally. Sherry was very
pompous. You know how darkies love to act impor-
tant. I don't think Miss Isabel would have said that
if she had any intention of carrying out her threat."
But she had invented the story to give Mother a
ray of hope. Sherry had told her nothing except that
Jay was doing as well as could be expected and he
had put a good deal of hostility into that.But Jay was their friend! Even if Julia had
treated him badly, he wouldn't let his mother do

come here yesterday and made that dreadful scene.
It was melodramatic of him to have shot himself,
but she realized, as none of the others apparently

adored Julia, how terribly hurt he had been. It
wasn't fair to judge him by this fact, to criticize

past. If he was spoiled, that was his mother's fault.
He was young and high-spirited, sensitive, really
fine. She had almost hated Dusty last night when
he'd implied that Julia was lucky to be rid of him.
Jay! How could they misjudge him so cruelly.
"Just three more spoonfuls," she said, relieved to

probably feels quite different to-day and is awfully

thinking that having already been untruthful she
might as well embroider a little, "if she'll come again
or write a note of apology. Don't worry, darling.
It simply isn't possible that she'd drag all of us
through a lawsuit."
"I don't really care," Mother said, surprising
Caroline so that she nearly dropped the glass. "I'm
too tired to care any longer what people think. Will
probably should be prosecuted," she went on steadily.
"He's been dishonest as well as weak. It's my fault,
Caroline. I am entirely to blame.""Oh, Mother, no!" Caroline set the glass on the
table. "You've been wonderful. If he's any good at
all, and he is, it's because you've believed in him
and supported him with your strength."
"But I haven't," she said. "I've only made people
think that I do. I shouldn't have shielded him all
these years. If I hadn't, if I'd made him face issues
instead of shielding him he might have gained a per-
sonal strength so that this couldn't have happened.
No, mistakenly, for selfish reasons, I've done him a
great injustice."
"Mother, why did you? We've known, of course,
that Dad had certain weaknesses. But you wouldn't
admit them. It's been so terribly lonely for you. If
you had let us help you, Julia and I- Don't talk
about it if you'd rather not
"
"I want to!" Mother's voice strengthened and
color deepened in her cheeks. "What I have done
has been an injustice, too, to you and to Julia. I had
no right to let this happen."
"How could you have prevented it?"
"As Daddy said yesterday, I should have left him
when you were children. It wasn't fair to you, for
the sake of my own selfish pride, because I wouldn't
admit that what my family, my friends, said of him
was true, because I loved him, to expose you and
Julia to the humiliations you have suffered, and,
now, to this."
"But we haven't suffered," Caroline protested,
knowing how difficult it must be for Mother to speak
in this way of Dad, wanting to give her comfortand reassurance. "We've had a happy life. I don't
think that either of us has felt especially deprived.
And we're fond of Dad. I've been proud of him,
working on the paper with him, seeing how people
have gradually changed their opinion of him, come
to respect his opinions and rely upon his judgment.
He's done an awfully good job."
"Have you been proud of him?" Mother asked
eagerly, wistfully. "Have you had a happy life? If
I could believe that, I wouldn't feel so terribly to
blame. I've been thinking of it, lying here this morn-
ing since I woke, realizing how selfishly proud I've
been, wondering if, when I spoke of it, you would

"Oh, no, darling. We-I've minded only for you.
I have been exasperated. There've been times when

I've felt, sometimes, forgive me if I hurt you, that
Dad resented your attitude, too, that he-" She
