But Mother was smiling, a wry smile of self-
reproach.
"I know," she said. "He tried to live up to my
ideals of him. They were sincere, at first. I was
barely eighteen when I married him. You know that
my family did everything in their power to prevent
the marriage. I thought they were being unjust to
him. I wouldn't believe the things they told me.
When I knew they were true, that Will was a charm-ing wastrel, I was still young enough to believe that
I could change him, that if I was patient, if I loved
him enough, I could make of him what I wanted him
to be because my strength and my will were greater
than his, because, in his way, he loved me devotedly.
I succeeded only in aggravating him, driving him to
companions who were less exacting, into situations
which might have been avoided if I had worn my
halo at a more rakish angle. Don't marry a man
with the idea of reforming him, Caroline. Be sure
that you can have a real tolerance for his weak-
nesses, that you can overlook them if there are suffi-
cient compensations. Devoting a life-time to trying
to make a man something temperament or heredity
has not equipped him to be isn't very rewarding."
Was there a personal implication in Mother's
words? Caroline wondered. Did Mother know that
she loved Jay? But Jay wasn't like Dad. Mother
couldn't know, she told herself reassuringly. She
was thinking only of her own relationship to Dad.
"You're tiring yourself," she said, aware of the
deepening flush in Mother's thin cheeks, the effort in
her voice. "Don't talk now. Try to rest."
"I want to talk!" Mother's head lifted from the
pillows, emphasizing the urgency of her desire. "I've
wanted to talk to you, especially, because I feel that
you are a little like me, Caroline. I've never worried
about Julia. She's less intense than you are, gives less
of herself to the people she loves, though I believe
that she loves Scott sincerely, in her way. But you-
I feel shy, talking to you intimately." Motherdropped back against the pillows again. "That's not
strange, is it? I haven't since you were a child. And
probably it will do no good, but, darling, don't re-
peat my mistake. Be certain, before you marry him,
that a man is everything you demand because you
won't change him, fundamentally, and I'm afraid
you'd try. And don't be too harsh with Julia. She
did tell the truth. It wasn't her fault."
"But it is, Mother!" Caroline cried stormily, un-
able to exonerate her sister, unwilling to retract a
single word she'd said last night when Julia, expect-
ing her to approve, had told her that she was going
to Washington with Scott. "Julia has been laying a
foundation for a smash like this all her life. If she'd
always been truthful, not caring what people thought
of her, not so susceptible to flattery and fawning,
this couldn't have happened. That she did tell the
truth, this once, doesn't excuse her. Of course it was
wrong for Dad to use the money. It was wrong and
stupid, whatever his motive. But if Julia hadn't
promised to marry Jay, knowing she didn't love him,
not intending to keep her promise, Mrs. Summerfield
wouldn't have thought of prosecuting. It might be a
lesson Dad needs, but you don't want him to go to
jail for a miserable eighteen hundred dollars. You
don't want people to know. That would be the end
of everything. No one would ever trust him again.
And Dad is, comparatively, a young man. He has
years to live. And so have you. You-you didn't
mean what you said."
"No, no, I didn't!" Mother started up from thepillows, then as though that exertion had exhausted
her last ounce of strength, fell back again. "What
can we do to help him?" she asked hopelessly, tears
running down over her face, her voice scarcely louder
than a whisper. "Caroline, baby, what can we do?"
It was at that moment that Julia entered the
room. She was obviously dressed to go out, looking
pretty, always so damnably pretty, Caroline thought
bitterly. Her simply cut dress of heavy cream-colored
silk was belted with coral to match her sandals. Her
wide brimmed hat of coral-colored straw cast a be-
coming reflection over her face, shaded her wide dark
eyes, giving them a poignant expression of sadness,
which, Caroline felt, was entirely a work of art.
Julia was off to make her peace with the town, to
put herself in the right, to win back any admirers
who might have had the discourtesy to think she
was even a little to blame. Caroline deliberately
turned her eyes from the pleading glance Julia gave,
stood watching her sister with bitter scorn she made
no effort to conceal.
"Don't cry, Mother." Julia bent over the bed.
"Everything is going to be all right."
"I'm a little tired, darling." Caroline saw Moth-
er's face brighten as she looked up at Julia. What
was the secret art Julia practiced, that Mother
should respond to what she must know was a com-
pletely unfounded assurance? Did Mother, every-
one, prefer charming insincerities to the truth? How
could Julia speak so confidently, look so pretty,when she was going away with Scott and leave them
to get out of the difficulty as best they could?
"Of course you are." Julia's lips brushed Moth-
er's white cheek. "But don't worry. Sleep if you can.
Scott is a lawyer, you know. He'll find a way out for
us. He wants to do anything he can to help."
"That's good of Scott."
"He's wonderful. I know everything will be all
right. I'm going downtown, to the office to see Dad.
I hadn't a chance to talk to him last night and when
I woke this morning he was gone."
Julia's voice trembled. If it was acting, Caroline
thought with a treacherous softening toward sym-
pathy, it was expertly done. But it was acting- No,
that was unfair. Julia was troubled and distressed
now, seeing Mother look so ill. The tears misting
her eyes were real enough, but how soon they van-
ished! If Caroline knew her, and she had no doubts
about that, the first admiring glance that fell upon
her, as she walked downtown, would send her spirits
flying up toward the sky again. There was simply
no justification for Julia's behavior, she told herself
firmly to stiffen into disapproval the reluctant ten-
derness that stirred in her heart. She, at least, wasn't
going to be cajoled into even a semblance of forgive-
ness by Julia's pretty tricks.
"Can I bring you anything? Can I do anything
for you downtown?"
Julia's pleading glance across the bed which sepa-
rated them was very disarming. Caroline felt her
defenses weakening, as they had threatened to lastnight when Julia had confided in her, had told her
that Scott wanted to take her to Washington with
him. Julia had wanted her to sympathize, to assure
her that what had happened wasn't her fault, to
share in the happy excitement which, in spite of
everything, had set the words Julia spoke to music,
had filled her eyes with melted stars. Julia wanted
that now. To deny her that was like punishing a
child for unintentional naughtiness, for-
Child? Julia was nearly two years older than she.
Now, as she had last night, Caroline strengthened
her hostility by thinking of Mother, of Dad, of
Jay
"No, thank you," she said coolly. "I think you've
done quite enough for all of us."
She saw quick tears brim up in Julia's eyes, saw
her lips quiver before she turned and went out of
the room. Caroline stood motionless, hearing the
tap of Julia's heels along the hall, down the stairs.
She wanted to call her, run after her, but a stubborn
certainty of being in the right, triumphed over senti-
ment. The screen door, which always squeaked,
opened and closed.XIX
JULIA INTENDED TO KEEP HER PROMISE TO Scott.
Even last night when Caroline had told her plainly
that this catastrophe was entirely her fault, she
hadn't thought of going to see Jay. She intended
to keep her promise, Julia reminded herself, walking
along the sidewalk where sunshine coming through
the boughs of the maple trees made shifting patterns
of light and shade. She didn't care how Caroline mis-
judged her, if Caroline never forgave her as long as
she lived
But she did care. She simply couldn't imagine
spending the rest of her life in the shadow of Caro-
line's disapproval. Caroline was terribly worried, of
course, but she might have been a little more con-
siderate, not jumped to the conclusion, as she had
last night, that she was going with Scott to avoid
the unpleasantness of being at home. She hadn't been
able to make Caroline understand that Scott had
made the original suggestion, had had to persuade
her that to go with him was the wisest, the safest
thing she could do.
She'd felt, yesterday, that Dad understood, that
Mother did not blame her. Why, then, should Caro-
line act as though she had deliberately plotted to
destroy the family, to kill Mother and send Dad toprison? She hadn't slept a wink last night, well, not
until daylight, anyway, just remembering the things
Caroline had said to her, forgiving her for them,
but hoping for, expecting an apology this morning.
And if Caroline, her sister, had turned against her,
what would other people think? -
That wasn't important, of course. She didn't care.
She'd dressed attractively to go downtown to see
Dad, as attractively as she could, looking wan, as
she did, with great circles under her eyes. And Caro-
line had misjudged her motives, had looked at her,
there in Mother's room, as though she despised her.
Tears, not only of self-pity but of genuine grief,
sprang to her eyes again, blurring her vision so that
she did not notice the group on the Carters' front
porch until Allie hailed her, came bounding like an
angular gazelle down the steps and across the lawn,
followed by Cora Belle Caton and Jinny Lucas.
Julia winked back the tears, drew a steadying
breath, returned the greeting with a suitably tem-
pered smile of saddened cordiality. For they knew.
Of course they knew!
"Oh, Julia! We've just heard!" Allie drew her
mouth down into an inverted curve but her pale
eyes gleamed with excitement behind the lenses of
her glasses. "Our Rosa is the Summerfields' Hallie's
cousin so, of course, Rosa told us when she came
this morning that Jay had shot himself."
"And we're simply agonized with sympathy for
you," Cora Belle Caton put in, lisping a little as she
did when she was excited. "We were just saying be-fore we saw you that you must be utterly wrecked
with anxiety."
The appraising glance of her small bright eyes
embedded in her round face like raisins in a bun
indicated that she found Julia's condition anything
but wrecked. What a strident voice she had! Julia
made her own voice gentle and musical.
"I'm terribly sorry, of course," she said softly.
"I'm sorry for any misfortune that happens to one
of my friends."
"Twice in succession must be a little shattering,"
Jinny Lucas said in an off-hand voice but with a
meaning Julia could not ignore. Jay rather liked
Jinny Lucas, Julia recalled. He said she had a good
mind. He'd taken her to the Christmas dances the
holiday that she, Julia, had spent in Richmond visit-
ing the Lacey twins. Jinny was handsome, she sup-
posed, if anyone's taste ran to brawn and tailored
shorts.
"But why should Jay Summerfield have done such
a thing?" Allie asked.
"After having been so ill and just gotten home
and looking simply wonderful," Cora Belle added
breathlessly. "I saw him yesterday and he was the
picture of health."
"Une belle dame sans merci," Jinny murmured
causing her companions to regard her with respect
but complete incomprehension.
The situation did have a romantic aspect, Julia
thought, translating Jinny's French a little sketchily.
There weren't many girls for whom men shot them-selves. But there was less of awe and admiration
than of gloating inquisitiveness in the glances of
these girls who were supposed to be her friends.
Their eyes, fixed upon her, picked her to pieces and
not only their eyes but the eyes of Mrs. Carter and
Cora Belle Caton's mother peering interestedly from
the porch. She felt, curiously, humiliatingly, that she
had lost caste, like royalty dethroned and set upon
by the rabble which yesterday had knelt and fawned.
Was her popularity based upon the fact that for so
long she'd been Jay's girl and in high favor with
the Summerfields?
She knew how to put them in their places. All she
would have to do would be to tell them that she was
on her way to the Summerfields' now, that whatever
Jay had done was no indication that she had lost
favor. She imagined, with pleasure, how that an-
nouncement would take the wind out of their sails.
But she had promised Scott that she wouldn't and
she intended to keep her promise. These people
weren't important. Only Scott was important, being
worthy of his trust and his love.
"I know how interested and distressed you are,"
she said softly, "but I'm afraid you'll have to call at
the Summerfields' for details. I don't feel that I can
discuss Jay's affairs even with my best friends. I hate
having to run along but I have an important message
for Dad. Come to see us whenever you can. Caroline
and I love having our friends drop in."
The virtuous glow of having resisted temptation,
of having kept her promise to Scott, supported herthrough the ordeal of getting away from them, of
knowing they could scarcely contain themselves until
she was out of ear-shot. What they thought of her
was a matter of complete indifference to her, she
told herself, walking on at a purposely sauntering
pace, maintaining, since you could never be sure that
invisible eyes weren't peering through curtains or
vines, the tempered expression of saddened serenity
which she had put on for her friends.
But the glow faded when, at the corner, she met
Mr. Glenworthy turning into Main from a side
street, and he merely bowed very gravely to her,
addressing her as "Miss Perry," though he'd always
called her "Miss Julia," and passed her without
stopping. Not that she'd wanted to talk to him, she
assured herself, watching him almost run across the
street, though she'd been certain that he had in-
tended to go on downtown. The rector was taking
no chances, she thought, with a bitterness that was
new and torturing. Mrs. Summerfield's contributions
were the mainstay of the church and she had prom-
ised a stained glass window in memory of her hus-
band. Whatever Mr. Glenworthy had been told or
surmised, he didn't intend to have Mrs. Carter and
Mrs. Caton see him walk with her until he was
certain such seemingly open partisanship would not
offend Jay's mother.
She didn't care, she told herself, wanting to turn
and run home as fast as she could, forcing herself
to continue on her way downtown. She'd done noth-
ing of which she had any reason to feel ashamed,nothing that merited being treated like an outcast.
If Mr. Glenworthy considered contributions and
stained glass windows more important than simple
courtesy, that was a reflection upon his character
and not upon her own. After the way she'd slaved
over his pageant, too, keeping on, though she was
bored to tears, because he had imposed upon her
kindness of heart. He was ridiculous, anyway, with
his accent and his meditations.
She didn't care- But if by going to see Jay, she
could end this terrible tension- She knew that she
could. If Jay saw her, wan, as she looked this morn-
ing, with circles-no, violet shadows-under her
eyes, he would do anything she asked. Jay had loved
her devotedly for so long. Not that she wanted to see
him. The thought of having to face Miss Isabel
made her feel positively faint. But wasn't she shirk-
ing a duty, when she and she alone had the power
to convince him that he must not let his mother do
what she had threatened to do? Of course Jay
wanted to see her. How fortunate that she'd taken a
little trouble with her appearance this morning, that
she was wearing -
No. She had promised Scott. She had told the
truth and she would see it through. But Scott
couldn't even imagine how she felt, walking alone
down this familiar street, the target for prying eyes,
for snubs, where once she had been loved and ad-
mired. Where had the confidence gone she had felt
last night, sitting with Scott in the hammock, takingstrength from his strength, loving him, wanting to
do anything he asked?
A car drew up at the curbing beside her and a
familiar voice broke in through the confusion of her
thoughts. Bryan Dudley, when she glanced around,
was all but hurling himself through the lowered win-
dow of his mother's coupe.
"Darling!" he cried, his voice shrill with emotion
and excitement. "I was on my way to you, but how
fortunate to find you here. Come on into the drug-
store-" He broke off flushing up over his round
forehead to the edges of his hair. "How gauche of
me!" he apologized, putting a volume of meaning
into the adjective. "Get it. We'll drive around to
the square where we can talk."
She didn't want to talk to Bryan Dudley. But
the coupe would serve as a temporary haven and
Bryan looked friendly, at least, though even more
than ordinarily conspicuous in fawn-colored cordu-
roy pants and a raspberry shirt which must certainly
once have been blue before he dyed it because there
were mottled patches of purple here and there. She
waited while he sprang out of the car to assist her,
not unaware of the careful grasp of his hand on her
arm, as though she might faint at any moment. The
car jolted into motion.
"Julia darling! I've simply suffered for you!" He
lifted his hands from the wheel to emphasize his
emotions and the car took a swerve toward the ice
truck coming toward them.
Julia gave a scream."Did I frighten you? What a state your nerves
must be in! Simply shattered."
"I'm sorry," she said gently. "I am a little jumpy
this morning. Will you please keep your hands on
the wheel, Bryan dear?"
He demonstrated his contrition by driving the car
at a snail's pace, but eventually they reached the
courthouse square, nearly deserted at this hour on
Friday morning, and parked in the shade.
"Julia," Bryan said intensely, free, now, to use
his hands in passionate gesticulation. "I want to
assure you of my utter devotion and loyalty. I in-
formed my mother and sisters-Ella is visiting with
offspring-that I simply refused to listen to any
criticism of you."
"Do they are they criticizing me?"
"I know, knowing how kind and how sweet you are
-You are as nearly pure spirit, Julia, as any woman
I've ever known. I know that you think everyone is
as sweet and as loving as you are," Bryan went on,
floundering among emotional phrases. "But beauty
will be envied, my dear, and people are only too
ready to croak and gloat."
He went on to discourse upon the envious nature
of plain women, women who were not so pure in
spirit as Julia was, or so sensitive or so kind. Soothed
a little by his fidelity, though only half hearing the
words he spoke, Julia's gaze drifted across grass,
green after the rain yesterday, across borders of
geraniums, to the courthouse across the square. The
square building, white-pillared, looked peaceful thismorning with birds pecking at peanut shells scattered
on the steps, darting in and out of the vines that
traced a leafy pattern against the mellowed bricks. It
was there that Dad's trial would be held. She pic-
tured other trials; cars packed in the square, people
crowding around the steps of the courthouse, sky-

clarity she realized, for the first time, the possible
consequences of Mrs. Summerfield's threat, saw
Mother, Caroline, herself, pushing through the
crowd, jeered at as the families of other criminals
had been, saw Dad-
"Do you feel faint, Julia?"
Bryan's voice came to her from a distance. She
jerked herself back into the safety of the present,
this moment, while there was still time
"I didn't sleep last night. The shock-" She broke
off, remembering just in time that Bryan had no
idea, yet, how severe the shock had been.
Fortunately, he assumed that she referred to
Jay.
"I know," he said. "I had a premonition that
Jay Summerfield was going to do something des-
perate, yesterday, when I saw him fling himself
into his car."
Scott simply did not realize what he asked of
her. No matter in what direction she tried to look,
her eyes kept turning back to the white pillars on
the courthouse. She'd been too abrupt in telling
Jay, wanting to do what Scott thought was right.She knew Jay better than Scott did. Scott didn't
know him at all.
"I've always felt that I was psychic," Bryan was
saying when she gave him her partial attention
again, "and now I'm sure of it. When the telephone
rang this morning I-"
It wasn't because she cared what people thought
of her. Oh, of course, she cared, but not enough to
break her promise to Scott. She did care for Dad
and Mother and Caroline, though. She silenced the
voice which kept reminding her of her promise by
picturing Mother's joy, Dad's relief, Caroline's

no matter how it distressed her.
"I was ready for Allie Carter when she called
to give Sis the news." Julia was listening, now, giv-
ing Bryan her entire attention. "I told her," he
went on, "that Jay was cleaning a gun and it went
off accidentally. Not a very original story, I'm
afraid."
She knew, now, what she must do and how she
was going to do it.
"You are psychic, Bryan," she said, tilting her
head back to give him a look of wonder and ad-
miration, "because that is what happened, though
I'm afraid there are people so uncharitable as to
spread malicious gossip."
Bryan's expression altered. He looked curiously,
almost amusingly deflated, a Sidney Carton who hadoffered himself upon the guillotine only to find that
it was a sight-seeing bus he had mounted.
"But it couldn't have been," he protested. "That
is what we must make people think, of course, but
you and I know, Julia. Remember I saw him when
he drove away and if ever I've seen despera-
tion"
Her voice became gently reproachful. Her lashes
fell, then lifted.
"I'm afraid you've been listening to gossip," she
said. "I suppose it's only natural, but I thought, I
hoped, that I could count on you, Bryan, that you,
at least, were a faithful friend."
"Oh, Julia, I do assure you-," he began in dis-
tressed tones.
"Because I was on my way to the Summerfields'
when I met you," she went on, "which doesn't look
as though there was any enmity between Jay and
me."
"But we, everybody, had supposed that you were
going to marry Jay until this summer when this
Mr. Mitchell has been so attentive to you. So, nat-
urally, when this happens as soon as Jay comes home,
it does look as though he'd shot himself because
you jilted him. Don't think that I would dream of
discussing your private affairs with anyone," he

"I know you wouldn't." Julia let her hand fall
forgivingly on his arm. She knew that he would,
though. Bryan adored flitting from front porch to
front porch with choice tidbits of gossip. Shecouldn't have found a more reliable messenger.
The town would have been well informed before
evening. "And now, will you do me one more favor?
Will you drive me to the Summerfields'?"
"Why, certainly, Julia." He set the car into mo-
tion obediently but with obvious reluctance.
He talked earnestly as they drove out of the
square and on along Main street, reinstating him-
self in her favor, apologizing with veiled reticence
for having entertained the idea that Julia might
have jilted Jay. Everyone would be doing that be-
fore evening, Julia thought, paying very little at-
tention to what Bryan said, the girls she's met this
morning, the rector, Caroline. Scott would under-
stand when she told him. How happy they would
all be at dinner to-night, for, of course, Aunt Gena
would send them the money to repay Mrs. Summer-
field for what Dad had used. Aunt Gena could do
that instead of helping to pay for a wedding. She
and Scott would be married very simply at home,
though probably Aunt Gena would insist on a wed-
ding, too, because she had no daughter of her own
and was sentimental about ceremonies. How for-
tunate that she'd happened to meet Bryan! He was
silly and posed a good deal but she couldn't think

She felt really fond of him and made gentle com-
ments whenever she thought that he needed to
pause for breath.
But she made him stop the car and let her out
when they turned the corner into the street whichsloped down to the river. He showed an irritating
disposition to linger so that she was obliged to be
a little curt in her thanks before he got in behind
the wheel again.
Was he waiting to see whether or not she was
actually going to the Summerfields'? She resisted
a strong desire to look back as she walked along
the high brick wall which enclosed the Summerfields'
garden, her pace consciously slackening as she ap-
proached the gate set flush with the street. The

doubt as to whether or not she had actually heard
him start the car.
She had no reason to feel nervous, she told her-
self. Absurd to have the idea that the wall, the
wrought iron gate, formally merely a pictur-
esque detail of the property, were there to snub
her, to keep her out. The garden behind the wall,
the beautifully proportioned house, the lawn sloping
down to the river were as familiar to her as was
her home. Even having told Jay the truth, the
scene that had been reported to her ought not to
give her this humiliating, this frightening feeling
of being an interloper, an unwanted intruder. And
she was breaking her promise to Scott -
-
She stood with her hand on the latch of the gate,
looking through iron scrollwork at the side entrance
to the house. She saw herself coming out, Sherry
beaming behind her in the doorway, Miss Isabel
seeing her off, sad, of course, but gracious and un-
derstanding, and possibly Jay- But no, Jay wouldbe in his room, that big room on the second floor
which contained in addition to a choice array of
authentic antiques at least twenty pictures of her.
The comfort which this vision gave her was of short
duration. She was still standing outside, her hand
on the latch of the gate -
Perhaps Scott knew best. There was still time
to keep her promise. But had Bryan gone? She
looked back over her shoulder. The Dudleys' coupe
was no longer in sight but, as she stood, like Lot's
wife looking back, Scott's car passed the intersec-
tion at the top of the grade, Scott's car with the
top down and Scott, himself, behind the wheel.
Was he spying on her? For an instant she was
furious with him for making her feel guilty and

She wasn't certain that he had seen her. She

"Mister Jay doin' as well as can be expected,
Miss."
"Miss" to her in that formal way when she'd

"May I see him?"
"Mister Jay ain't receivin' no comp'ny, Miss."

But perhaps she could bring Scott here when the

Flushed with the triumph of her victory over

CAROLINE GLANCED UP FROM THE LAST SHEET OF
proof she was correcting as Dusty came into the
front office from the pressroom, the sleeves of his
shirt rolled up over his elbows, his collar and tie
loosened, his hair tousled, a streak of ink on his chin.
"Nearly through, Ca'line?" he asked.
"Just finished." She arched her back against the
back of the chair, closed her eyes to free her vision
from dancing lines of type, opened them, handed
the corrected proof to Dusty.
"Pretty tired, aren't you?" he asked sympa-
thetically.
"No. Fresh as a daisy." She went wearily about
the accustomed business of tidying the desk, throw-
ing crumpled wads of paper into the wastebasket,
sorting through the litter of rubber bands, pencils,
paperclips, printed sheets of advertising, crumpled
packs of cigarettes which accumulated on Dad's
desk during the course of a day.
"Let Nelse do that. Oh, all right. Go ahead. I'll
see that this last page is struck off, then I'm going
to take you home." He stood looking down at the
top of her curly brown head with an expression of
protective tenderness in his eyes. She glanced up,
caught the expression and smiled."You're a comfort, Dusty. But don't bother to
take me home. You must be tired yourself. You've

"Which should entitle me to a little considera-
tion," he drawled. "I've fixed it up with Nelse and
Jim. They're working overtime and no nonsense
about it being beneath a boss printer's dignity to
fold papers and stick them in wrappers." He lin-
gered beside the desk, the sheet of proof in one ink-

to get myself invited for dinner, if there's going

you'd let me buy you a meal somewhere. You look

"Certainly there will be dinner at home. And
you're invited. I can promise you food but not a
very cheerful atmosphere."
"Don't you worry about that. It's a break that
we're getting out the paper, isn't it? We can blandly
ignore young Jay's latest escapade. Think what a
dither there'd be in the pressroom to-day if we didn't
have that sewed up."
Did she imagine that his reference to Jay was

when he'd come to the house, when she'd burst

It was because to absolve Julia, even secretly, in

Wasn't the fact that Julia had treated him badly

glance at his profile told her nothing. "It wasn't my

"but you know how your Dad is. He can't get downto the tiresome business of check-stubs and figures.
I tried to cover up as well as I could. It was just
possible that he might have made a killing some-
where. A good deal of business is done that way,
Ca'line. I'd be too cautious to try it myself which
is probably why I'll never set the world on fire as a
financial genius. Besides, I was working on a scheme
of my own and if I'd had a little more time I might
have pulled it off."
"What was that?" Caroline asked, interested in
Dusty's schemes which were usually both practical
and ingenious.
"When I found out Mrs. Summerfield wanted to
sell the paper," he replied, "I set off in my busy lit-
tle way to form a corporation. Neither your Dad
nor I had the cash, which she wants, to buy it out-
right, so I tackled people who have. Judge Carroll
helped me considerably. We agreed that the most
practical way to go about it would be to sell shares,
reserving a few for your Dad and for me at the
start and then buy them in as the profits of the
paper increased, for they would have increased. The
combination of your Dad's ability and personality
and my tiresome efficiency makes that darned little
country paper the best investment in the county at
the moment. Judge Carroll knew of the shortage,
too. He agreed with me that it would not be illegal
to lump that in with liabilities, providing your Dad
would assume responsibility for restitution and we
were able to raise Mrs. Summerfield's price.""Then you and Dad would have owned the
paper?"
"Not for a good many years," Dusty replied.
"Never, maybe, but we would have had free rein
in formulating policies. We could have done away
with some of the dodo notions that have hampered
us, policies that have persisted because Mrs. Sum-
merfield wasn't interested and nobody had a legal
right to throw them on the scrap pile where they
belonged. With a corporation behind us we couldn't
have lost, and Mrs. Summerfield wouldn't have been
able to threaten prosecution. If I'd had even a few
more days, I might have managed to swing it."
She was moved by his earnestness, touched by the
wistful tone in his voice.
"Oh, I think that was swell of you, Dusty," she
said, looking up at him with admiration in her
brightened glance. "When you knew what Dad was
doing-"
His eyes narrowed and twinkled and his grin
slanted a little teasingly.
"Don't be too set up, Ca'line," he drawled. "I
wasn't doing it because I think you're a pretty grand
gal, though I do. I was doing it from pure selfish-
ness, for Beverley Goldsborough Price, because I'd
rather own a hunk of the Observer than be Wil-
liam Randolph Hearst himself. So if you're busy
making a tin halo for me you might just as well
save yourself the trouble."
"But would you trust Dad again?" she persisted."I mean if this thing could be adjusted? Would you
be willing?"
"Like a shot," he said, as she faltered. "He's
a good editor. I'm not and never would be. But I'd

he added, diffidence exaggerating the naturally

comes down off her high-horse, if she'd graciously

it be possible? I mean "
"You mean could we scrape up the amount in

of course. Aunt Gena would help and Uncle Tom.
They'd make a great splutter about it, I suppose,
but they'd come across."
"I've got a couple of hundred dollars," Dusty
said. "If you'd need it"
"Oh, Dusty!" Her voice trembled. "Dusty, you
dear."
"Shut up, Ca'line," he said almost brusquely.

"But I we do appreciate your thinking of it.
Only-" The eagerness faded from her voice. "Only

"I can imagine," Dusty said grimly. "The Duchess
has been offended. Off with a dozen heads. You

I think I could point out a startling resemblance."She smiled wanly and looked away from Dusty
across the Carter's lawn, green in the translucent

blooming against the clipped privet hedge to the

This tragedy that threatened them couldn't be real.
She found comfort, for a moment, in the thought
that when she and Dusty reached home they would

But it was. Dusty's face, when she glanced up at

"If we only had something on the Duchess," he

and the Summerfields without the assistance of sev-

Coursey who made a fortune selling flour full of

Caroline laughed, then asked, "That would be

She had offended him. He was hurt and trying

"

"She deserves to be thrown to the lions!" Hervoice rose again. "Everyone defends her because

never forgive her as long as she lives. If she
"
Dusty stopped, halting her progress.
"You don't mean that," he said gently. "It isn't

He was right. She knew that if anyone else in
the world had been involved, she would have ap-

Jay, of idealizing him, was too strong to be lightly

She felt that she couldn't wait to see Julia, to make
some gesture of kindness toward her sister.
But softening emotion hardened into hostility
again when, as she and Dusty walked through the

the porch being charming to Mr. Glenworthy.
"It was kind of you to come," Julia was sayingin soft, slightly saddened tones when Caroline and
Dusty reached the foot of the steps.
"I was calling at the Elliotts' and saw the doctor's
car here." The young rector held Julia's hand in a

hope you don't think it was presumptuous of me to

"We do appreciate your coming." Julia's expres-

"Mother will be sorry not to have seen you, but
the doctor insists upon absolute quiet."
Yes, the gleam in her eyes was an expression of
triumph. Julia, in spite of everything that had hap-
pened, could look smug and triumphant because that
Miss Nancy in clerics held her hand.
The smug expression vanished when the rector
became aware of her presence and Dusty's, had

"Well, that's that," Julia said, actually preening
herself like a bird beside a pool. There was a note
of excitement in her voice, excitement in the smile

"I have something to tell you, Cal," she went on,all animosity apparently forgotten as Julia did for-
get anything that it pained her to remember. "You,
too, Dusty."
"Are you about to announce your engagement to
the rector?" Caroline asked coolly, resenting Julia's
light-hearted manner. Julia looked fresh and rested
as though she had slept all afternoon while the rest
of them suffered and toiled, which was probably ex-
actly what she had done.
Julia looked surprised, then a little offended, but
too interested in the news she had to convey to nur-
ture resentment.
"I think everything is going to be all right," she
said brightly. "Wait until I tell you, Cal."
"Tell Dusty." Caroline walked past her sister, de-
liberately holding herself aloof, seeing the bright-
ness fade out of Julia's face, hearing Dusty's mut-
tered exclamation. "I'm going up to see Mother,"
she said, "then out to feed the dogs. I'm sorry I
can't regard this as a slight misunderstanding.
You're fortunate in having an optimistic disposi-
tion."XXII
THE DOOR BETWEEN HER ROOM AND JULIA'S STOOD
open when Caroline went upstairs. Julia sat at her
dressing-table, leaning toward the mirror, working
interestedly with her hair. Caroline kicked off her
slipper and it fell with a thud on a bare space of
floor between the rugs. Julia gave a start, turned,
still seated, so that Caroline saw both her face and,
reflected in the mirror, the soft dark curls pinned in
at the nape of her neck. If Julia had only been born
homely, she thought with grim humor. If only she'd
looked like Cousin Mollie instead of having inherited
great-grandmother Peyton's romantic beauty. Or if
she'd been born with a shred of character instead of
having been blessed with Dad's irresponsible
charm
-
"Oh, Cal!" Julia said, hesitantly, as though she
wasn't certain that her sister wanted to speak to
her. "I knew you'd have to rush so I've drawn your
bath."
"Thanks," Caroline said briefly, intending to get
across to Julia the idea that she wasn't to be won
over by sisterly attentions, though it was considerate
of Julia to have thought of it. Perhaps a hot bath
with pine salts would relieve the weariness thatdragged at her limbs. If only there were some such
simple way of relieving her mind
"I would have laid out your clothes," Julia con-
tinued, "if I'd known what you wanted to wear."
"I didn't know we were giving a party." Caroline
yanked her linen frock off over her head and sent a
disapproving glance through the open door.
"I can't see that it does any harm to look nice."
Julia rose from the stool before the dressing-table,
folds of flesh-colored chiffon patterned with deeper
pink azaleas and pale-green leaves settling with a
rippling motion against her slender figure and pretty
legs. "When everybody feels so terribly, looking nice
does help."
"Heavens, Julia!" Caroline cried, stung to in-
creased irritation at the sight of Julia patting per-
fume from a tilted bottle on the lobes of her ears,
her wrists, the palms of her hands. "Please reserve
those platitudes for conversations with your pretty
rector. I can't endure any more of your 'God's in
his heaven, all's right with the world' to-night."
But Julia's faith in the future was shaken. Caro-
line was aware, when Julia came to the door, that
her sister was genuinely distressed. Her wide eyes
were dark and frightened and her face was white
under petal touches of rouge. She'd looked like that
downstairs in the hall when Dad and Scott had come
in, when she and Scott had met and he'd greeted her
so gravely.
But they'd all been grave and frightened. She,
herself, hadn't realized how strongly they'd countedon Mrs. Summerfield changing her mind when she'd
gotten over her first frenzy of anxiety for Jay, until
Dad told them that he and Scott had been to see
her and that no persuasion, no promises, no sug-
gested arbitration had had any effect upon her de-
termination to carry out her threat. They had re-
ceived the ultimatum with varying expressions of
emotion. Dusty had cursed with eloquence. Scott
had looked grave and troubled. Dad had slumped
down on the chesterfield, so exhausted and despair-
ing that she'd gone to sit beside him. Julia had at-
tempted to be cheering, at first, but had finally given
in and wept. Mother, alone, did not know that all
hope was lost.
"Cal," Julia said tremulously. "Cal, what's going
to happen?"
"I should think that would be perfectly obvious,"
Caroline said, wanting to hurt Julia because she her-
self was suffering, hardening her heart to the ap-
peal in her sister's lovely face, in the listless way
she crossed the room to drop down on Caroline's
bed. "You were cheerful enough this afternoon," she
continued, "telling Dusty and me that everything
was going to be all right."
Julia winced, then continued.
"But that was before Dad and Scott came home.
I didn't actually believe she'd do it, not even after
what she said-" She broke off, sat twisting an end
of dark-green ribbon velvet that lay across her lap.
"Cal," she said, after a moment, "what will hap-
pen? I mean how will they do it?""According to the usual procedure, I suppose."
Caroline snatched a bathrobe from a hook inside
the closet door. "The sheriff will appear with a
warrant for Dad's arrest, either here or at the of-
fice."
"Not Jim Merrick?" Julia's eyes widened with
incredulity. "Why, he and Dad are friends. They
go duck shooting every fall."
"It will probably be very painful to him, but he'll
be obliged to do his duty." Caroline took up a bottle
of bath-salts from her dressing-table but came back
to the foot of the bed. "Dusty will be obliged to do
his duty, too. He'll have to sit on the witness stand
and testify that he knew Dad was stealing. And
Dusty really loves Dad."
Julia sat looking down at the length of velvet
slipping through her fingers. Presently she raised
her head.
"When will it happen?" she asked. "I mean, when
will they take Dad to jail?"
"Immediately, if we can't find someone to post his
bail." She was horrified when she realized what she
was doing, standing here in her white toweling bath-

spoke, taking this queer savage pleasure in terrify-
ing her sister. Anger drove her on, the certainty
that nothing could avert the tragedy which threat-
ened her family, the rankling humiliation of know-
ing that Dusty knew she loved Jay. She did not feel
like herself. It was as though the hostility she felt
had an identity of its own, that it was beratingJulia while she, who loved Julia, stood by, helpless,
frightened, ashamed.
"How long will they keep him in jail?"
Julia's questions, asked with the simplicity of a be-
wildered child, increased her exasperation. Julia
simply didn't realize, even yet, that this was tragedy,
the end of the family, complete and irrevocable dis-
grace.
"I don't know," Caroline said crisply. "That will
depend upon the sentence Judge Carroll imposes."
"Judge Carroll?" Julia whispered the name in a
sort of breathless horror and again her eyes widened
incredulously. "Will Judge Carroll have to sentence
Dad?"
"This isn't a Never-Never Land, Julia, where
ugly things can't happen because you are sweet-
tempered and pretty and dress for dinner." Caro-
line's sarcasm stung through the air with a sound
like a whip cracking, frightening her with unantici-
pated violence of her desire to hurt her sister.
"You've been waltzing over rose gardens for so long
that you've lost sight of the fact that there is a
courthouse in the square with a jail behind it. That
courthouse isn't merely an ornament to the town.
Trials are held there, in case you've overlooked the
fact, and Judge Carroll's sole occupation in life isn't
drinking mint juleps with Dad."
A quiver passed over Julia's face, lifted to Caro-
line's furious glance.
"I know," she said quietly. "There were birds on
the steps this morning and flying in and out of thevines." She seemed completely detached, not re-
senting Caroline's open accusation, thinking her own
thoughts, whatever they were, making, for once in
her life, no attempt to justify herself with the curious
logic she usually employed. Caroline stood watch-
ing her, puzzled, wondering what thoughts moved
through her head under the silky dark hair, resent-
ing the childlike faith in her luck, in her charm,
which made it possible for her to sit there in her
dainty dress with perfume on the lobes of her ears
so quietly, so apparently unmoved.
"Don't you realize, Julia?" Caroline's voice rose
to an hysterical pitch. "Haven't you any idea what
this means to all of us? But of course you haven't,"
she added bitterly. "You'll be out of the mess. You'll
be in Washington with Scott."
Again a quiver passed over Julia's face. Her lips
trembled and tears came into her eyes. She winked
them away and looked up at her sister, holding
Caroline's eyes in a direct, unresentful, oddly moving
glance.
"You're being very unfair," she said slowly, "but
I suppose you can't help it. You really believe that
I'm to blame for all this so of course you despise
me." She rose from the bed, her filmy skirt floating,
looking with the curling fringe on her forehead, the
bodice of her dress slipping off her shoulders, like
the portrait of great-grandmother Peyton which
hung in a museum in Richmond. "If I tell you I can
make this come right, it will only exasperate you fur-
ther. But I can and I will." She stood beside hersister, looking directly at Caroline, her face reflect-
ing in its brightened expression a lifting of spirits
though there was sadness in the smile that trembled
across her lips. "Whether you believe it or not, I'd
do anything for you and Dad and Mother. Don't
worry, darling. I'll make it all come right."
What did Julia mean? Nothing, probably. She
was merely trying to ease the situation by one of
her expressions of unfounded optimism. Julia did
not realize, even yet, that this was actual tragedy,
a reality that could not be averted by any of her
pretty tricks or charming deceptions.
But though she made an effort to maintain the
scorching heat of justified anger and exasperation,
Caroline felt the force of her hostility melting into
vague concern for Julia. She watched her sister's
graceful figure disappear through the door through
a film of contrite tears.XXIII
JULIA KNEW THAT SCOTT WAS AWARE SHE HAD
broken her promise. She'd known it when their eyes
met this afternoon, when he'd come home with Dad
after the interview with Mrs. Summerfield. Every
glance that he gave her across the table, across the
centerpiece of sweet william and cornflowers and
white sweet peas she had arranged because it was
the evening before the Fourth of July and because,
though Caroline ridiculed the idea, it didn't do any
harm to make things look pretty, reaffirmed that
knowledge.
But in spite of the patriotic centerpiece, in spite
of thin curls of Virginia ham and salad and beaten
biscuits and iced tea with sprigs of mint, dinner was
a dreary affair. As though by mutual agreement, no
one mentioned the situation in which they were all
involved. They might as well have, Julia thought,
because each person at the table knew that the others
were preoccupied with the subject which limited con-
versation and dragged out interminable intervals of
silence.
Dusty made an occasional attempt to lighten the
gloom, but he and Caroline had evidently quarreled
and his heart wasn't in it. Caroline had merely ex-
changed her rumpled linen frock for a fresh one asa pointed criticism of her making herself look at-
tractive. She ate scarcely anything, avoided the
glances she, Julia, sent her, replied to comments
directed to her crisply and with brevity which got
them nowhere.
Scott was silent or introduced topics of conversa-
tion in which no one, not even he, himself, was in-
terested. Mother's chair was vacant. Dad looked
up from his plate only when someone spoke directly
to him and then his expression was so humble, so
ashamed, his eyes moved around the table with such
an obvious consciousness of being with them only on
sufferance, that she'd had hard work to wink back
the tears that kept springing to her eyes. Even
Louisa suffered from the contagious despondency
of the atmosphere. She minced in and out looking
as though she expected the big bad wolf to spring
in through a window and gobble her up at any
moment.
She, herself, had tried, at first, to be cheerful. But
Caroline's snubbing replies to her overtures were dis-
couraging and Scott looked at her in such a grave
and troubled way that she'd given it up and sat
eating small bites of ham and biscuit, choking down
sips of iced tea in miserable silence. She couldn't
help contrasting this dismal meal with the first din-
ner Scott had had here at the house. How handsome
and genial Dad had been that night, serving fried
chicken in his expansively hospitable way. She re-
membered how gracious Mother had been, Caro-
line's yellow dress, Dusty's amusingly flippant re-marks, Scott's dark-blue eyes meeting hers through
the candlelight with that expression of affectionate
amusement which she'd resented at first and then
had come to love. The contrast between that dinner
and this gave her a realization of the gravity of the
situation more pointed than Caroline's sarcasm, so
that she felt increasingly miserable as the meal pro-
gressed.
It was a relief to leave the dining-room, though
that meant that now she would be alone with Scott,
would have to explain to him why she had broken
her promise, what she intended to do. If only he
would look at her as though he loved her, she
thought, feeling the palms of her hands grow moist
with nervousness as she walked beside him through
the hall and out on the porch. She'd had to do what
she'd done. She must carry out the plan that, all
day, had been taking shape in her mind. She'd felt
that it would be a relief to tell him, to be comforted
by his sympathy and understanding. But now, when
he stood holding the door open for her, looking
down at her, she was frightened. She didn't want to
be alone with him. She wished that Dusty and Caro-
line would join them on the porch, that Dad would
come out to smoke a cigar.
But Dusty left almost immediately and Dad and
Caroline went up to Mother's room. She sat in the
hammock. Scott seated himself beside her, lit a ciga-
rette, tossed the match out on the grass. He didn't
intend to help her. Dusk was gathering but the light
was still so clear that, glancing up, she saw withdistressing clarity the grim angle of his jaw, the
way his lips set into a stern line each time he re-
moved the cigarette. He kept looking away from
her straight across the porch, as remote, as silent
as though she weren't there. She couldn't bear it,
she simply couldn't endure feeling shut out like this.
She loved him so terribly much.
"Scott!" she said with a low despairing cry. "Scott,
are you angry with me?"
"Disappointed." He did not turn to look at her.
One hand was engaged with his cigarette; the other
lay motionless in his lap. If he would help her, touch
her, look at her- She winked back tears, swallowed
the lump in her throat, drew a quivering breath.
"Scott," she said, feeling her heart faint within
her breast. "I-I broke my promise, Scott. I-I
went to the Summerfields' this morning."
He turned to her, then.
"I wondered if you would tell me."
"I had no other intention." She was surprised at
the relief her confession obviously gave him, com-
forted by the softened expression in his eyes. "You
saw me, didn't you? I wasn't sure. But I would have
told you, anyway."
"Would you, Julia?" He seemed to be almost
begging her for the truth, desperate for reassurance.
She gave it gladly, forgetting the thoughts that had
passed through her mind when she'd seen his car
pass at the top of the grade beyond the Summerfield
place.
"Why, Scott, of course," she insisted. "I shouldn'thave made that promise. You shouldn't have asked
it. I know that you were thinking only of me, but I
can't think just of myself. This morning when I saw
the courthouse and thought of what maybe would
happen there" Her voice trembled into silence.
"Did you see this Summerfield boy?"
His voice was stern again, demanding the truth.
"No," she said. "His mother, Miss Isabel, told
me that he doesn't want to see me. She was horrible
to me," she added, indignation boiling over, washing
out of her mind the phrases, the words, she had
been rehearsing all afternoon to explain her reason
for breaking her promise. "She treated me like a
servant she was about to dismiss. After pretending
to be fond of me, too, making a fuss over me all
these years. If you could have heard the things she
said to me!"
"I can imagine." His tone was grim. "I had an
interview with her this afternoon."
"She hates me. She's always hated me." It was a
blessed relief to pour out to Scott the indignities
she had suffered. She'd wanted to tell Caroline, but
Caroline had been so hateful, storming away at her
as though this dreadful situation was all her fault.
"She's a jealous, vindictive old woman," she went
on, luxuriating in Scott's sympathy, healing the
wounds that Mrs. Summerfield, that Caroline, had
inflicted. "She hates me because Jay loves me. I was
never so embarrassed, so humiliated in all my life
before."
"Darling!" Scott's arms held her close. "Oh,Julia, you're so sweet and such a precious little idiot.
I've been miserable all day because you broke your
promise to me. But perhaps it's just as well. You
tried and you failed. You realize, now, that it's use-
less for you to stay here. We'll talk to your family
to-night and I'll take you to Washington to-morrow,
because I must go to-morrow. I had a wire from my
office to-day. The situation here looks pretty grim,
I'll admit. But you can't help and apparently I can't
either. Unless Mrs. Summerfield relents, there's
nothing anybody can do. So we'll talk to your family,
your father and Caroline, anyway, if your mother
doesn't feel up to it. I'm going to take you with me
to-morrow."
"No, we can't talk to the family. I can't go with
you, Scott."
She felt his arms relax, felt him draw a little
away from her.
"Why not?" he asked quietly.
"Because people, everybody would think "
"You still care more for public opinion than for
me?"
"Oh, no, no!" she protested, terrified by the tone
of his voice. "I don't care what anyone thinks or
says except you and Caroline and Mother and Dad."
She thought she was telling the truth. In the neces-
sity of convincing Scott the events of the morning
receded into the dim background of her mind. Allie
Carter, Jinny Lucas, Mr. Glenworthy, fat puffing
Mrs. Dudley, remembered briefly and dismissed, did
seem of no importance, now when Scott spoke toher with bitterness, when she felt him drawn away
from her in critical distrust. "But Caroline despises
me for thinking of going with you. I told her last
night. She thinks I'm running away leaving her to
face everything alone. She's so odd and vindictive,
Scott. And I do love Caroline. She simply loathes
and despises me."
"Of course she doesn't," he said patiently. "She's
had a shock. She'll understand that you can't help
here, that there's nothing you can do."
"But there is."
"Be sensible, darling." She was aware of the exas-
peration that had crept into his voice. "You've been
to see Mrs. Summerfield and you're convinced that
you can't persuade her to change her mind."
"I can persuade Jay."
"But you said she told you that the boy doesn't
want to see you."
"That was a lie." She did not realize that her
prompt reply rang with triumph, the triumph she'd

she's doing, or if he does, when I talk to him
"
"You aren't thinking of that, are you?" he asked
with an ominous steadiness in his deliberate tones.
"Why, of course," she said, vaguely irritated, a

obvious duty. "There's nothing else to be done. Mrs.
Summerfield will do anything for Jay and when I
see him and make him understand, he'll stop her.
Jay loves me. He wouldn't do that to me, to us."Scott made no reply. The silence lengthened. It
was growing dark now. She felt, rather than saw,
the shocked incredulity, the train of conjectures that
her simple statement had aroused in Scott's mind.
"You-you understand, don't you?" she asked
when the silence had lengthened beyond endurance.
"You do see why I can't go away with you, Scott?"
"It's a reasonable explanation," he admitted
slowly. "I should accept it, I suppose, and spare
myself the torture of questioning you. But I've got
to be sure, Julia. I must ask you. You haven't
changed your mind, have you? You aren't regretting
that you told the Summerfield boy you couldn't
marry him?"
She was too surprised, too genuinely hurt by his
question to reply.
"You see," he went on, "I didn't realize until this
afternoon what marriage into the Summerfield fam-
ily would give you. I didn't know that his mother
is planning a diplomatic career for him, that she has
the means to make it possible. I can't promise you
an exciting life like that. I've done fairly well, com-
paratively, but I haven't, will probably never have,
any great wealth. I imagine the rosewood furniture
in that small sitting-room where we talked is worth
half of my yearly salary."
"Were you thinking of that during dinner," she
asked, "when you looked so stern and I thought it
was because you knew I had broken my promise?"
"I've been thinking of it all afternoon, wonderingif I am being fair to you, wondering if you realize
what you are giving up for a prosaic life with me.
The boy is your own age, too. I'm years older, Julia.
Haven't you thought of that? Haven't you, since
you were there this morning, regretted giving up the
chance to be a member of the Summerfield family,
the wealth, the distinction, the life you'd have as
Jay's wife?"
"Indeed I haven't," she replied with such honest
indignation in her voice that he laughed and relaxed.
"I've been gloating because Miss Isabel is afraid
of me and planning like a scheming hussy to use
every ounce of affection Jay has for me to outwit
his mother."
He laughed again and his arms held her close.
She gave herself to the unclouded bliss of loving
him, happy in the moment, forgetting her interview
with Mrs. Summerfield, Caroline's hostility, the
dreary dinner, not thinking of the future, nor doubt-
ing the success of a direct appeal to Jay. But when
it was time for him to leave sadness fell like a
shadow across the bright ecstasy of her love.
"You must go to-morrow?" she asked, clinging
to him as she stood with him in the darkened sanc-
tuary of the porch.
"Must, darling. But Washington isn't far away.
I'll call you every day. I'll come again. You'll let
me know as soon as you have accomplished this
miracle?"
"Oh yes, Scott, yes.""And if you can't, you'll let me know, let me
come for you?"
"Yes. But I will accomplish it. Oh, I wish you
weren't going. Will you come by in the morning?"
"No, sweet. I'm leaving early, before you'll be
awake." His voice became grave. "Can I trust you
not to do anything too generous or heroic, because
you know, Julia, that there are limitations to what
any one of us can do for another person? I'm leav-
ing my life and my heart in your hands. Will you
bring them back to me, darling?"
"You're taking mine with you. A fair exchange."
"Not fair at all. Yours are lovelier than mine.
Julia! Darling! I do trust you. But be careful, sweet.
I'm afraid to leave you here. So many people love
you. It isn't safe to go away without you."
"But I must stay and I will be careful. I love you,
Scott."
She wept when he had gone, when the sound of
his car had died away in the distance. She wept,
leaning against the gate where they'd stood, with
her face buried in the hammock cushions, wept be-
cause she loved him and he had gone and the disaster
which had destroyed the peace and serenity of her
home, forgotten for an interval, became an actuality
again. She wept lying in the hammock, missing
Scott's arms, the warmth of his vitality, his lips.
But presently she brightened, went into the house,
used Caroline's compact lying on the table in the
hall. "Dear Jay," she phrased in her mind, runningCaroline's lipstick over the lips that Scott had kissed.
No- "Jay, dear," perhaps. How enormous her
eyes looked, how shining and dark in the pale oval
of her face! "Darling Jay." She missed Scott al-
ready, ached for him. "I must see you, Jay."XXIV
JULIA WAS DUSTING THE LIVING-ROOM WHEN LOUISA
came in through the hall. She was skipping a good
many chair rounds and the parts underneath things
that didn't show because she'd spent so much time
arranging flowers that she'd forgotten to telephone
the grocery order until it was too late for the morn-
ing delivery. And now she'd have to drive to the
store before lunch. But dust didn't matter and the
downstairs rooms did look nice with the shades low-
ered and flowers everywhere. She was especially
pleased with the arrangement she'd placed on the
top of the old square piano, a spraying mass of white
phlox and violet-blue larkspur and those pink Orien-
tal poppies from the Polk garden which faded quickly
but were so lovely while they lasted.
People were kind about sending Mother flowers,
though she didn't seem to take much interest in
them. She took no interest in anything except re-
minding Louisa how Dad liked things cooked and
asking whether his linen suits had gotten back from
the laundry. And she didn't do that as though it
gave her any pleasure to make Dad comfortable
but more as though thinking of him was an old habit
that nagged at her and wouldn't let her relax.
Julia felt frightened whenever she thought ofMother. She had remained in bed all the first week
after that dreadful day when Mrs. Summerfield had
descended in wrath upon the household. Now, dur-
ing this second week, she'd dressed each day and
come downstairs. But she paid very little attention
to the household routine, refused to see anyone, even
the rector; just lay in a deck-chair out under the
pear tree in the back yard all day long in a listless
way so unlike her former activity that Julia had hard
work not to cry when she took her a tray which she
scarcely touched, or flowers somebody had brought,
or went to tell her that Miss Jenny was calling and
wouldn't she like to see her.
She, Julia, had tried to take Mother's place in the
household, but, though she made lists and questioned
grumpy Louisa a thousand times a day she could
never seem to keep things running smoothly. Caro-
line was simply no help at all. She was at the office
all day and when she came home spent every moment
until dark, except for the time it took her to bolt
her dinner, working over her dogs. Of course Caro-
line did work at the office and Dad said he couldn't
get along without her there but it was certainly more
exciting to be downtown where things were happen-
ing than to be cooped up here at home getting your
hands all rough with housework and being pleasant
to callers, not daring to let them know you were
anxious about anything except Mother's condition,
and being firm with Louisa who was getting slack
now that Mother's vigilance had relaxed.
Not that she minded, she told herself, aware thatLouisa stood in the doorway watching her but pre-
tending she didn't know Louisa was there because
she had no intention of being dictated to by a serv-
ant. She was willing to do anything to make this
horror of suspense and uncertainty easier for every-
one, work her fingers to the bone, sacrifice any
thought of amusement, moulder and decay in the
brooding atmosphere that lay like a choking smudge
over the household. She did think that she deserved
a little credit from Caroline, though. Not credit, she
corrected, because she wanted to do her duty, but
a little kindliness, at least, some recognition of the
fact that she, Julia, wasn't exactly enjoying herself.
She couldn't treat Caroline as Caroline treated her
no matter what her sister had done. And because
Caroline had quarreled with Dusty was no reason
for making her, Julia, feel like a leper, like someone
too horrible to be given a civil word.
And here was Louisa primed to give her a scold-
ing because she'd forgotten the grocery order.
"Well, Louisa," she said, trying to imitate Moth-
er's courteous but firm way with servants. "Do you
want to speak to me?"
"Somebody else wants to speak to you." Louisa
obviously had neither noticed nor intended to imply
any criticism of her somewhat sketchy dusting. Her
face was solemn, but excited, too, as it was whenever
she had an important message to convey. "Dat Buck
waitin' fo' you down at de back gate."
Buck? Her heart beat quickly, but she must con-
ceal any evidence of agitation from Louisa's sharpeyes. She was certain that Louisa knew everything,
but since no member of the family had told her
directly, she could pretend that she didn't. "What
does he want?" she asked, making her voice sound
casual, even a little annoyed.
"He don't say," Louisa replied. But Julia knew
that Louisa had discussed in detail with Buck, the

Julia." Louisa's innocent expression was quite as

got no time to mix in wid other people's business,"

"Thank you, Louisa." Julia went past her with

It was humiliating to be dependent upon the un-

said-
She'd purposely avoided thinking of Scott be-cause thinking of him was no longer pleasure but

He hadn't told her in advance that he was coming

I'll drive it. Mother will drop the whole matter

father to buy the paper if he wants it. You need have
no doubts about that."
"But she doesn't want you to marry me." Indigni-
ties recalled to her mind gave her the courage to
protest.
"Oh, that!" He laughed briefly. "Did you believe
it? She would accept the situation if we were mar-
ried. She doesn't dislike you, Julia, though she may
have treated you badly. She thinks that if we mar-
ried I wouldn't do crazy things. She's right. I
wouldn't. It's been because I've loved you so and
never been sure of you. Darling, you won't regret
it. We'll have a wonderful life. In a little while you
will have forgotten everything that troubles you now.
You are adaptable, you know. You're a little chame-
leon and you might as well turn my colors. They'll
be gay colors, Julia darling, when we've gotten away
from here"
If she had only herself to consider, she would
have the courage to tell him exactly what she
thought. But she kept seeing Mother in the deck
chair under the pear tree, the closed door between
her room and Caroline's, Dad's pitiful shame andhumility, the faces of her friends turned against her
wherever she went. No, people weren't important,
but her family was. If only -
"This" she steadied her voice. "This is the
only way out?"
He was silent for a moment while she held her
breath in a torture of suspense swinging between
hope and despair.
"The only way out," he said, with a finality that
crushed her last remnant of hope. "Don't hate me,
Julia, not even for a moment. I'm not certain that I
could convince Mother, anyway. She's pretty bitter
and as proud as Satan. But when we are married
everything will be all right. She'll drop this idea of
suing your father and you and I will be together. We
won't come back here. We'll sail for England at
once, you and I alone. Darling!"
She'd known from the first that she would go away
with him if he demanded that sacrifice. Now, while
she nodded in mute consent, she realized that in her
heart she had, since her first meeting with Jay, been
bidding farewell to Scott. For an instant memories
of times they had been together, the remembered
ecstasy of being in his arms, the way her heart
skipped a beat each time she saw him again, his
dark blue eyes smiling at her with the expression of
affectionate amusement she had grown to love
through candlelight, moonlight, sunlight, dusk,
crowded through her mind, terrifying her with an
intensity of pain that could not be endured. Sheforced them into the background of her mind. Some-
time she would remember. To-morrow
Mother and Dad and Caroline! How relieved they
would be. She pictured the joy the news would bring
them, regretting that she would not be there. Caro-
line would be remorseful because she'd been so hate-
ful to her, would love her for the sacrifice she was
making. That was odd, though, because Caroline
had been critical of what she considered her lack of
honesty and yet when she'd told the truth Caroline
had despised her. And certainly this wasn't honest,
running away with Jay when she loved Scott with all
her heart.
...
"We'll get away before Mother comes home to-
night," Jay was saying when she gave up the effort
to tidy the confusion of thoughts that filled her mind
and gave him her attention again. "We'll be mar-
ried in Elkton tonight, then drive on to Wilming-
ton."
"But you can't drive with your arm in a sling."
"You can, darling. We'll put up at the DuPont
in Wilmington and stay there until we've seen
Mother. You aren't dreading that, are you?"
"No," she said and she wasn't. She felt, ignobly
perhaps, that to meet Miss Isabel as Jay's wife
would make up a little for the humiliation she'd
suffered the morning she'd called on her, for what
Dad and Scott had suffered, later, in the same rose-
wood furnished room. Strange that now the decision
had been made, she was able to catch, to some de-
gree, the contagion of Jay's excitement. Or perhapsit was the relief of having something happen, any-
thing, after the period of strain and tension. She
simply could not endure seeing people suffer, living
in an atmosphere of depression and gloom.
"Mother is having dinner with the Brooks in
Easton," Jay went on, his voice excited and eager,
his face as he looked at her, lit with the expression
that gave it dark beauty. "You must make some ex-
cuse to get away from home and meet me out beyond
the college at half past six. I'll have Buck drive me
there and wait for you."
"But what about luggage?" She was glad there
were practical details to be considered, that she felt
this sustaining stir of excitement. Her voice, in her
own ears, sounded almost gay. Maybe she was hys-
terical. She did feel curiously light-headed, as though
she were floating through space.
"Pack a small bag when you go home and put it
in your garage. I'll have Buck get it this afternoon.
Don't bother to bring anything you won't need to-
night. We'll shop to-morrow. I'll love going shop-
ping with you." His eyes adored her. "Think of
shopping in London, in Paris. You're so lovely-look-
ing, Julia. Buying pretty clothes for you will be a
very rewarding occupation."
She laughed shakily, convinced that she was hyster-
ical. If she weren't how could she laugh when tears
tasted salty on her lips?
"That seems strange, doesn't it?" she asked. "You
buying clothes for me."
"It seems to me the most natural thing in theworld. Oh, Julia!" His voice softened in appeal.
"You don't hate me, do you? You must love me a
little when I love you so much. You are fond of
me. I'll be content with that just at first. If you're
fond of me -
"I am fond of you, Jay." She might as well do
this with what grace she could, she told herself. And
she was fond of him. She supposed she ought to hate
him but hating wouldn't help. She thanked Heaven
that it was practically impossible for her to hold
a grudge, though perhaps that, too, was a lack of
character. Still it would make everything a good deal
easier than if she had Caroline's disposition. Caro-
line might call it a lack of character but she called
it common-sense. "I am fond of you," she repeated,
relieved to find it easier this time. "I don't hate
you. Maybe I, or anyone else, would do what you
are doing, given the same motive and opportunity."
For the first time since they had sat there on the
grass at the end of the lawn, screened by willow
branches from the high white-pillared house behind
them, Jay looked humbled, a little ashamed.
"You're sweet, Julia," he said. "I know it seems
like a rotten trick. But I swear I'll make it up to
you, that I'll never give you any cause for regret."
He bent sidewise and kissed her, a reverent kiss,
neither stirring nor alarming. It moved her no more,
she thought absently, than if a willow frond had
blown across her lips. The excitement she felt was
not caused by any physical response to Jay's slight
graceful body there beside her, nor by any anticipa-tion either of dread, resignation or possible delight.
With her faculty for ignoring unpleasantness, for
living in the moment, she was able, almost, to forget
that he was there. She did not think of Scott and
least of all of herself. She was sustained, at that
moment, by picturing the joy that release from strain
would bring to her family, by the certainty that in
this way she would make her peace with Caroline.XXVI
CAROLINE WENT UPSTAIRS AFTER AN INTERVIEW
with Louisa, knowing that she'd have to hurry if she
was to have the bath she wanted before dinner but
too weary to make any effort. Miss Harriett was
visiting her sister, Mrs. Judge Carroll, and she'd
stopped in to see her on her way home from the
office. Both the ladies had been cordial and she had
relaxed in their pleasant impersonal friendliness so
that she had stayed longer than she realized and
had left feeling more cheerful than she had felt for
weeks. She had fed the dogs and gone into the kitchen
whistling, only to be told by Louisa that Julia had
forgotten the marketing again and that dinner would
consist of left-overs from yesterday garnished by
salmon out of a can.
That was inexcusable of Julia, Caroline thought,
the antagonism she felt for her sister increased by
this last evidence of Julia's complete unreliability.
Not that Julia hadn't been sweet, really wanting to
help, undertaking impossible tasks which she or
Louisa had had to finish while Julia waltzed on to
untried ventures. She had embraced the adventure
of keeping house while Mother was ill with bright
enthusiasm. But the trouble with Julia was she had
no sense of proportion. She had evidently spent theentire day arranging flowers and had neglected the
marketing when all she had to do was make a tele-
phone call.
Where was she? Caroline had seen no sign of her
downstairs or on the porch and the second floor
seemed unusually still. There were sounds to indicate
her presence when she was there, her voice humming,
singing snatches of song, the light tapping of her
heels on the floor, water running in the bathroom,
the activity and interest that centered about Julia.
Caroline missed that cheerful stir, now. Though
she felt that she thoroughly despised her sister, hon-
esty compelled her to admit that Julia's presence
was the only cheering element in the brooding de-
spondency that filled the house.
The door into Mother's room was open and Car-
oline saw her lying on the couch in the shallow bay
window. Dad sat in a willow armchair beside the
couch, looking wretchedly tired and as though he
would like to smoke but refraining from doing so
in self-inflicted punishment. They weren't talking.
Neither seemed to be finding any comfort in the
presence of the other. They were simply there to-
gether making each other miserable. Caroline went
into the room.
"Hello!" she said, making her voice sound breezy
and bracing. "How are you, Mother?"
"Stronger, I think." Mother turned her cheek
listlessly to receive Caroline's kiss.
But she wasn't stronger. She seemed weaker each
day, a pale spiritless ghost of her former self. Sheroused only to instruct one of them in some detail
that would insure Dad's physical comfort as though
that was a duty she could not shirk while breath
warmed her body. Was this Mother's way of pun-
ishing Dad? Caroline knew that the shock had been
crushing. But there'd been something indestructible
in Mother which should be reasserting its sustain-
ing power. She'd had shocks before and survived
them.
She loved Mother but she felt, ignobly perhaps,
a deeper affection for Dad. Now, seeing the expres-
sion in his eyes when he looked at Mother, she went
to him, dropped down on the arm of his chair, laid
her face against his cheek.
"Hello, Baby." He turned, his face brightening,
gave her a grateful glance that made her want to
cry. She smiled, instead, spoke of her call at Judge
Carroll's, then inquired for Julia.
"Mother says Julia won't be here for dinner,"
Dad said.
"Which is probably just as well since there won't
be much here. Julia forgot the marketing again.
Where has she gone?"
"The Laceys, wasn't it, Frannie? Those Phila-
delphia people that bought the old DeCoursey place
at Rock Hall."
"She mentioned them," Mother said, looking past
Dad to Caroline. "I inferred that Jinny Lucas was
taking her in her car."
"Smart of Julia to get herself invited out fordinner when she neglects the marketing." Caroline's
voice was grim.
"Julia does try," Mother said in gentle rebuke.
"It isn't fair to be too critical. She's never done
housework before and she misses Scott. I thought

to be a little more sympathetic?"
She ought to be accustomed to hearing Julia de-

she knew only slightly? And with Jinny Lucas? Julia

Dinner alone with Dad, the evening, stretched drear-

last time he'd had dinner here, that ghastly dinner

that if Julia were here she would make some gesture
of friendliness.
But she wouldn't. She couldn't forgive her sister
though there were times, such as this, when she
longed to with all her heart, when she felt guilty
and ashamed of the hostility she cherished. Juliawas to blame, she told herself, trying to justify her
attitude, never entirely succeeding. She dropped her

It was then that she saw the corner of an envelope
protruding from under her vanity box. She lifted

The painted wooden box had concealed two enve-
lopes. One was addressed in Julia's handwriting to

Dear Cal,
Jay and I have gone away to be married. That
will make everything right because his mother will
drop the whole business about Dad when I am Jay's
wife. Jay has promised me and I know it's true so
you needn't worry any longer. But don't tell Mother
and Dad until you hear from us. We'll wire some
time to-night.
Will you mail this letter to Scott for me? And
if you ever see him again, will you tell him that I
mean every word I've written with all my heart?
He'll believe you because he knows you are honest
and sincere and I'm afraid he has his doubts about
me.
He's right, of course. There's nothing honest about
my going away with Jay except that we've made a
bargain and he knows I love Scott. But maybe Iwasn't meant to be honest because when I did tell
the truth it made a dreadful mess.
I've been wanting to talk to you but you were
too busy despising me to give me a chance. I hate
going away without seeing you because, next to Scott,
I love you better than anyone in the world. I won't
be there, Cal darling, so you can open the door be-
tween our rooms again.
She didn't mean it, Caroline thought in instinc-
tive reaction to what Julia had written. This was a
trick, one of Julia's pretty deceptions that usually
resulted in complications. Of course it was a ges-
ture, she repeated to still the clamoring voices of
fear and self-accusation. Julia didn't love Jay. She
loved Scott. She'd left a loop-hole for escape some-
where. Julia simply hadn't it in her to go through
with a thing like this.
But was it a gesture? She read the letter through
again. Julia had written simply, resisting, if she had
had it, any impulse to dramatize herself. There were
no blots that might have been intended to convey
a touching impression of tears falling while she
wrote. The stark simplicity of the words she read

yet entirely convinced, wrung her heart.
Caroline, the letter clutched in her hand, opened

She went hurriedly to the closet, jerked open the

in a heart-shaped silver frame, the photograph of
herself and Julia taken when they were six and

Conviction, realization, struck her with the force

She had forced Julia to this. She would have
known that even if Julia had not added that last

supported her as she should have, as she'd really

She had criticized Mother, in her mind, in her heart,

Yes, she and Mother were alike. As Mother, herself,had suggested. She had punished Julia, cruelly, de-

Caroline dropped down on the padded stool where
Julia had so often sat before the gilt-framed oval
mirror. Jay would suffer for her own stubborn hos-

grudges. She wouldn't be able to endure an atmos-

charming. He was counting on that, of course. It
was terribly unfair of him to have taken advantage
of the situation to force Julia to marry him. It was
dishonorable and ungenerous.
But she hadn't time to think of Jay. Caroline
sprang up from the padded stool. She must prevent
this marriage. She must prevent it if by doing so she

But they mightn't be there. Tell Dad? Julia had
asked her not to tell Mother and Dad until she

Dusty would come if she called him, think of some-

Summerfields'. Maybe something had happened to

She ran down the steps, not waiting to reply to

Jinny, herself, answered the call. No, Julia wasn't
