TWILIGHT HAD DEEPENED INTO THE STILL SOFT
dark of the summer night. Children had been called
in from the streets to go to bed and the only sound
which broke the stillness, except insect sounds and
the sleepy chirping of birds, was the Merryman's
radio sending the rhythm of a swing tune across the
street. Caroline paced back and forth along the
length of the porch, too restless to be still, her eyes
straining through the darkness for the sight of Dusty
coming in through the gate, listening for the whir
and jangle of the telephone in the hall.
It was some slight relief to be able to move, not
to be obliged to conceal the strain and suspense that
were tearing her nerves to shreds. She'd made an
effort to appear casually cheerful with Dad through
dinner but she'd given a little scream when Louisa
dropped a glass and it crashed on the floor, had
flown to the telephone when it rang only to find that
Miss Jenny Polk was calling to inquire for Mother.
Dad hadn't appeared to be especially curious. They'd
lived so long with jumpy nerves that nothing seemed
remarkable. He, too, had made an effort to be cheer-
ful and had been pathetically grateful when she sug-
gested that they walk out to see the dogs, after
dinner, while he smoked a cigar.She'd wanted to tell him but had decided that
knowledge of the sacrifice Julia was making for him,
for all of them, would come soon enough. She was
relieved when he had gone upstairs to Mother and
she was alone.
Where was Julia now? If they'd gone directly to
Elkton, they must be married now, at this moment.
Mother had told her in reply to her own cautious
questions that Julia had left the house a little before
five, that she wore the thin dark dress they'd bought
in Baltimore last spring and the dark halo hat that
matched it. She'd thought the costume a little odd but
had supposed that some plan for the evening made it
appropriate, and Caroline had agreed with her. That
plain, dark, tailored dress when Julia, in satin and
tulle, would have been such a beautiful bride. Some-
how, knowing Julia as she did, the thought of the
simple dark dress and halo hat seemed a more poign-
ant sacrifice than marrying Jay when she loved Scott.
Elkton was less than an hour's drive away. They
must, already, have been married. Odd that now,
though she'd tried, she couldn't think of Jay. Her
concern was all for Julia. Walking back and forth
across the porch she thought with agonizing contri-
tion of their childhood, the growing-up years, school
days at Manor Hall, the confidences they had shared,
their close, sometimes bickering, but always affection-
ate relationship. She'd recalled the countless times
that Julia had considered her feelings and her happi-
ness when she might so easily have relegated her to
the ignoble position of being the younger sister of abeauty. She had repeated the last paragraph of Julia's
letter over and over. "-because, next to Scott, I
love you better than anyone in the world." Tears
stung her eyes again and her throat ached unbear-
ably. If Julia would forgive her, would give her
another chance.
The telephone whirred and jangled. She had sped
in through the screen door and lifted the receiver
before the ring had ended.
"Hello!" she cried breathlessly into the mouth-

"Just a moment, please," came in the mechanical

Thirty cents, please."

"And where is Jay?"
"He's there, too. Julia says he's perfectly wild and
getting plastered. Isn't that just like Julia?" She was
laughing, but crying, too, crying with relief and
impatience at having to waste time in explanation.
"Little fool! Starting out with the best motives in
the world and then muffing it and getting herself in a
jam. Dusty-" Her hand gripped his arm. "You
said once that I could count on you, that you'd help
me in any way you could. Will you?"
"I'm here," he said briefly.
"Will you go with me for Julia ?"
"To Old Vienna?" The humorous notes in his
drawling voice were very comforting.
"It's that place this side of the Riverton bridge.
I think I remember. I was frantic when I found the
letters she'd left, one for me and one for Scott. Icouldn't think how I could stop them." Her voice
trembled and was still.
"Are we going to bring them both home?"
"We'll bring Jay but not Julia."
"Jay?"
"He can't drive with his shoulder hurt."
"Oh-" Dusty was silent for a moment. "You
seem to have arranged everything," he said, finally.
"How are you planning to dispose of Julia?"
If she hadn't been so excited, so near the verge of
hysterics, she would have resented the implications
not too subtly hidden in the words Dusty spoke, in
the considering tone of his voice. At the moment,
however, she was not concerned with Dusty's re-
actions.
"I'm going to call Scott," she said, "and if I can
get him, tell him that Julia will be waiting for him
in Elkton some time to-night. She really loves Scott,
Dusty, and he loves her." She gave a low shaken
laugh. "I can't spend the rest of my life getting Julia
out of trouble. It's too exhausting. Scott can take
over where I leave off."
"He'll probably do an excellent job. But Caro-
line-" Dusty stopped, then went on. "Are you doing
this just for Julia ? I suppose I haven't any right to
ask you that, but I can't help-wondering."
The issue which, at the moment, seemed of greater
importance crowded secondary considerations out of
the foreground of her mind.
"I'm doing it for myself, I guess," she replied.
"If you consider them carefully, I suppose all acts ofgenerosity have, somewhere, a selfish motive. Yes, I
guess I'm doing it for myself."
"I see," Dusty said gravely. "Well, go in and
make your call. Do you want to use my car or
yours?"
"Ours, I think, if you don't mind." She was frantic
with impatience to get away. "There'll be more room
if-in case we need it."
"You have thought of everything. I'll get it out
while you make your call." Dusty started down the
porch steps, then swung around. In the light coming
out from the hall, lying in a rectangle across the
steps, Caroline saw that his face looked oddly trou-
bled. "You're an opportunist, Caroline," he added.
"But I'll be hanged if I'll wish you luck."
Dusty's remark slipped through her mind and
was gone in the concentrated effort of remembering
Scott's address. She recalled it, though, when, shut
into the closet again, she had put in her call and sat
waiting for the connection. Dusty thought she was
rescuing Julia, sending for Scott, because she loved
Jay and wanted Julia out of the way. She'd told him
her motive was selfish. But she hadn't thought of
Jay, then. She'd thought only of making up to Julia
for the wrong she had done her so that her own
mind and heart would be relieved of the burden of
guilt they had borne.
But was Dusty's surmise correct? Crouching there
on the pile of galoshes and boots with the closet door
opened for air, while she waited, she made an honesteffort to think clearly. It was useless. She was totally
unable to concentrate upon her feeling for Jay. Im-
patience tugged at her nerves, making thought impos-
sible. She could think of nothing but talking to Scott
and then getting started.の

HE SAT AT A ROUND OIL-CLOTH COVERED TABLE IN
a corner, his elbow braced on the table, his cheek in
the palm of his hand. He had loosened his coat so
that it fell back from the bandaged shoulder and the
arm that lay in a black silk sling. A glass, nearly full,
stood before him and an ash tray overflowing with
ashes and butts.
She had wondered what she would feel when she
saw him. Standing outside when Dusty and Julia had
gone, realizing that it was not only expediency but a
desire to see Jay and talk to him which had kept her
here, she had tried to anticipate what, if any, emo-
tion, being with him alone would arouse. Now, as
she stood just inside the door, looking at him through
a blue haze of smoke, through the dim light from
dangling bulbs shrouded in crepe paper streamers,
she was shocked at the tenderness for him, the pity
that flooded her heart. He looked so abandoned
there in that dingy corner, staring straight ahead at
the trellis covered with paper roses against the oppo-
site wall.
She had hoped that she would feel no emotion. She
had wanted, in this time which was given her to be
alone with Jay, that she might lay that ghost for-
ever, free herself from the secret romantic idealismTUESDAY NEVER COMBS [323]
of him which had made her miserable. Now, the

Jay's car.
But he saw her, then. He lifted his head and
smiled at her across the distance which separated
them, a tortured smile of recognition and bravado.
"Hello, Caroline," he called and rose unsteadily

"Hello, Jay." She realized that the bartender was

stare, heard a girl at a table in the opposite corner
laugh and make a remark to her companion.
"Did they leave you here to play nursemaid?"
Jay asked and made an only partially successful at-
tempt to pull back her chair for her.
"I thought it might be dreary for you waiting
here alone." She dropped quickly into the chair, glad
to turn her back to curious eyes.
"Deary? Oh, no." His assumed jauntiness wrung

place, isn't it? So cosmopolitan. That gentleman at
the bar with the mustache is the chef of the 'Lazy
Lady.' The girl in the corner won a beauty contest

"No! No, thank you, Jay."
"Must have a drink." His voice rose. "Must drink
to the bride. Julia's getting married to-night. Pretty
Julia, the fairest flower of old Maryland. Julia—"
His bravado deserted him and he slumped down in

with pain. "She's gone!" he whispered hoarsely.
"She's gone into Elkton to meet that man. My
Julia."
"She loves him, Jay."
"Yes, she told me." His lips twisted into a tor-

the day after I came home. She pretended she still

me in the drug store, afraid I'd make a scene.
Wouldn't you call that a rotten trick?"
"It was wrong of her not to tell you the night

"All's fair in love and war."

"If I love her?" He flung the words across the

She checked her thoughts abruptly. She was like

She wondered if Mother, before they were mar-

spirit which was destroying him frightened her.
"Have a drink, Caroline."
"No, Jay, no! Please sit down."
"Must drink to Julia." His voice rang out above
the music. "Must drink to Julia. Julia's getting mar-
ried." He walked away from the table and toward
the bar, his quick cat-like tread surprisingly graceful
and steady. "Champagne," he called to the bartender
with the lordly air of a young prince whose every
whim was gratified. "Got to drink to Julia. Julia
is getting married."
The bartender, a middle-aged man with a round
good-natured face glanced at Caroline. She shook her
head.
"Where do you think you are, buddy?" the bar-
tender asked in jollying tones. "We ain't got cham-
pagne."
Jay swayed, steadied himself with his hand against
the edge of the bar.
"Scotch and soda, then."
"Ain't got any Scotch. What you've been drinking
is straight Maryland rye."
"All right. Have to drink to Julia in Maryland
rye. 'There's a girl in the heart of Maryland, With
a heart that belongs to-' Mix them, Charlie. Drinks
all around. Drink to Julia."
"Can't do it, buddy. After hours.""You mean you refuse to sell me another drink?"
Jay drew himself up with drunken dignity. "Do you
know who I am?"
"Wouldn't make any difference if you was the
King of France. County law."
"You're a liar!" Jay's voice was ominously con-
trolled. People looked in from the room at the rear.
The girl in the corner giggled nervously. Caroline
half rose from her chair.
"Is that a nice way to talk?" The bartender as-
sumed an injured expression. "It ain't my fault. I
don't make the laws. You've had plenty, haven't
you? How about a nice cup of coffee while you're
waiting for your friend?"
Caroline saw Jay's hand reach for a bottle stand-
ing on the bar. She sprang up sensing his intention.
But she was too late. She stood frozen with horror,
unable to move or make a sound, watching Jay hurl
the bottle at the bartender. The man behind the bar
ducked and the bottle struck the mirror with a splin-
tering crash. Jay slumped forward against the bar.XXX
LIGHTS SHONE FROM THE WINDOWS OF THE SUM-
merfields' house. The lanterns at either side of the
gate were lit though it was nearly two o'clock. Caro-
line stirred on the back seat of the sedan, the left
side of her body aching and cramped from the dead
weight of Jay's body slumped against her. The ride
home had seemed interminable, but now they were
here.
"All right, Caroline?" Dusty opened the door.
"Has he roused?"
"I'm all right. No, he hasn't moved."
"I thought he wouldn't. He's out like a light."
Dusty reached in across her, lifted and supported
Jay's inert body so that she was able to move, to
stretch, to stumble out of the car. She watched Dusty
settle Jay into a secure position, then walked with
him off a little distance from the sedan.
"What are you going to tell his mother?" Dusty
asked, and his face in the light of the gate lanterns
looked as it had when they were leaving the Old
Vienna Beer Garden and she'd told him she wanted
to ride home on the back seat with Jay.
"I'm going to tell her everything," she said quietly.
"Do you mean that, Cal?" His face brightenedwith eagerness and relief. "I wondered- I
thought"
"I know what you've been thinking and you were
right. I can't promise you that I'm going to enjoy
it." She looked up at him steadily. "Old habits seem
terribly hard to break, illusions you've clung to, no
matter how mistaken they were. But I'll tell her if
you'll help me."
"If I'll help you!" Dusty's whispered exclamation
had the force of a war-whoop. "If I'll help you!
What do you think?"
She smiled faintly.
"You'll have to have help with Jay. I'll go in
first."
"Keep your chin up, Ca'line."
He opened the gate for her. She took a step to-
ward the side entrance of the house, then turned, laid
her hand on Dusty's arm.
"You've been grand, Dusty, to-night, and always.
It was only humane to try to protect Jay's shoulder.
I'd have done it for anyone."
"I know. Skip it." He gave her a quick reassuring
pat. "Up and at 'em, Ca'line. Unless the Duchess
isn't the gal I've thought, the Perrys are about to
be avenged."
Caroline walked up to the door, glanced back at
Dusty watching her. He stood between the gate
posts and the light shone on his tall lanky figure, on
his sandy hair and his dear funny homely face. It
wasn't excitement she felt, then, it was a more sus-taining emotion. She blew him a kiss from the tips of
her fingers and lifted the knocker.
The door opened almost immediately and Sherry's
figure stood outlined in the rectangle of light.
"Oh, it's you, Miss Ca'line." He squinted to peer
past her toward the headlights of the car, his black
face puckered into wrinkles of distress. "Is you
brought Mister Jay?"
"Yes, we have, Sherry. May I speak to Miss
Isabel?"
"Yes, Caroline." Jay's mother came hurriedly to
the door, ignoring the punctilious ceremonies which,
ordinarily, were rigidly observed. "You say Jay is
with you?"
"Dusty Price and I brought him home. Dusty will
need help."
Mrs. Summerfield's hand touched her throat above
the lace and chiffon of her negligee.
"Is is he hurt?" she asked.
"He's been drinking. Otherwise he's all right. But
he can't walk."
Jay's mother made a moaning sound and her face
turned clay-colored under powder and rouge.
"Never you mind, Miss Isabel," Sherry said sooth-
ingly. "Young gent'man will kick up they heels now
and then. I know what to do fo' Mister Jay when
he been indulgin."
"Come in, Caroline," Mrs. Summerfield said when
Sherry had gone out toward the gate. "Come into
my sitting-room."
But though she led Caroline there, she lingered inthe hall until Caroline, waiting inside the room, heard
footsteps along the hall and up the stairs, heard
Dusty giving quiet directions, Sherry mumbling to
Jay in undertones as though he crooned a lullaby to
a hurt and frightened child. Caroline rested her
forehead against the cool veined marble of the low
mantel shelf, feeling faint with exhaustion, fighting
waves of nausea, gritting her teeth in the intensity
of her effort to regain self-control.
"Now, if you please, Caroline." Miss Isabel's
voice roused her, her tread on the thick flowered
carpet, the perfume she used, delicate but penetrat-
ing like crushed ferns and arbutus.
Caroline lifted her head.
"I'm sorry," she said. "I'm terribly tired."
"Sit down, my dear."
But she remained standing, bracing herself against
the mantel while Mrs. Summerfield sank down on
the love-seat between the windows.
"Where did you find Jay?" she asked wearily.
"I knew he'd gone away with Julia. I got that out
of the servants when I came home and found he
wasn't here. But where is Julia?"
"Dusty took her to Elkton. She's married to Scott
now, I hope. I sent for him when she called me."
"And where is Jay's car?"
"Standing in front of a roadhouse near the River-
ton bridge." Waves of nausea swept over her again
as she recalled the dimly-lit, smoke-filled room, heard
again the sound of splintering glass, saw Jay crum-
pled against the bar in that interval before Dustycame. "We couldn't very well bring it home. The
bartender assured me that it would be safe there."
"It doesn't matter. I was afraid there might have
been an accident." She waited, then making a visible
effort, asked, "What-what has Jay done?"
"He threw a bottle at the bartender."
"Hurt him?"
"No. He, the bartender, anticipated his intention
and ducked."
Miss Isabel covered her face with her hands.
"Jay!" she moaned. "Oh, Jay!"
Jay's mother was suffering, now, as they had
suffered. But Caroline didn't feel vindictive. She felt
nothing except utter weariness, then the cramp in her
shoulder where Jay's weight had rested, the waves of
nausea that were so terribly hard to control. The
room swam before her eyes, the delicate colors blend-
ing and blurring. Mrs. Summerfield's voice, raised
to a pitch of alarm, came to her faintly as though
from a distance. Dusty's voice was clearer. Her eye-
lids seemed weighted with lead but she lifted them
and saw Dusty bending toward her, supporting her
with his arm around her shoulders, concern and ten-
derness mingled movingly, comfortingly, in his ex-
pression.
"Ca'line!" he said. "Darling."
"I'm all right." She smiled faintly.
"Wait." Jay's mother moved mistily before her.
"I'll get some brandy, some ammonia."
"No, no, really I'm all right." The mist was clear-
ing but she clung to Dusty's hand as he led her to achair, clung to it when he stood beside her and Mrs.
Summerfield had returned to the love-seat.
"Perhaps you can tell me what has happened,"
Jay's mother said, speaking to Dusty.
"Yes, I can tell you." Dusty's drawling voice was
unusually crisp. "I left Caroline there with Jay while
I drove Julia into Elkton. She, we, were afraid that
he might drive away and he'd have been in no con-
dition to drive a car even if he'd had the use of both
hands. He would certainly have killed himself or
somebody else."
"Yes, yes, that was wise." Miss Isabel seemed
completely exhausted, Caroline thought, her spirit
broken, her pride humbled, but, oddly, there was
kindness in her expression as she faced them across
the softly lighted room. "And then?"
"When I returned I found that Jay had thrown a
bottle at the bartender," Dusty went on, "and he,
himself, was out like a light. He'd been drinking
steadily for four or five hours."
"But why did he throw the bottle?" Mrs. Sum-
merfield asked.
"Dusty before he left had told the bartender not
to give him anything more to drink. He asked for
another and was refused. You know Jay goes simply
insane when he's crossed. I saw what he meant to do,
but I couldn't reach him. I-" Caroline's voice fal-
tered, was still.
"I know." There was acceptance in the admission,
an acknowledgment of her own share in the blame,
of her doting indulgence. She sat in silence for amoment, looked down at the ringed hands clasped in
her lap. She looked old, Caroline thought, old and
broken, she who had borne so regally the proud tra-
ditions of the DeCourseys and the Summerfields,
Miss Isabel with her beautiful figure, her suave gra-
ciousness, her keen dominating mind. She would
never, the thought continued, walk quite so proudly
again. Caroline, secure in her youth, in the future
which lay before her, felt a stir of pity for Jay's
mother, a releasing certainty of having been forever
freed from her own romantic infatuation for Jay.
"I'm grateful to you for preventing that marriage,
for bringing him home." Mrs. Summerfield raised
her head. "Will you believe me when I tell you that
I had no idea he was planning this?"
"Then why didn't you have Dad arrested immedi-
ately?" Caroline asked. "Jay told me to-night that
it wasn't true that your lawyer is in Europe."
"Jay asked me to wait. I thought his motive was
kindness and was glad to take no immediate action.
I was prepared to drop the whole matter. I had no
idea that he would use the circumstances to force
Julia to marry him. That isn't important, is it?" She
sighed heavily, closed her eyes, then opened them.
"Yes, I suppose it is. It's an indication of how terribly
I have failed with Jay. You may tell your father,
Caroline, that I will permit him to make restitution,
that I will not bring charges, that, through you and
Julia, I am more deeply indebted to him than he
can possibly be to me."
"Do you suppose arrangements might be made forthe purchase of the paper?" Dusty asked. "You
would appreciate that, wouldn't you, Caroline ?"
She knew, when she glanced up at him, that her
reply meant more to him than the words he had
spoken conveyed. She knew that a personal question
was implied in his appeal to her.
"Yes, Dusty," she said and tightened her hold on
his hand.
Mrs. Summerfield rose from the love-seat. "I, my
lawyers for me, will agree to any reasonable arrange-
ment," she said. "I shall take Jay away at once. You
may rest assured that I will never, by word or by
deed, refer to the matter again. I have been fond of
you and of Julia, Caroline. I have regretted my
harshness to her. Human beings do cruel things to
each other in the heat of temper, when primitive
instincts force one beyond the restraint of civilized
kindness and self-contol. Will you give Julia my love
when you see her and ask her to forgive me?"
"She will, gladly, Miss Isabel." Caroline rose and
pressed the older woman's hand extended to her,
feeling a quick rush of sympathy for Jay's mother.
"Julia doesn't bear grudges."
"No. Julia is sweet-tempered and kind. I wish,"
she added with humility and a sincerity which Caro-
line could not question, "that Jay was strong and fine
so that she might have loved him. It is a mother's
weakness, I suppose, to blame others for faults in
her children. I'm grateful to you both for bringing
Jay home safely."
"We're grateful to you, Miss Isabel," Carolinesaid, feeling tears on her lashes in spite of the happi-
ness in her heart. "Dad did do wrong. Whatever his
motive it was wrong and dishonest of him to use
money which belonged to the paper."
"But two wrongs never made a right." Her weary
smile included both Dusty and Caroline. "It's
strange, isn't it? If we live long enough we say all
the things that have been said before. Sherry will
see you out. If you'll excuse me, I'll go up to Jay."
The night air felt cool and pleasant. The sky was
scattered thickly with stars. Dusty closed the gate
behind them and the lanterns winked out. There was
silence and darkness around them and beyond, at the
end of the street, the river calmly reflected the stars.
"I can't even hate her," Caroline said, standing
with Dusty's arm around her on the pavement beyond
the scrolled and filigreed gates.
"Don't try. When you answered my question, did
you know what I was asking?"
"Yes."
"And is the answer the same?"
"Yes, Dusty, yes!"
"All that nonsense about young Jay?"
"I'll despise you if you ever speak of it again."
"And you won't go to Connecticut with the dogs?"
"No. No, I'll stay here. We're a couple of hicks,
you and I. I love this funny sleepy old town."
"And me?י
"And you."
He drew an exaggerated sigh. "I wish you were
a little more like Julia, Ca'line. Not that I'm com-plaining, understand. But don't you think there are
times when we could do with a little less restraint?"
"Darling! Darling Dusty!"
"I told you what would certainly happen if you
ever called me that again." His arms held her close.
"I've loved you so and been so exasperated with
you. I don't know, now, whether I most want to
shake you or kiss you breathless."
"Try both just to be sure."
"Ca'line!"
Presently she drew away from him.
"Don't you like it?" he asked.
"I love it, but we have all the rest of our lives."
She ran toward the car. "Come on, Dusty. There
may be a wire from Julia at home and I can't wait
to tell Dad and Mother!"
